feature: Getting Startedcontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true1387 words · ~6 min

Install Kepler on Windows, macOS, or Linux, connect an agent and your trackers, and put an agent on real work in a few minutes.

Last updated: October 2026

Kepler is GitKraken's Agentic Development Environment (ADE): a place to direct coding agents across your real work, in as many repositories as you need, at the same time.

You bring the agent. Kepler pulls in the issues and pull requests already assigned to you, hands any of them to an agent with the context attached, and takes the result through to a reviewed, mergeable change.

Kepler is in public preview. A free GitKraken account is all you need.

Install Kepler

Platform Installer Minimum OS
Windows 64-bit, ARM64 Windows 10+
macOS Apple Silicon, Intel macOS 12+
Linux (x64) .deb, .rpm, .AppImage, Flatpak, AUR Ubuntu LTS 18.04+ / Debian 10+ / RHEL 8+ / Fedora 39+
Linux (ARM) .deb, .rpm, .AppImage Ubuntu LTS 20.04+ / RHEL 8+ / Fedora 39+
  1. Go to gitkraken.com/kepler/download and pick the installer for your platform.
  2. Run it.
  3. Launch Kepler.

If you open an older copy of Kepler than the one that last used your data, Kepler stops at Kepler is out of date before it opens, because the older copy can't read everything the newer one saved; sessions in particular may look like they have disappeared. The dialog names the version your data needs, and offers Open Newer Version when a newer copy is installed, or Update and Restart when this copy can update itself, plus Quit and Open Anyway. A Snap install doesn't offer Update and Restart.

Sign in

Kepler opens on a welcome screen, Launch any agent. Land every PR. Click Sign in with GitKraken. If you do not have an account, you'll create one in the next step.

Kepler opens the sign-in page in your browser, and the button's place becomes a sign-in panel: Continue in your browser. Kepler continues on its own once you're signed in. If no browser opened, use Reopen browser, or Copy link and open it in any browser. You can also paste the code from your browser under Or paste the code from your browser, then click Sign in. If Kepler can't reach GitKraken, it says Unable to reach GitKraken to start sign-in. Check your connection and try again.

The screen's top corner shows Kepler's version. Click it to check for updates: the corner shows Checking…, then Up to date, the download's progress, or Unable to check for updates with Try again. When an update has downloaded, it reads Update ready to install with Update and restart, so you can update before you sign in.

In a window connected to another machine, the screen says Continue in your other Kepler window: sign-in happens in the Kepler window on this computer, and the remote window carries on once you finish there. If that window can't be signed in, it says Unable to sign this window in with your Kepler account. Reconnect it and try again.

The Watch Quick Start Tour tile on that screen plays a short tour if you'd rather watch first, or watch the Kepler Quick Start Tour playlist on YouTube.

Choose your agents and your look

After you sign in, a guided start takes you through two steps. It fills the whole window, without the top bar. I'll explore on my own skips it at any point. Kepler also sends existing users through it once, with a Welcome back note that your tasks are where you left them.

Choose your coding agents. Kepler looks for the coding agents installed on this computer. Switch on the ones you want Kepler to use, install any that are missing, and make one the default. Under New sessions start in, pick Chat, Kepler's own view of the agent, or Terminal, the agent's own command-line app. Next waits until at least one agent is installed or switched on. If your code lives on another machine, Work on another machine? lists the machines Kepler found in your SSH configuration, in WSL, or saved in Kepler, and moves this window there. Remote environments need a paid plan, so that choice offers Try free and connect or Upgrade to connect where your plan doesn't include them.

Make it yours. Pick a theme, or open the Theme gallery, and choose how Kepler gets your attention under Notify me:

  • Everything: every reply and request on the desktop as well
  • When I'm needed: questions, approvals, and errors on the desktop, while replies stay in Kepler. This is the default on the desktop
  • Only in Kepler: no desktop notifications

Send a test notification shows what an alert looks like. You can change any of these later in Settings. Finish takes you to Tasks in progress on the Dashboard, with the task composer open for your first task.

During the guided start, Kepler also adds the repositories you already work in, so your repository picker isn't empty: the folders of agent sessions you ran outside Kepler, and the repositories GitKraken Desktop, GitLens, and the GitKraken CLI remember.

Finish the setup checklist

After the guided start, the Setup entry in the toolbar tracks 2 things, and shows Setup · N/2 until they're done. Open it for Finish setting up Kepler and a progress readout. Once both are done, the Setup entry leaves the toolbar.

# Item What it does
1 Connect an AI agent At least one coding agent. See Agent Integrations
2 Connect issue & PR trackers Any provider. This is what fills your Todo list. See Issue Tracker Integrations and Pull Request Integrations

Expand More setup options for optional items:

  • Set a default repositories folder: where Kepler clones repositories it does not find locally
  • Set a default worktree folder: where Kepler creates one worktree per repository, per Task
  • Connect a remote environment, covered in Remote Environments, where your plan can use it
  • Control Kepler remotely, also in Remote Environments
  • Add repo commands, once you have a repository

Add repo commands is worth doing early if your project needs a setup step. Save a repository's install or build command once and Kepler can run it automatically every time it creates a worktree, so an agent never starts in a checkout it cannot build. See Tasks and Resources.

Folder paths and everything else live in Settings. Hide from toolbar (still available in Settings) hides the checklist from the toolbar, and you can still reach it there.

Put an agent on real work

After you connect a tracker, the Todo segment of the Kepler interface fills with the issues and pull requests assigned to you.

  1. Pick an item in Todo.
  2. Click its Action button.

Nicely done! Kepler automatically does the following:

  1. Creates a Task.
  2. Attaches the repository and the item.
  3. Sets up an isolated worktree.
  4. Starts an agent with the context already in place.

The left half of the button runs the sensible default:

  • Plan for an issue
  • Review for someone else's pull request
  • Address Feedback for one of yours

The chevron next to the button offers the rest of the Actions.

Every one of those prompts is yours to change, and you can add your own. That's the part worth reading next: Actions.

Start something that is not tracked yet

Click New task or press Cmd/Ctrl+N. From outside Kepler, Shift+Alt+N opens the quick launcher. Write a prompt, attach a repository if the task needs one, and go. You can attach nothing at all and turn it into real work later. See Create a Task.

feature: Quick Answerscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true875 words · ~4 min

Short answers to common Kepler questions, each linked to the full page. Run agents on Windows, in WSL, or on another machine, use several accounts of one agent, check on agents from your phone, set up every new worktree automatically, and have a second agent review the work.

Last updated: October 2026

Quick Answers collects the questions people ask most about Kepler, answers each in a sentence or two, and links to the section with the full detail. Start here when you know what you want to do but not which page covers it.

Run agents where you work

Kepler runs agents on your computer, in WSL, or on another machine. Remote environments, which include WSL distros and SSH hosts, need a paid plan, and the number of accounts you can add for one agent depends on your GitKraken plan. The following table answers questions about where and how your agents run:

What you want to do Short answer Full details
Run several agents at once without their changes colliding Give each Task a worktree, a private Git working copy. Agents change files there, so several can run at once without touching each other's work or your own checkout. Tasks and Resources → Worktrees
Run Kepler on Windows Kepler installs natively on Windows 10 and later, on 64-bit and ARM64. It also runs on macOS 12 and later, and on Linux. Getting Started → Install Kepler
Run agents in Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL) Kepler treats a WSL 2 distro as a remote environment. It finds your version 2 distros and lists them under Found on this machine in Settings. Remote Environments → WSL environments
Run agents on another machine Add the machine as an SSH host. Kepler installs itself on the host over SSH, so you don't need to install anything there first. Remote Environments → Add an SSH host
Use several accounts of the same agent Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Auggie, and Grok Build can each hold more than one signed-in account. To add one, open Settings → Agents, expand the agent, and click Add account. Agent Sessions → Several accounts for one agent

Start work

You can start a Task from an issue in your tracker or from nothing at all. The following table covers starting work and getting each new worktree ready:

What you want to do Short answer Full details
Start an agent from a Jira, Linear, or GitHub issue Connect your tracker, then fire an Action from the issue's row. The issue becomes a Task, and Kepler attaches what it read, so the agent starts with that context. Issue Tracker Integrations, Actions
Start without an issue Click New task or press Cmd/Ctrl+N, then write a prompt. You can attach nothing at all and turn the Task into real work later. Getting Started → Start something that is not tracked yet
Install dependencies or copy .env files into every new worktree Add a per-repository command and turn on Run on worktree creation. For example, pnpm install installs dependencies, and cp "<SOURCE_PATH>/.env" .env copies your .env file from the main repository. Settings → Per-repository commands

Keep track of your agents

Kepler shows you what your agents are doing and when they need you, on your computer or your phone. Remote Access, which opens Kepler on a phone, needs a paid plan. The following table covers watching and answering your agents:

What you want to do Short answer Full details
See what every agent is doing The Agent Graph draws every Task, session, turn, tool call, subagent, and file the agents touch. It updates live as they work. The Agent Graph
Find out what needs you Open Ask Kepler and ask. Its chat can see what's on your screen and check on your Tasks and sessions. Ask Kepler
Answer an agent's permission request The request pins to the top of the session. Choose Allow, Allow for this session, Allow always, Reject, or Reject always. Agent Sessions → Permission requests
Check on your agents from your phone Turn on Remote Access and scan its QR code with your phone's camera. Kepler opens in your phone's browser, and your work keeps running on your computer. Remote Environments → Using Kepler on a phone

Review and ship

An agent finishing isn't the same as work that's ready to ship. The following table covers reviewing an agent's work and getting it merged:

What you want to do Short answer Full details
Read what an agent changed Open the Task's worktree and read the diff in the Changes overlay. From there you can comment, stage, and commit. Review Changes
Undo an agent's change to a file Use Discard changes, which asks first. Your current version goes to the Trash (the Recycle Bin on Windows), so you can get it back. Review Changes → Staging and committing
Have a different agent review the work Set the Review Action to a different agent from the one that wrote the code. Actions → Review with a different agent
Apply the review comments on a pull request Fire Address Feedback on the pull request. It triages the comments, applies what's worth applying, and pushes back on the rest. Actions → The four built-in Actions
Open a pull request Kepler has no "create pull request" button, so the agent opens it, or you open it from a terminal or your Git host's website. Kepler attaches it to the Task once it exists. Review Changes → Opening a pull request
feature: Kepler Interfacecontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true7136 words · ~32 min1 TODO

"Kepler opens on your work - every issue and pull request assigned to you, alongside the tasks you already have running. Learn how to read it, work several items side by side, and start work from it."

Last updated: October 2026

Kepler opens on your work, not on an empty editor or a blank prompt. It has one interface instead of a set of views to switch between, and you shape it to fit how you work. See Arranging Your Work.

Kepler pulls every issue and pull request assigned to you across your connected trackers and Git hosts into one list, alongside the tasks you already have in motion. To start work, pick something that's already there instead of describing it from scratch.

Beyond this list, you'll find a task's own page, Settings, and remote connections. The top bar displays the following, leading edge first:

  • The window switcher
  • Back and Forward, in the desktop app only
  • New task
  • Your setup progress, only until the primary setup steps are done
  • The remote indicator, the environment chip for the machine this window works on
  • Remote Access, a monitor-and-phone button whose colour shows whether Remote Access is on. Hover it to turn Remote Access on or off and, while it's on, scan its QR code or copy its link. See Remote access.
  • Ask Kepler (Cmd/Ctrl+L), which opens a chat and quick terminals in a panel beside the page. See Ask Kepler.
  • Feedback
  • Settings
  • Your account

The guided first-run flow owns the whole window, so Kepler doesn't draw the top bar there.

While an update downloads, a chip in the trailing cluster shows its progress as a ring and a percentage. Hover it for Downloading update (then Verifying update), the version, how much has arrived and about how long is left, and Keep working. Kepler will let you know when the update is ready to install.

When an update is ready, an Update available chip takes its place. It opens a popover that says what a restart would do to running work (how many agents it stops, whether terminals close) and offers Restart to update. When agents and terminals can survive the restart, the popover says Running agents and open terminals keep going through the restart. The same chip shows on every screen, so an update never waits on a banner you scrolled past.

On Windows and Linux the native title bar is gone and Kepler draws its own. The menu button at the leading edge holds the full application menu (File, Edit, View, Window, Help), including Settings, Check for Updates…, and About. macOS keeps its native menu bar, and both menus open the same About Kepler dialog.

The top bar sheds controls as the window narrows based on the room actually available, rather than at fixed widths, so a control disappears only when it will not fit.

Menu items and button tooltips show a command's keyboard shortcut beside it, wherever the item does exactly what the key does. The full list is in Settings.

The window switcher

Windows in the top bar lists every open Kepler window and what its agents are doing, so you don't have to go and look. Its trigger carries the reason to: Windows · {count} waiting elsewhere, or {count} running elsewhere. This window marks the one you're in, and New window (Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+N) opens another on the same connection as the window you're in. From a remote window, New local window opens one on this computer instead. The same list is on the tray icon.

Each window's own title leads with the remote it's connected to and how many of its sessions are waiting on you. Each window keeps its own layout, and if you close the last window while Kepler keeps running, the next one opens where that window was.

New task

New task (Cmd/Ctrl+N) opens the Task Composer. To start a task from a conversation you began outside Kepler, use Continue an external session in the composer. See Create a Task.

Todo, Tasks in progress, and the Agent Graph

The control at the top left switches between 3 views of your work.

Segment What it shows
Todo Issues and pull requests related to you on each connected integration, and not yet picked up. What counts as related depends on the provider (see below)
Tasks in progress The tasks you already have running in Kepler
Agent Graph A live visualization of every task, session, turn, tool call, and file. See The Agent Graph

Providers differ in what they can be asked for, so what Todo lists depends on the integration:

Integration Issues Pull requests
GitHub, GitHub Enterprise Yours, assigned to you or mentioning you Yours, assigned to you or ones you review
GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server Yours or assigned to you Yours or awaiting your review
Bitbucket Cloud None Yours, or awaiting your review in your workspaces and repositories added to Kepler
Bitbucket Data Center None Yours or awaiting your review
Jira, Jira Server, Linear, Trello Assigned to you None

The Related to me chip in Todo's header lists the same thing for each integration you have connected, across every organization you belong to; hover it to read it. To browse issues beyond it, use New task → Issues → All visible.

Todo is where a working session starts; Tasks in progress is where it lives while underway. The Agent Graph is a segment of its own rather than a way of arranging the list, because it replaces the list instead of laying it out. It scopes over every task, narrowed by your search and filters, and the Group and View controls step aside while it's on.

When launching the app, Kepler opens on Tasks in progress. When you start Kepler for the first time (like on a new machine), a guided setup takes over the whole window to connect your agents and trackers, and Kepler covers the list with a loading screen while it decides whether to send you there. See Getting Started with Kepler. With no tasks yet, Tasks in progress shows the Task Composer under Your agents' work lives here; see Empty states.

To populate the Todo tab, first connect at least one provider. See Issue Tracker Integrations and Pull Request Integrations.

External sessions

Todo lists only issues and pull requests. Agent conversations running outside Kepler no longer have a section of their own in the list, and they don't open as panels. To pick one up, open New task and use Continue an external session in the Task Composer. See Create a Task.

For what those sessions are and what you can do with one, see Agent Sessions.

Reading a row

A row reads differently depending on whether it holds a tracked issue or pull request, or a task you already started. The next 2 sections cover each case.

Todo rows: issues and pull requests

Each row shows, left to right:

  • The provider
  • A type badge (Issue or PR) with the reference (issue key or PR number)
  • The title
  • Your role on a pull request
  • The repository
  • A status pill
  • Any session activity: the session dots of the task linked to the item, and that task's running terminals
  • The people: a pull request's author, with a face for each reviewer ringed in the colour of their review (green for approved, red for changes requested), or an issue's assignee
  • When it last changed
  • An Action button

Hover the people for one card that names everyone on the item and where each reviewer's review stands.

An issue that's on your list only because it mentions you carries a muted Mentioned badge, so you can tell it apart from work assigned to you.

When a provider fails to load, Kepler keeps showing the rows from its last successful load and marks each one's provider chip with a dashed outline and a crossed-out cloud. Hover the chip for Not refreshed: shown from the last successful {provider} load.

Right-click a Todo row for its menu, which leaves the open panel alone:

  • Open task page, when the item already has a task
  • Open on {provider}
  • Copy link (Cmd/Ctrl+C on a focused row)
  • Copy title
  • Copy number
  • Copy branch name, on a pull request

When the list is narrow, each row folds onto two lines instead of dropping cells. On the Board layout, a pull request's card also shows its branch.

Your role on a pull request is one of:

Role Meaning
Yours You authored the pull request
Review You're a reviewer on it
Unattributed The provider did not say

Task rows

Task rows show:

  • The title
  • The repository, or every repository when the task spans several (app, api +2 beyond two)
  • The task's pull request and issue as chips, each with its state, and +N when there are more
  • A progress control
  • What the task needs from you
  • Session activity, and a pill for its running terminals
  • Time

The needs cell gives one reason, the most pressing first:

  • Approval needed for {tool}
  • Asking: {question}
  • Waiting for you
  • Terminal waiting for you, when a program in one of the task's terminals is waiting on you
  • A pull request's verdict, such as Changes requested
  • New reply

A task with a terminal waiting on you shows its title in bold, like one with a session waiting. The terminals pill draws a square for each running shell and a triangle for each running repository command, in the session-state colours. Hover it for Open terminals, or click it to open the most urgent terminal. Click the session dots to open the most urgent session.

A Done task that's ready to archive also gets an Archive task button on its row. When the list is narrow, each row folds onto two lines: the title and time on top, and the rest below.

Task rows carry no type badge. Everything in Tasks in progress is a task, so a chip saying so earned nothing and cost the width the timestamp needed; a narrowing row drops other cells before it drops the time.

A task that spans several repositories is listed under each of them when the list is grouped by repository, rather than under only the first one.

The task's own operations sit behind the ⋮ menu, and on a right-click:

  • Open task page and Open in new window
  • Reopen, while the task is Done, and a Progress submenu. See Arranging Your Work.
  • Mark as read, when any of the task's sessions is unread, which marks them all read. Otherwise Mark as unread.
  • Rename task (F2 on a focused panel)
  • Move up, Move down, and Move to top of {section}, while the list is sorted by Manual. See Arranging Your Work.
  • Copy task link (Cmd/Ctrl+C on a focused row), a kepler:// link to the task
  • Copy task prompt
  • Add resource…
  • Refresh, which re-reads the task's worktrees and its linked pull requests and issues. While the provider asks Kepler to wait, the row is locked and says how long to wait.
  • Shut down all agents
  • Archive task, or Restore task when the task is already archived
  • Delete task

Task rows deliberately have no reference column and no Action button. A task's reference is the head of its identifier and names nothing you'd recognize, so the title takes that cell instead. Firing an Action from a Todo row is how an untracked issue or pull request becomes a task in the first place; you manage a task that already exists from its ⋮ menu.

Status

Both segments carry a status, but they answer different questions.

Where Values What it means
Task rows Exploration, In Development, In Review, On hold, Done, Archived Where the work has got to. Kepler derives it from the task's checkouts and pull requests unless you or an agent set it by hand. In Development reads uncommitted changes or commits ahead of the branch's recorded base; In Review needs an open or draft pull request of the task's own; Done needs at least one of the task's branches to have landed, with no open pull request and no commits left unlanded. On hold and Archived are filing decisions, not evidence
Todo rows Open, Draft, Merged, Closed for a pull request; the tracker's own state for an issue The provider's own state on the pull request, and the workflow state your tracker uses for the issue (such as a Linear In Progress or a Jira To Verify) rather than a flat Open

Each pull request and issue state has its own icon and colour, so a column of pills reads at a glance.

Done waits for the work to land, however it got there: a pull request merged normally, by rebase, or by squash; a branch that diverged locally after its pull request merged; or a branch merged into its base with no pull request at all. A task that's done but still has uncommitted changes or unpushed commits in a checkout reads Done · local changes, so you can decide whether to keep them before you archive.

When the uncommitted-changes reading behind a task's stage comes from a cache rather than a live check, the progress control's tooltip says when it was taken: Uncommitted changes as of {time}. Open the task to refresh them.

The progress control on a task row is also a menu: click it to set the stage yourself, or put the task on hold. A stage you set holds until the evidence catches up or moves on. See Arranging Your Work.

Each task row also carries one dot per distinct session state, with a count of each.

screenshotReplace — ade-session-dots-01: a v0.15 task row with two or more session states and a count above 1, one of them dormant so its dot is an outline, with the hover card open (its {count} agent sessions title and per-state tallies, for example 2 Waiting (ended), 1 Ready). The current figure stays until it's replaced.

This shows what the agents are doing, a separate question from where the work stands. The vocabulary is the agent's own:

  • Spawning
  • Ready
  • Running
  • Idle
  • Unread
  • Waiting
  • Error
  • Working in background
  • Reconnecting
  • Ended

A waiting session also says what it's waiting on: Permission, Question, Plan review, or Prompt not sent. A session with a Kepler approval request nobody has answered counts as waiting, even while its agent keeps working, and reads Approval needed (or Approval needed, destructive). A live session resting after a failed turn, such as a rate limit, an exhausted quota, or an overloaded agent, reads Error.

A dormant session draws as an outline rather than a filled dot. Hover the dots for a card titled with the number of agent sessions, then a count of each state, most urgent first; a state held by only one session names it. A dormant session is marked (ended), as in 2 Waiting (ended).

Kepler places a task at the furthest stage any of its checkouts reached, except Done. Done needs at least one of the task's branches to have landed, no open pull request of its own, and no checkout still carrying commits that haven't landed. A sibling checkout you never touched doesn't hold a task back.

Starting work from a row

Click the Action button on a Todo row to hand the item to an agent with its context already attached: the repository, the issue body, the branch, and the diff. One click, no copy-paste.

The left half of the button runs your preferred Action for that kind of item; the chevron opens the full list. The defaults are:

  • Plan for issues
  • Address Feedback for pull requests you authored
  • Review for everyone else's

All of these defaults are editable. See Actions.

Open a panel

  • Click a row to select it and open the side panel. A plain click replaces whatever was open, except for panels you've pinned.
  • Shift-click a second row to open both side by side. The panel holds one column per open item, each with its own chat, so you can work several tasks at once without leaving the list.
  • Cmd-click (Ctrl-click on Windows and Linux) to add one row to the open set, or to close it again.
  • Double-click a row to open the item's full task page. From a Todo row that's the page of the task behind it. A row with no task yet has no page, so a double-click opens its panel with the cursor in the prompt box instead.

From the keyboard, the list is one tab stop that you walk like a tree:

Key What it does
↑ / ↓ Move between section headers and rows. Landing on a header opens nothing
→ Unfold a folded section, or step into an open one
← Fold an open section, or climb from a row to its section header
Home / End Jump to the first or last row
Page Up / Page Down Move by a screenful
Space Open the focused row's panel, or close it if it's open
Enter On a row whose panel is open, move focus into that panel
Alt+Enter Open the item's task page, like a double-click

On the Board layout, ← and → cross to the next column instead. Tab leaves the list rather than walking every row, and when the focused row disappears, focus moves to its neighbour.

A new task appears in the list as soon as you press Start in the Task Composer, before its worktrees are ready. Its row shows a Preparing pill while Kepler sets it up, or a Failed pill if setup fails. You can't drag or select a task that's still being prepared. Its panel shows Kep, Kepler's mascot, narrating each step: Fetching {repo}. while a repository clones (with Cancel), Making your worktree., Starting {agent}., and Opening the chat. (or Opening the terminal.). If setup fails, Kep says That didn't go through. (or Task not started. after you cancel), with Edit and retry, so you can change the task and start it again, and Dismiss. Without Kep, the panel shows Getting your task ready, with Cloning {repo}… or Setting up worktrees…, and a failure reads Unable to start this task. When a task starts without some of its repositories, Kepler warns Some repositories weren't added and names them.

Shift-clicking ranges from your last plain click, the way it does in Finder or VS Code. You can open up to 16 panels at once. In the Columns panel layout, opening another closes the leftmost unpinned one. For Grid, see Lay panels out in a grid.

Every open panel gets its own column straight away. Until its item loads, the column shows a placeholder that says it's loading, that the item is gone, or that Kepler was unable to load it, with Try again when retrying can help. A pull request or issue opened while you're signed out reads Sign in to see this pull request (or issue) with Sign in. Every placeholder can be closed. A remembered panel for a task that's since been deleted closes on its own.

Resize. Drag the divider between two columns to resize the one on its left. Hold Shift as you drag to give every panel the same width at once. Double-click the divider, or press Home on it, to split the space evenly again. The list keeps a minimum width of its own, so a wide panel cannot shrink it too far. Kepler remembers the widths per window, across reloads, restarts, and segment switches.

Scroll. Once the panel strip grows wider than the window, it scrolls instead of squeezing the columns below a readable width. Scroll it sideways with Shift and the mouse wheel, or a sideways trackpad swipe, even over a terminal.

Reorder. Drag a panel by its header to a new place in the strip. A panel's Panel actions menu (⋮) offers Move panel left and Move panel right, and so do Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+Shift+← and Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+Shift+→. The older Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+← and Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+→ still work.

Maximize. Once two or more panels are open, Maximize panel in the ⋮ menu (or Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+M, or a double-click on empty header space) spreads one panel across the strip, leaving a sliver of each neighbour. Click anywhere outside it, or use the Restore panel button in its header, to put the layout back exactly as it was. Switching to another panel from the keyboard moves the maximize with you, so you can read panels one after another at full size.

Move between panels. Ctrl+Tab opens Recent panels, a switcher of your open panels and the ones a plain click replaced since, most recently used first, so you can switch back to an item you moved away from. Delete or Backspace closes the highlighted panel, or removes a panel that's no longer open from the list (Remove from recent panels). With two or more unpinned panels open, the switcher moves among the open ones only. Cmd/Ctrl+1 through Cmd/Ctrl+8 jump to a panel by its position from the left, and Cmd/Ctrl+9 to the last.

Starting a task from New task while you're on the list opens the new task as a panel and leaves the list where it was, rather than throwing you onto its page.

When the list reorders under you (a session finishes, or a task moves bucket), the row you have selected glides to its new position rather than jumping, so you can see where it went.

Open several at once. Open all, in the controls strip (or in Options when the window is narrow), opens every listed row in panels, up to the panel limit. Each section's header has the same button for that section alone, Open all {count} in this section in panels. When not everything fits, the button reads Open {opened} of {total} in panels, and Kepler tells you how many didn't fit.

Lay panels out in a grid

Open panels sit side by side in Columns by default. To see more of them at once, switch the Panel layout to Grid, from its button in the controls strip or with Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+L. The button shows the current layout, such as 3 × 2.

In the Panel layout popover, pick a Grid size from the matrix of cells. The default is 3 × 2, and cells past the 16-panel limit are faded and can't be picked. The footer says how many panels the size holds, and Up to 16 panels, fewer in smaller windows. Kepler keeps the size you picked but draws only what the window fits, and the button's tooltip says what this window shows. Reset sizes evens out rows and columns you've resized. On a phone, panels always use Columns.

In a grid:

  • Drag the dividers between grid columns (Resize grid column) and rows (Resize grid row). Hold Shift to resize every track at once, and double-click a divider or press Home on it to even them out.
  • Panels that don't fit the grid go on further pages that scroll sideways.
  • Drag a panel by its header to reorder it, row by row. Move panel up and Move panel down (Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+Shift+↑ and Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+Shift+↓) join the left and right moves.
  • Maximize panel spreads one panel over its page.
  • Panels in short cells start with their headers folded, until you unfold them.
  • Opening a panel never closes another. Once the grid is full, Kepler refuses the new one and says The grid is full ({count} panels). Close one to open another.

Pin a panel

A panel is a place you're browsing until you pin it. Pin panel, in the panel's ⋮ menu, holds it in place. After that:

  • A plain click on a row opens beside your pins instead of replacing them.
  • Following a link in Related swaps the panel out in place if it is not pinned, and opens the linked item beside it if it is.
  • Switching segments keeps the pinned panels and drops the rest.
  • Nothing evicts a pin when the open set is full.

One slot always stays unpinned so browsing never has to evict a pin; once every other panel is pinned, the menu row reads Keep one panel unpinned for browsing and will not take another. A pinned panel shows Unpin panel in its header to release it.

The side panel

Selecting a row opens a panel beside the list with everything about that item, as a stack of collapsible, resizable sections.

Kepler does not draw a section that has nothing behind it.

Section What's in it When it's there
Summary The issue or pull request description, with its labels shown as badges. You can select and copy the description text An issue or pull request that has a description. Tasks carry none of their own
Related ({count}) Linked issues and pull requests, counted in the heading so a folded pane still says how many There's at least one
What's running Session tabs and draft session tabs. A session tab holds the live agent conversation; a draft tab holds a prompt box plus the same Actions. This is the chat Always, on an item you can start work on. Resizable, but not foldable: it's what the panel is for
Plan The plan the conversation on screen has proposed The agent produced one
Changes The checkout's commits, working changes, and file diffs, in the side pane You opened a worktree's changes from its pill
Terminals Terminal tabs across the task's checkouts. A shell tab shows its program's status, such as Running, Needs you, Done or Failed, when the program reports one A terminal is open. Its ✕ takes the section away without stopping the shells; the header's terminal control and Cmd/Ctrl+J are the way back

Kepler sizes Summary and Related to their contents and then the chat takes whatever height is left over.

To reorder the tabs in What's running or Terminals, drag a tab to a new place. Kepler remembers each task's terminal tab order and selected terminal across reloads and in new windows. Closing the selected terminal moves you to the leftmost one left.

Pressing Delete on a focused session tab in What's running asks first: Archive session?, Stops the agent and moves {session} to Archived sessions. Restore it any time. The tab's own archive control and its menu row still archive in one click.

A plain shell tab takes the name of what's running in it: the app that reports its status, the job in front (such as pnpm dev), or on Windows the window title the program set. Back at the prompt it returns to its own label, such as Terminal 2. When the two differ, hover the tab to see the label and the job's command line or title. Hover a tab with a program status for how long the program has been in that state, its progress as a bar, and, for a program that's blocked or failed without saying why, what to do next, such as Approve it in the terminal.

If an agent fails after you start a session, What's running shows {agent} was unable to start with the agent's message. Press Retry to launch it again, or Open agent settings to fix the problem.

For unstarted issues or PRs, the side panel's primary button follows that item's preferred Action (Plan, Implement vs Review, Address Feedback).

In the side panel for a Task, the primary button reads Start, and once a session exists the composer becomes that session's chat, where it reads Send. If the preferred Action is None, no longer exists, or cannot aim at this kind of item, the button falls back to Start.

A task you created with a prompt but haven't started reads Start with the task prompt, or describe something else…. Press Start on the empty box and the task's prompt goes out as the first message; type something to send that instead.

The header carries:

  • The item's badges and reference
  • On an issue or pull request, the people on it: a pull request's author, its Assignees, and its reviewers, each ringed and badged by the state of their review with a tally such as 1 of 2 reviewed, or an issue's assignee. The reviewers fold to a line of faces
  • The title, with a dot when a session needs you: blue for an unread turn, orange for one waiting on you
  • The progress control, on a task
  • The terminal control, on a task
  • Restore panel, when the panel is maximized
  • Unpin panel, when the panel is pinned
  • Close panel (Cmd/Ctrl+W on the focused panel)
  • Panel actions (⋮): Close panel, Open task page, Open on {provider} for an issue or pull request, Pin panel, Maximize panel, Move panel left, Move panel right, Close other panels, and Close all panels. On a task, these rows join its Task actions menu. Maximize panel, the moves, and the two close-many rows appear only once more than one panel is open

Each pull request, issue, or artifact linked to a task has a chip under the header; hover one for its card, or click it to read it in the side pane. A pull request whose branch name was used before reads Opened from an earlier branch with the same name. The Resources chip lists the task's notes, files, folders, and links, plus a Related tasks group, and every row opens in the side pane.

Hover a task's title to read its prompt, cut off after 10 lines, with the hint Double-click to rename. To copy the whole prompt, use Copy task prompt in the ⋮ menu. The prompt is a context item of its own, labelled Prompt, shown first among the task's notes in the card, the preview, and the task page's rail, and it can't be deleted.

To rename a task, double-click its title, choose Rename task from its ⋮ menu, or press F2 on its focused panel, and edit the name in place. Enter commits the change; Escape or clicking away discards it.

The side pane

A task panel reads its linked items, resources, and changes in a side pane, so you don't have to leave the list for the task page. These open in it:

  • A linked pull request or issue chip
  • A row of the Resources chip: a note, a file, a folder, a related task, or the task's links as one list
  • The uncommitted or commits chip on a worktree pill

When the panel is at least 960 pixels wide, the pane opens beside the sessions. Drag its edge (Resize side pane) to resize it, and close it with Close side pane or Escape. In a narrower panel, the pane takes the sessions' place, with Back to sessions to return. The pane shows the same view of the item as the task page does.

Click the same chip again to close the pane. The switcher in the pane's title row moves between the task's linked items and resources without closing it. Two shortcuts toggle it on the focused panel:

  • Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+G shows or hides the worktree's changes.
  • Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+E shows or hides the last resource the panel showed.

Kepler remembers the open pane for each item and the pane's width for each window.

Issue comments

An issue you open in a side pane, or on the task page, shows its discussion under the summary as Comments, with the count in the heading. Each comment has the author's avatar and name, how long ago it was posted, and its text. Kepler reads the newest 100 comments through the same connection it reads the issue with:

  • Show earlier comments reveals older ones that Kepler already loaded. When the issue has more than that, the section says that more may be available on the provider.
  • Refresh comments reads them again.
  • With none yet, the section reads No comments yet. When a read fails, it reads Unable to load comments. with Retry.

Comments work for GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Jira, and Jira Server issues. For other providers, when you're signed out, or when the connected account can't read the issue's comments, the section says so instead. Kepler shows comments to you; it doesn't hand them to the agent as task context.

The header folds down to its title row with the chevron beside it, for a task, a pull request, or an issue. A folded pull request still shows its number and review decision. Each segment remembers its own fold.

A Done task with no unpushed work and no agent working or waiting on you shows Ready to archive? in its header. See Arranging Your Work.

An archived task reads Archived where its progress control would be, in both its preview and its detail header, so you can't mistake a filed task for a live one.

The worktree pill

Below the header, each of the task's checkouts is a pill with two rows: who it is on top, and what's in it below.

Row What it shows What it does
Top The branch and repository, the worktree's own pull request, and the upstream control The branch name opens or closes the worktree's changes in the side pane (Open worktree changes / Close worktree changes); its ⋮ opens the worktree's menu (see below). The upstream control runs the verb its counts call for: Pull when behind, Push when ahead, Publish when the branch isn't on a remote yet, and Force push behind a confirmation when the branch has diverged or after you apply a rebase. Fetch and the upstream row are in its chevron
Bottom Chips for uncommitted files, commits, and the merge target, plus Open in and Run The uncommitted and commits chips open the worktree's changes in the side pane, on that scope, and the merge target chip shows how the branch compares with its merge target. Its own menu also offers Rebase onto {target} and Merge {target} into this branch, which open the update view. See Review Changes. Open in opens the checkout in your default editor, with the rest in its chevron. Run runs one of the repository's commands

Click the uncommitted or commits chip that's showing again to close the side pane, or the other one to switch scope.

The pill says what needs doing rather than just counting: unpushed commits, a paused rebase or merge you can continue or abort from the pill, a merge target it was unable to read with a retry, a detached HEAD explained, and No changes only when there really are none. Hover any chip for a card that leads with what a click does; right-click it for the worktree's menu.

The worktree menu is the same wherever a worktree appears, on the pill and on the task page:

  • Open changes (or Close changes while they're showing), Open in, and the worktree's pull request
  • Continue {operation} and Abort {operation} during a stopped rebase or merge
  • A Branch submenu: Rename branch, Rebase onto {target} and Merge {target} into this branch, Push, Pull and Fetch, Set upstream or Change upstream, and Change merge target…
  • New session here, New terminal here, and Run command here
  • Copy branch name, Copy worktree path, and Copy remote URL
  • Detach worktree… and Delete worktree…, or Recreate worktree for a checkout that's gone

Set or change the upstream. Set upstream (or Change upstream, which shows the branch it tracks now) opens a searchable list of remote branches, Search remote branches. A tracked branch that's gone comes first, marked Not found, then any remote's branch with the same name (Same name), then the rest; the branch tracked now is marked Current. Pinned above the list are Publish as {remote}/{branch}…, when no remote has a branch of the same name, and Unset upstream, when the branch tracks one. Refresh branches, beside the search box, fetches the list again. Any remote branch can be the upstream, under any name, and Push then goes there. The row is hidden on a detached HEAD. As the panel narrows, the chips shrink to their icons and counts rather than disappearing, and the branch name is cut last.

More than one worktree. A task's worktrees collapse into one pill, led by a count such as 1/3. Step through them with the switcher on the branch name, or with the up and down arrow keys on the pill. Until you pick one, the pill follows the worktree with the latest changes. The toggle on the pill's leading edge (Show all {count} worktrees) lays them all out at once. Worktrees whose branches have merged share a single row, with a way to delete them through the same cleanup list archiving uses.

From the keyboard, a pill is two tab stops (its changes and its actions), with the left and right arrows moving inside each.

The terminal strip's + becomes a chooser listing the task's checkouts by branch when the task has more than one.

An issue or pull request keeps a status line as well, with its provider state, session dots, repository, and last activity, and the people on it sit in the header. A task does not, because the row you clicked already showed that information.

Back and forward

Back follows one rule: it leaves the place or mode you're in. It never retraces a search you typed, a filter you set, or a change of view mode, so pressing it after narrowing the list takes you out of the list rather than walking back through every keystroke.

Mac Windows / Linux
Back ⌘ [ Alt + ←
Forward ⌘ ] Alt + →

The Back and Forward buttons in the top bar do the same, as do a mouse's side buttons. On macOS, a two- or three-finger sideways swipe on a trackpad or Magic Mouse goes back and forward too, wherever nothing under the pointer scrolls sideways. A sideways scroll or a mouse's tilt wheel never goes Back on Windows and Linux, so a stray scroll can't throw you out of what you were reading.

Each set of open panels gets its own history entry, so Back closes the set you just opened rather than the whole visit. Moving between Settings sub-pages replaces a single entry, so Back from Settings returns you to what you were doing before you opened it.

Empty states

What you see What it means
Your agents' work lives here You have no tasks. Tasks in progress shows the Task Composer, with hints to add an issue, a pull request, or a repository or folder, to start from a saved Action, or to Continue an external session. After the guided setup it reads You're set up above the heading
No tasks yet The Tasks list is empty
No assigned PRs or issues Providers are connected, but nothing related to you is open. Hover Related to me for what Todo lists
Nothing matches your search Your search, with or without filters, excludes everything. On Todo it adds Todo only searches work related to you. Use the button beneath it: Clear search or Clear search and filters
Nothing matches these filters Your filters exclude everything. Use Clear filters beneath it
Nothing here One column of the Board layout is empty while its neighbours are not
Hand your issues and PRs to agents You have no provider connected, so Todo lists every provider as a one-click connect, grouped under Code hosting and Issue trackers. The same view introduces Todo the first time you open it
Unable to load your work Every connected provider failed to load. The notice below names each one, what went wrong, and the fix, such as Reconnect or Retry
Unable to load your issues and pull requests. Loading the whole list failed. Press Retry
Sign in to see your assigned work Connect a GitKraken account to load the PRs and issues assigned to you.
Unable to find this task The task you opened was deleted, or its link is from another machine: It may have been deleted, or the link may be from another machine. An archived task still opens

Kep

Kep, Kepler's planet mascot, keeps empty and waiting screens company. He appears on the loading screen while Kepler starts, over a new session's empty chat, over empty views, in loading spinners, in the About Kepler dialog and What's New, and on the crash and sign-in error screens. When your search or filters empty the list, he sleeps above the message. On a crash screen, Show details reveals Error details, with Copy details for a bug report.

Kep's eyes follow your pointer, except when your system asks for reduced motion. Kepler can also show him without his speech card, or not at all, in which case the plain text and icons he replaces come back.

feature: Kepler Interfacecontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true4285 words · ~19 min

One interface means you shape it rather than switch away from it. Choose an arrangement, group, sort and filter the list, save a combination as a named view, set a task's progress, and archive in bulk.

Last updated: October 2026

Kepler has one interface rather than a set of views, so you shape it instead of switching away from it. Four controls do most of the work: the grouping, the sort, the filters, and the layout. Each segment remembers its own, and a combination you come back to can be saved under a name.

This page assumes you know what's on screen; see The Kepler Interface for that.

Two more controls sit in the strip. Open all opens every listed row in panels, and Panel layout lays open panels out side by side (Columns) or in a Grid. See Open a panel. Group and Sort open as menus that grow to fit the window, so a long list of groupings is never cut off.

When the window is too narrow for the whole strip, Group, Sort, Filter, the layout switch, Panel layout, and Open all fold into a single Options button, followed by the saved views and finally the search box. A count on Options says how many searches and filters are still narrowing the list, so a narrowed list never hides behind a closed popover.

Two layouts

The View control decides how Kepler draws the list.

View What you get Best for
List A dense list you read top to bottom Scanning everything at once
Board The same list turned on its side, one column per group Seeing how far along things are, and moving tasks between stages

List is the default, and each segment remembers its own choice across restarts, so Todo can stay a list while Tasks in progress stays a board. Both layouts respect the grouping and filters below.

Board draws the sections of the current grouping as columns side by side, so what the columns are is up to the Group control. Grouped by Progress, the columns are the lifecycle stages: Exploration, In Development, In Review, and Done. They stay put as work moves between them rather than appearing and vanishing under the pointer, and an empty one reads Nothing here. On hold and Archived are the exceptions: each appears when something is filed there, and both appear while you drag a card so you can drop onto them. On every other grouping, Kepler doesn't draw a section with nothing in it.

Every column on the board shares one width. Drag the gap after any column to resize them all at once, or focus it and use the arrow keys, with Home and End for the narrowest and widest. Double-click the gap to return to the default width. Each segment remembers its own column width. When a column has more to load, Load more sits at its foot. From the keyboard, ← and → cross to the nearest column with a card.

Columns now names a panel layout, not this view. The board was called Columns before; the Panel layout control's Columns and Grid decide how open panels are arranged instead.

The Progress board is also where you can move a task by hand. See Set a task's progress.

The Agent Graph is a segment, not a layout. It sits beside Todo and Tasks in progress and replaces the list rather than arranging it. Selecting it hides Group and View and gives the graph's own controls the strip. See The Agent Graph.

Group

The Group dropdown reorganizes the list.

The available groupings differ by segment.

Segment Group by Default
Todo Type, Needs my attention, Priority, Age, Your role, Assignee, Provider, Repository, Status, None Type
Tasks in progress Inbox, Progress, Activity, Repository, None Inbox

Inbox

Inbox is the default on Tasks in progress, and it is viewer-relative: it sorts by what each task is asking of you, not by where the work has got to. It reads top-down from what needs you toward what's filed away.

Bucket What lands there
Unread A session finished a turn you haven't looked at
Needs attention A session is blocked on a permission request or a question, a Kepler approval request is waiting for an answer, or a program in one of the task's terminals is waiting on you
Running An agent is working on it
Recent Moved in the last 24 hours
Earlier Moved before that
On hold You or an agent put it on hold. Starts folded
Done Finished, waiting only on the decision to archive it
Archived Your own filing decision, which outranks everything else

A session stays marked unseen until you actually look at it, so a turn that finished while you were elsewhere doesn't quietly clear itself.

Inbox lists every task exactly once. A task that is both unread and blocked on you is listed under Needs attention, because what's blocked on you asks more of you than output you haven't read. Running is exclusive too: a task already at the top doesn't need a second row saying it's busy. So is Done, which stands in for the time bucket a task would otherwise get.

Drop a card on an Inbox section. You can drag a task's card onto another Inbox section to act on it:

Drop on What happens
Unread Marks the task unread. Refused when it has no sessions, is archived, or is already waiting on you
Recent or Earlier Marks an unread task read
On hold Puts the task on hold. Refused when it's already done
Done Marks the task done. Refused while an agent is still working
Archived Opens the archive confirmation
Needs attention or Running Refused: these clear when you respond, or show while an agent works

See Set a task's progress for how a drag looks.

A task stays in Running for 10 seconds after its work stops, so it doesn't drop out between one prompt and the next, and running tasks keep the order they started in rather than trading places as they work. Moves into Unread and Needs attention are immediate, since those are news.

On hold is your "not now", so a held task stays there even while an agent works on it. News still brings it back up: an unread turn or a blocked prompt lists it under Unread or Needs attention.

The 24-hour window is fixed rather than a setting: the axis is meant to be a quick daily sweep, not a tunable report.

The other axes

Progress and Activity are two different questions about the same tasks. Progress is how far the work has got: Exploration, In Development, In Review, On hold, Done, Archived. Activity is what the task's agent sessions are doing right now, in the sessions' own words: Waiting, Running, Ready, Spawning, Unread, Idle, Error, and Ended. Grouping by Activity also adds No sessions for a task nobody has started, then Done, and Archived last. Group by Progress to see the shape of the pipeline; group by Activity to see what needs a person.

Repository is the only axis that can list a task twice: a task bound to worktrees in several repositories appears under each of them, because the axis answers "what's happening in this repository" one repository at a time.

Tasks in progress has no Status grouping, because a task's status is its progress stage, a second axis under another name.

On Todo, Needs my attention is the viewer-relative axis: what each item is asking of you right now, most pressing first:

  • Ready to merge
  • Changes requested
  • Cannot merge yet
  • Needs my review
  • Re-review
  • Approved, comments open
  • Comments
  • Assigned to me
  • Filed by me, unassigned
  • No action needed, last, where it can't push a real obligation below the fold

Each section header carries a ? button (About {label}) that explains what lands there. For example, Ready to merge is Yours, approved, no open feedback and no blockers the provider can see. It only needs merging. It's an axis rather than a pinned section so it obeys the same rules as every other grouping: one bucket per state, collapsible headers, and no row shown twice.

On Todo, Priority reads an item's priority from its labels: names such as P0 to P4, a priority: prefix, or a bare blocker, critical, urgent, highest, or lowest, with the most urgent label winning. It groups into Urgent, High, Medium, Low, and Unprioritised; every pull request groups under Not applicable. Kepler doesn't read a tracker's own priority field, so an issue with no such label is Unprioritised.

Age groups by when an item was created: Today, This week, This month, Older, and No date. Assignee groups by who an item is assigned to on each provider, with Unassigned for the rest.

On Todo, Status groups issues by their tracker's own workflow state, such as a Linear In Progress or a Jira To Verify, rather than collapsing every open issue into one bucket.

Items with no repository or provider collect under No repository and No provider. A pull request whose provider didn't say who authored it groups under Unattributed; an issue, which has no author-or-reviewer axis at all, groups under Not applicable.

Fold a section

Every group header is a fold toggle, with the section's count beside its name.

Folding one unmounts its rows, so a long Done list stops costing anything to keep around. On the Board layout, a folded column shrinks to a narrow rail with its name and count, marked when it holds the open item; click the rail to expand it. Archived starts folded when you group by Activity or Inbox, since it's the section that work is filed into, and so does On hold in the Inbox. Folds last only for the current run; Kepler doesn't write them to disk.

Sort

The Sort control orders the items inside each group without changing the order of the groups themselves.

Segment Sort by Default
Todo Updated, Created, Name, ID, Type Updated
Tasks in progress Updated, Created, Name, Manual Updated

Dates sort newest first; Name and ID sort ascending. ID compares references naturally, so #9 comes before #10. Type puts pull requests before issues and keeps the recency order within each. Each segment remembers its own sort.

Put tasks in your own order

Manual sorts Tasks in progress in an order you set yourself, inside each section, in any grouping, on List or Board. To move a task:

  • Drag its row or card to a place in its own section. A line with a dot shows where it will land, and under Manual a row shows a drag grip on hover.
  • Choose Move up, Move down, or Move to top of {section} from the task's ⋮ or right-click menu. These rows appear only while the list is sorted by Manual.

You don't have to switch to Manual first. Drop a task in a new place in its own section while the list is sorted another way, and Kepler saves the order you see, with your move, as the Manual order and switches the sort to Manual. Switching to Manual yourself also keeps the current order, so nothing jumps.

When you already have a saved Manual order, a drop under another sort would replace it, so Kepler asks first, in a popover beside the dropped task: Replace your Manual order? Choose Switch and replace Manual to keep the move, or Cancel (or press Escape, or click away) to put the task back.

If a move fails, Kepler says Unable to move the task, and puts the sort back if the move had switched it.

Set a task's progress

A task's progress normally follows its git and pull-request evidence (see Status). Sometimes that evidence can't see what you know: a review was sent back, or the work landed somewhere Kepler can't tell. When that happens, set the stage yourself.

  • The progress control. The stage word on a task row, in the preview header, and in the task page header opens a Progress menu: Automatic (Updates as you work), then Exploration, In Development, In Review, On hold, and Done. Where there's no room for the word, the control shrinks to the stage's icon rather than disappearing.
  • The task menus. A task's ⋮ and right-click menus carry the same choices under a Progress submenu, plus Reopen while the task is Done. Its hint says where the task will land (Moves it to In Review).
  • Drag a card. In any grouping, on List or Board, drag a task's card or row. Grouped by Progress, drop it on another stage. On touch, press and hold to pick it up. Escape changes nothing, and dropping it in its own section reorders it (see Put tasks in your own order). You can't drag a task that Kepler is still preparing.
  • The Mark as bar. While you drag, a Mark as bar floats over the controls strip with chips for what the card can become: Read or Unread, the stages, Reopen for a Done task, and Archive task, or only Restore for an archived one. Drop the card on a chip.
  • From the keyboard. With a card focused on the Board grouped by Progress, Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+← and Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+→ step it one column, stopping at Exploration and Done.

While you drag, the card stays where it was, faded, and a line shows where it would land. Each section that takes the card has a dashed outline that goes solid under the card; sections that refuse it fade and say why when you hover them. On the Board, a folded column springs open after you hold the card over it for half a second.

The control's tooltip says whether the stage Updates as you work or was Set manually.

How a manual stage meets the evidence. A stage you set doesn't fight git; it waits for it.

  • Set a stage ahead of the evidence (Done before the merge, In Review before the pull request), and it holds until the evidence reaches it.
  • Set a stage behind the evidence (Reopen, or a review sent back to In Development), and it holds until the evidence changes at all, so a reopened task still follows its next pull request.
  • Once the evidence has caught up or moved on, the task goes back to following it. Pick Automatic to hand it back sooner. Dropping a card on the column the evidence already shows does the same thing.

Reopen on a task you marked Done falls back to what git and the pull requests show. On a task the evidence finished, it sets In Development, and the task's next pull request carries it forward again.

On hold parks a task without saying anything about how far it got. It reads On hold where the stage would be, with a tooltip saying who held it and where it stood before. Picking any stage or Automatic lifts the hold, and so does the task reaching Done, so a merged pull request ends it on its own. A task already at Done can't be put on hold.

Dropping a card on Archived opens the archive confirmation, and dragging an archived card out restores it first. An archived task's control reads Archived as a plain label, with no menu.

Agents can set the stage too, and put a task on hold, through Kepler's workspace tools. An agent that lifts a hold you placed tells you so.

Filter

The Filter menu holds one flyout per facet.

Kepler hides facets with nothing to offer, so a single-repo setup won't show a Repository facet at all.

Segment Facets
Todo Type · Linked work · Status · Needs my attention · Priority · Age · Your role · Provider, then one section per connected provider with its own Repository · Assignee · Issue type · Label · Project · Iteration or Sprint
Tasks in progress Progress · Activity · Repository · Agent · Session · Linked work

Issue type, Label, Project, and Iteration carry your provider's own vocabulary (a Jira issue type, your team's tags, a board name), not a Kepler-side translation of them. Azure DevOps calls the last one Iteration and Jira calls it Sprint, so the facet uses each tracker's word.

Linked work filters by whether an item is connected to work on the other side: Has a task in Todo, and Has a PR or issue in Tasks in progress. Session offers Has a session and Has a live session. To find the items without one, exclude the value.

Include or exclude any value. Click a value to include it. Hover a value and press its minus button (or Shift+Enter from the keyboard) to exclude it instead; an excluded value reads not {value}. On Todo, excluding a provider's value only subtracts from that provider's items, so excluding a GitHub label never hides a Jira issue.

Included values within one facet combine as a union. Ticking GitHub and GitLab under Provider shows items from either, not items that are somehow both. Excluded values subtract: an item that matches any of them is dropped. A facet with nothing chosen reads Any and narrows nothing. The Search filters… box finds a facet value without scrolling, and the menu reports {count} of {total} as you narrow. The Filter button names one chosen value and counts the rest, included in blue and excluded in red.

Refresh re-fetches the current segment from your providers.

Search

The search box matches on title, reference, or repository: Search title, ref, or repo….

Once a search or a filter is narrowing the list, pills at the right end of the search box say by how much, and each one clears only itself:

Pill What it counts Clicking it
The search pill The rows on screen out of every row: {count} of {total} Clears the search
The filter pill The rows your filters hide: {count} hidden Clears the filters

While a search is on, the filter pill counts only hidden rows that match the search, so a search that seems to miss something shows the filters hiding it. On Todo, which loads in pages, the counts read 40+ or 7+ hidden until everything has arrived.

When nothing matches, the empty list reads Nothing matches your search (or Nothing matches these filters when only filters are narrowing it) and offers a button named for what it resets: Clear search, Clear filters, or Clear search and filters.

Search covers the work already loaded. On Todo, that's the work related to you on each connected integration, so a miss there adds Todo only searches work related to you. See Todo, Tasks in progress, and the Agent Graph for what each integration lists.

Saved views

The Saved views chip, just after the search box, keeps a combination you use often under a name and takes you back to it.

A view captures:

  • The segment
  • The search text
  • The grouping
  • The filters
  • The layout (List or Board)

Picking a view restores all of it, including switching to its segment. The chip then reads View: and the view's name, and a checkmark marks it in the menu. Open preview panels aren't part of a view, so applying one never reopens a task or pull request that has since gone.

Menu item What it does
Save current view… Names the current combination and saves it as a new view
Update saved view Overwrites the applied view with your edits. Available only once you've changed something
Discard changes Puts the applied view back the way it was saved
Rename view… Renames the applied view
Delete view… Removes the applied view, after a confirmation

A dot on the chip means you've edited the applied view since you picked it. Views are shared by every window, and two views can't share a name: Kepler adds a number to a duplicate.

What Kepler remembers

Each segment remembers its own search, grouping, sort, filters, and layout, since they differ enough that sharing them would be meaningless. Kepler restores the segment you left along with its view. Kepler also remembers which panels were open, but only for the length of a run, so a round trip to the other segment doesn't cost you the panel you were reading. Panel widths are remembered per window across reloads and restarts; the set of open panels is not, so a relaunch never opens a panel for a task since deleted.

Saved views are the exception to per-segment memory: they're stored, named, and shared across windows until you delete them.

Archive, restore, and clean up in bulk

Archive task files a task away. It leaves the live buckets for Archived, its rows and sessions survive, and Kepler stops only the agents whose checkout is about to disappear.

When a task is settled at Done, none of its worktrees holds unpushed work, and no agent is working or waiting on you, the preview and task page headers suggest it: Ready to archive? with Archive beside it. Archive opens the usual confirmation; the X dismisses the suggestion for that task in every window.

The confirmation offers the same cascades the delete does: Also delete worktrees, Also delete branches (once worktrees is ticked), and Also delete the branch on the remote (once branches is ticked). This way, finishing with a task doesn't have to mean deleting it to clean it off the disk. With Also delete worktrees ticked, the dialog lists each worktree, riskiest first, with what will happen to it (Merged into main, 2 uncommitted files, In open PR #42). It also names the ones it keeps: the repository's main worktree, and worktrees another task still uses. A worktree that would lose work is kept unless you tick the extra box that names exactly what would be lost, and a row at risk can open its changes so you can judge first.

With Also delete the branch on the remote ticked, Kepler deletes each branch's remote copy after the local branch goes, unless a check says to keep it. A branch with no upstream, or a default branch, is kept without comment. Otherwise a Kept a branch on the remote toast says why, for example it has an open pull request, it has commits that are not on the remote, the remote has commits this branch does not, or the remote branch changed after it was checked.

Archiving outranks everything else, so a task you filed away can't climb back into a live section because a shell is still open on it.

Restore task takes the place of Archive task in an archived task's ⋮ menu, and is on the task page's own header too. It puts the task straight back. Only one active task can hold a given pull request, so if restoring would archive another task that holds the same one, Kepler names that task and asks first.

For more than one at a time, every section header on Tasks in progress carries a quiet Select. Press it, and that corner of the header becomes a bar: a select-all box, {count} selected, and the batch verbs as icon buttons, each named by its tooltip. The section's rows also grow checkboxes. One section selects at a time, and while you're selecting, a click ticks a row rather than opening its panel. A task that Kepler is still preparing has no checkbox, and select-all skips it.

Button What it does
Archive Archives every selected task that isn't already archived
Restore Takes Archive's place when everything selected is already archived
Delete Removes the selected tasks for good
Cancel Leaves selection mode and drops the set

Both Archive and Delete confirm, with Archive tasks or Delete tasks as the confirm button, and both offer Also delete worktrees, Also delete branches, and Also delete the branch on the remote. Deleting can't be undone, and the confirmation says so. The single-task dialog remembers what you ticked last time; the batch confirmation always starts with all three unticked. The batch confirmation doesn't list each worktree's fate. Instead, any worktree that would lose work is kept, and a toast afterwards says which ones and why. Open a task's own ⋮ to see the list before you confirm.

To clean up worktrees and local branches rather than tasks, open Manage worktrees and branches in Settings → Repositories. The Worktrees and branches page lists every worktree and every local branch no worktree holds, filtered by Safe to delete, Needs update, Conflicts, Stale, or All. From it you can Update many worktrees at once, bringing each branch up to date with its base, and delete worktrees and branches, including their copies on the remote. See Settings.

feature: Actionscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true1977 words · ~9 min

Actions are editable named prompts you fire at a task, issue, or pull request. Learn the four built-in Actions, how to change them, how to write your own, and how to have a different agent review the code than the one that wrote it.

Last updated: October 2026

An Action is a named prompt you fire at a task, issue, or pull request to start agent work.

Every Action is yours to configure. You pick the wording, you pick the agent, and you pick which Action is the one-click default for each kind of work.

The four built-in Actions

Kepler ships with four Actions. Every one of them is editable.

Action What it asks the agent to do Offered on
Plan Research the code first, write out the proposed approach, change nothing, and wait for your confirmation Tasks, Issues, Pull requests
Implement Read enough surrounding code to be confident, then make the changes directly rather than describing them Tasks, Issues, Pull requests
Review Review the pull request if one is attached, otherwise the uncommitted work in the worktree Tasks, Pull requests
Address Feedback Triage the review comments, apply what's worth applying, and push back on the rest instead of applying every suggestion blindly Pull requests

Kepler deliberately offers Review on plain tasks. Reviewing your own uncommitted work before you push is the obvious thing to want from a worktree. One flexible Review covers both cases, rather than splitting the list into "review a pull request" and "review my changes."

Every Action declares what it can be aimed at, which is why Address Feedback never appears on a task with no pull request.

You can edit and reset built-in Actions, but you can't delete them. What an Action applies to already controls where it shows up, so deleting one would be a weaker version of the same control.

Where the Action button appears

You fire Actions from a split button. The left half runs your preferred Action for that surface; the chevron opens the full list.

Surface Preferred Action comes from
A row in Todo (an issue or pull request) the row's own kind of item
The side panel, on the selected item the selected item's kind

Firing an Action from a Todo row is also how an issue or pull request that isn't tracked yet becomes a task in Kepler.

On a Todo row, the button rests as a quiet outline and fills in only while you hover or select its row, so a column of buttons doesn't compete with the rows. On a narrow card it shrinks to a play icon. On a phone, a row has a single ⋮ menu instead (Actions for {title}) that lists every Action, the preferred one included, and the side panel has no Action button.

In the list of Actions, hover a row, or move focus to it, to read that Action's prompt, line breaks included.

Inside a task, Actions live under the chevron on the composer's send button rather than on a split button of their own: type a refinement, then pick the Action to fire it with. In a composer that starts something new, such as the Task Composer or a panel's draft, picking an Action doesn't fire it: it sets the main button to that Action, and shows the Action's own agent in the agent pill if it names one, so you can check what will run before you press it. See Create a Task.

Task rows don't carry an Action button. A task's own operations live behind the ⋮ menu on the row instead.

A task row has no Resume button. Clicking the row selects it and shows its sessions, the way to look at running work without sending anything. Firing an Action on live work takes you to the session that Action landed in instead.

Firing an Action

An Action fires immediately unless Kepler is unclear on where to run the action.

The left half: one click

The left half of the button runs your preferred Action straight away, in the current session if one exists. It only stops to ask when the task has two or more worktrees.

The number of issues or pull requests attached to the task doesn't change what the Action does. An Action's prompt is its own content, word for word, and every attached item ships to the agent as shared context. All of it is the work. If you want the agent to look at one thing first, say so in the prompt box or in the chat; that's one sentence, and it's better in your own words.

The chevron: the deliberate path

Picking an Action from the chevron list always goes on to ask where to run it, whenever there is anything at all to choose, including new-session-vs-current on a task with a single worktree, which the left half answers silently.

At the bottom of the list, Manage Actions… opens Settings → Actions.

From the keyboard, the two halves are separate Tab stops, the Action first. Down Arrow or Alt+Down on the Action opens the list too, and closing the list puts focus back on the Action. Every split button in Kepler (Send, Open in, Run) works the same way.

Choosing where it runs

The Run in view asks two independent questions, and both have small answers.

New session, or a current one? A two-way toggle between New session and Current session. Current session lists every reusable session with its worktree, so clicking a row queues the Action into that exact session.

Which worktree? In New session, the rows are All N worktrees, A new worktree, and then one row per branch. In Current session, individual sessions replace branches that already have sessions.

Clicking any row runs the Action immediately, with no separate confirm step.

Two things worth knowing:

  • A worktree with no session open still runs under Current session. It starts one there, so all worktrees always means all of them.
