  • Kepler navigates to the first destination you picked, not to whichever session happened to start first.

Worktree choices are resolved against worktrees that actually exist on disk, so a task that still names a worktree you've since removed won't silently run somewhere else.

A task with no worktree runs the Action in a folder attached to it, or else in the task's own folder. With neither, Kepler says This task has no folder or worktree to start a session in.

Firing into a running session

Firing into a live session queues the prompt behind whatever the agent is currently doing. Nothing is dropped and nothing is cancelled, and Kepler takes you to the session the prompt went into.

Set your preferred Actions

To set your preferred Actions, open Settings → Actions → Preferred Actions.

Each row sets the Action that the left half of the button runs for that kind of item.

Setting Default
Tasks Implement
Issues Plan
Pull requests I authored Address Feedback
Pull requests from others Review

Pull requests split by who wrote them, not by what you want to do with them. Kepler works out review-vs-address-feedback from authorship, so the settings name the kind of pull request rather than the intent. When you want the other one, it's one click away in the chevron.

Any slot can be set to None. This simplifies the action button to only display the chevron icon.

Edit an Action

To edit an Action, go to Settings → Actions and click the Action's row under All Actions. The editor opens as its own page.

Once you have a custom Action, the list splits into Built in and Custom. Until then, it shows the built-in Actions without labels.

The editor has these fields:

Field What it does
Title The name shown on the button and in the chevron list. Required: Title can't be empty.
Prompt Sent to the agent word for word. It Can be, or start with, one of your skills: type / (or $ for Codex) to pick one. Required: Prompt can't be empty.
Applies to Tasks, Issues, Pull requests: controls where the Action is offered. Pick at least one
Agent Which agent runs it. See below

Create your own

To create an Action, click New Action in Settings → Actions.

Custom Actions work exactly like the built-in ones, and they're the point of the feature: if you'd do something more than once (backport a fix, write the migration, audit a dependency bump), it should be a button rather than a prompt you retype.

Restore defaults

To undo your edits to one Action, click Restore default on its row. The row shows Restore default only after you edit the Action. To undo your edits to every Action at once, click Restore all defaults in the All Actions section header.

Kepler stores only what you changed, not a copy of the whole Action. This creates the following considerations when restoring defaults:

  • An Action you've never edited picks up improved wording in a future Kepler release.
  • Restore default works by forgetting your edit. Editing an Action back to its original wording has the same effect.

Deleting a custom Action that a preferred-Action slot points to is the same as setting it to None. You will see rows with only the chevron, instead of the preferred-Action + the chevron. Revisit the Actions setting to set a new preference.

Setting an Action's agent

By default an Action runs on your default agent, configured in Settings → Tasks → New tasks → Default agent. The Agent field in the Action editor reads Inherit until you name one.

Naming an agent is an all-or-nothing override: it pins the provider, account, model, mode, and options together, rather than changing one of them and inheriting the rest. This is what makes per-Action cost tuning work: a cheap, fast model for triage and review, a heavier one for implementation.

Three rules govern which agent actually runs:

  • A model belongs to its provider, so a pinned model never runs on a different agent.
  • If an Action names an agent you don't have installed, the override falls back whole to your default agent.
  • An agent you pick explicitly at the point of firing beats an Action's override.

Review with a different agent

You can have one agent write the code and a different agent review it. A reviewer that didn't write the code is less likely to repeat the author's mistakes.

To give Review its own agent, take the following steps:

  1. Go to Settings → Actions, and click Review under All Actions.
  2. Name a different agent in the Agent field from the one that writes your code. Implement uses your default agent unless you've changed it.
  3. Click Save.

Review now runs on that agent, whether you fire it at a Task's uncommitted work or at an attached pull request. The override is all-or-nothing, so it also pins that agent's account, model, and mode.

To use a different reviewer just once, pick the agent when you fire Review. An agent you pick at that point beats the Action's override.

The review is a report, not an approval. You read it in the session and decide what to change before you commit.

feature: Taskscontent_type: conceptstatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true6116 words · ~28 min

A Task is the unit of work in Kepler. Learn what a Task holds, how worktrees work, and how everything attached to a Task reaches your agents.

Last updated: October 2026

A Task is the unit of work in Kepler. It's one thing you're trying to get done, plus everything the agents working on it need: the repositories, the issue or pull request it came from, the branches, the notes, and every agent session you've run against it.

A Task doesn't have to start with much. You can start one from an issue in the Kepler interface and it arrives with the repository and issue body already attached, or you can start one with nothing at all (no repository, no branch), chase an idea, and attach the real work to it later.

What a Task holds

Everything attached to a Task is a resource.

Attach them from Add resource at the bottom of the task view's rail, or from the + on any group header, which opens the same dialog on that category. The dialog's title bar says what it adds. Offering everything a Task can hold, it reads Add to this task, with a row of tabs beneath it: Repos & Folders, Issues, Pull requests, Notes, Links and Files. Opened for a single kind, the title names that kind, such as Add a note or Add a link.

Resource What it is
Sessions Agent conversations running against this Task
Terminals Shells opened inside one of the Task's working directories
Worktrees Git working copies the agents make changes in. Grouped as Changes in the rail, because that's what you go there to read
Folders A directory that isn't a Git repository. The agent works on it in place
Files A single file, when the whole folder isn't the point: either linked from disk by its path, or a copy uploaded to the Task
Pull requests Pull requests from your connected Git hosts
Issues Issues from your connected trackers
Links Any URL worth keeping with the work
Notes Text you write: standing instructions, decisions, reference material. The Task's own Prompt leads this group

A Task can hold more than one of any of these. Several repositories, several issues, several pull requests: a Task spanning three repos with two linked issues is a normal Task, not a special case.

For the screen itself (the rail, the columns, split panes, tabs), see The Task View.

Picking issues and pull requests

The Issues and Pull requests tabs list work from your connected providers, under a search field and a row of filters. Each row shows the item's title, a line with its identifier, repository and provider, and its age. Show preview, beside the search field, splits the list with a preview of the focused item: its repository, author, labels and description. Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+P does the same, and Kepler remembers the choice on this computer. The preview needs room, so a narrow dialog doesn't offer it.

The list works from the keyboard:

Key What it does
↓ from the search field Moves into the list
↑ / ↓, Home / End Moves between rows
Space Selects or clears the focused row
Enter Adds what you selected, or the focused row when nothing is selected

Double-click a row to add just that one. The main button counts what you picked, as in Add to task · 1, and Add & keep adding adds the selection without closing the dialog.

Pasting an issue link

The Links tab takes any URL. Paste an issue's URL and Kepler looks it up (Looking up issue…) before you can add it. When it finds the issue, the field reads Adds {provider} issue {key}: {title}, and the link is added as that issue, under its own title, rather than as a plain link. To keep it as a plain link anyway, press Add as plain link.

When the lookup finds nothing, or can't reach the provider, Kepler says so and adds a plain link instead:

  • No issue found for that link. It may not exist or you may not have access; it will be added as a plain link.
  • Unable to look up that issue. It will be added as a plain link.

Files: linked or uploaded

The Files group holds two kinds of file, and Kepler treats them differently because only one of them is Kepler's to destroy:

Kind Where it lives Its row reads Removing it
Linked file Wherever it already was on disk. Kepler links to it by its path and never changes it Its path Detach file… leaves the file where it is
Uploaded copy Inside the Task's own folder, and belongs to this Task alone Task folder Delete file… deletes the copy

A file attached to a prompt that Kepler copies into the Task when you send it is an uploaded copy too. In a window connected to another machine, the file picker on the Files tab of Add resources also offers Upload from this machine, which copies files from the machine you're sitting at into the Task's folder. Either kind can be written somewhere else with Save as… from its menu.

Issues and pull requests attach themselves

You don't have to remember to attach the pull request. When a Task holds no pull request yet and one of its worktrees is sitting on a branch that exactly matches an open pull request's head branch, Kepler records that pull request as a resource on its own, however the pull request was opened, including from a terminal, from GitKraken, or on the web.

This auto-attach behavior is deliberately narrow, because a wrong guess would put a stranger's work into your agent's context:

  • The branch names must match exactly. A near-miss doesn't count.
  • Kepler attaches only open pull requests whose head branch lives in the same repository. A pull request from a fork never auto-attaches.
  • If another active Task already owns the pull request, Kepler leaves it there.

Issues attach the same way. An issue whose branch matches one of the Task's checkouts is correlated and attached on its own, so a Task started from a branch name finds the ticket behind it.

Auto-attach only adds a link; it never removes one. Detach it yourself if you don't want it on the Task.

Work item URLs attach as issues. When you or an agent attaches one of the following URLs, Kepler records it as an issue rather than a plain link:

  • A GitLab work item, such as https://gitlab.com/{group}/{project}/-/work_items/{number}
  • An Azure DevOps work item, such as https://dev.azure.com/{organization}/{project}/_workitems/edit/{id}, or the organization-level https://dev.azure.com/{organization}/_workitems/edit/{id}

A GitLab work item that belongs to a group rather than a project stays a plain link.

Keeping them current

A Task's pull requests and issues refresh themselves when something that could change them happens: an agent ends a turn, one of the Task's branches is pushed, or a pull request is published. Kepler re-reads only that Task, so a busy Task doesn't cost a refresh of your whole account. Each link is looked up where it lives: in the repository it names, or on the Jira site or Linear workspace in its URL. Two repositories with the same issue number, or two Jira sites with the same key, don't get confused.

A Jira or Linear issue that moves to a new key stays attached under the link you added, and keeps refreshing from it.

To refresh by hand, use the refresh on the Pull requests or Issues group header, or Refresh in the Task's ⋮ menu. A refresh you ask for re-reads the provider rather than redrawing what Kepler already had, and it re-reads every stored link in full, merged pull requests included.

Worktrees, and when you don't need one

Most work benefits from a worktree: a private Git working copy for this Task. Agents make their changes there, so nothing they do touches the checkout you're working in, and several agents can run at once without stepping on each other.

When you add a repository to a Task through Add resource, the Isolated worktree toggle decides how it's set up. The Task Composer's own repository chip, used when you create a Task, reaches the same in-place-or-isolated choice through a different control with an added branch-name field. See Create a Task.

Setting What it says
On Its own folder. Nothing else can switch its branch.
Off Uses your repository folder. Anything that switches its branch switches it here too.

When the branch you picked is already checked out somewhere, Git can't check it out a second time, so the toggle locks and says where the Task will work instead: Uses {worktree}, which already has this branch. or Uses your repository folder, which already has this branch.

With isolation off, the picker reads directly in repo.

Turning isolation off means the Task has no branch of its own. The Task uses your repository folder exactly as it stands: nothing is copied and nothing is guaranteed. Use it when you deliberately want an agent working in the checkout you're sitting in; leave it on otherwise.

Kepler always isolates a repository linked to a pull request, and locks the toggle: This repository is linked to a pull request and always opens in its own isolated working copy.

You can also pick the branch when you attach the repository: Use this branch to work directly on an existing branch, or New branch to branch off the repository's default branch. Each option says where the work will happen, such as Branch off {branch} in a new worktree. Kepler names a new branch for you unless you set one. The branch list is read fresh each time you open it, and Refresh branches beside Find a branch… fetches from your remotes first.

A new branch forks from the repository's remote default branch, not from whatever branch you happen to have checked out. Naming a base yourself overrides that default. A local base forks from its upstream, unless it has commits you haven't pushed: then it forks from your local tip, so those commits come along. If no remote default can be resolved (no remote, a shallow clone, offline with nothing cached), Kepler falls back to the current HEAD rather than refusing to start.

Automatic branch names are suggested from the Task's own context, led by the issue identifier where there is one, and namespaced under kepler/ unless you configure another prefix. See Create a Task.

Prefer not to use a worktree? A Task does not require a worktree, or even a repository. Attach a plain folder and the agent works on it in place. Attach nothing and you have a space to think with the option to turn it into real work whenever the idea earns it.

When a worktree goes missing

A worktree deleted outside Kepler stays bound to its Task rather than quietly disappearing from it. The row is flagged Gone from disk, and the pane shows the metadata Kepler recorded: Current branch, Base branch, and Recorded path. Recreate worktree, on the pane or in the worktree's menu, brings the branch back at the recorded path. If the branch itself was deleted, Kepler restores it at its last commit when that commit still exists in the repository, history and all. Failing that, it creates the branch again from the recorded base, or from the repository's default branch when the base is gone too (as it usually is once its pull request merged), and says what it branched from. That way a git worktree prune doesn't cost you the Task's record of where the work lived. Recreating one worktree clears only that worktree's stale Git entry, so other missing worktrees, such as ones on a drive that's unplugged, keep theirs.

The same goes for an attached folder that's been removed. Start a new session in a worktree or folder that's gone and Kepler asks first (Recreate the worktree and start the session?), then rebuilds it before the session starts, rather than launching an agent into a directory that isn't there. A Task with no repository or folder at all starts its sessions in the Task's own folder.

Make a new worktree ready to build

A fresh worktree is a clean checkout: no node_modules, no build output, nothing your setup script would normally leave behind. An agent that starts there has to install dependencies before it can do anything, or it fails on the first build.

Commands solve that problem. Save a repository's setup steps once (pnpm install, a codegen step, whatever your project needs) and tick Run on worktree creation. Kepler runs them in the new worktree's folder every time it makes one for that repository, in order, before the agent starts. Commands you don't flag stay on demand: right-click a worktree in the task view rail and pick Run command here.

The worktree's menu is the same wherever the worktree appears. Beside Run command here it holds New session here, which opens a draft session in that worktree, and New terminal here. Its Branch submenu holds Rename branch, the upstream, Change merge target…, and Rebase onto {target} or Merge {target} into this branch. Below them come the copy actions, Refresh, and Detach worktree… and Delete worktree…. See Review Changes for the upstream and the update.

Set them up in Settings → Repositories, on the repository's own page. See Settings for the fields, the Variables a command can use, and what happens when one fails.

Shared context: how resources reach your agents

Kepler sends everything attached to a Task to every agent session in it as shared context. That's what a prompt means when it says "described in the shared context above": the Task's prompt, the issue body, the pull request, your notes, the links.

The prompt goes in full, framed as background to the work rather than as an instruction: the agent acts on the messages you send it. Each note goes as a reference, its title and size, and the agent reads the note's text when it needs it, through Kepler's MCP server. A session with no route to that server gets every note's text in full instead.

A launched Task brings its issues' descriptions along. When you launch a Task with linked issues, Kepler reads each issue fresh and saves its description as a note titled Issue {identifier}: {title}, headed by the issue's URL. Each description is capped at 8 KB, and all of them together at 16 KB; a longer one is cut short with a pointer to the URL. Agents are told the text comes from a third party, so they treat it as information rather than instructions.

You'll see it in the conversation as a collapsible row reading Task context added the first time, and Task context updated whenever it changes, with a count of the items included. Expand it to see exactly what the agent was given.

You don't edit shared context directly. You change it by attaching, detaching, and editing resources. A change reaches each session with its next prompt rather than interrupting an agent mid-turn.

The Task's prompt is context too

What you wrote when you started the Task is a context item of its own: the Prompt, at the top of the Notes group. It's what every session on the Task is working toward, so it rides along with the rest of the shared context, and you can edit it like a note when the goal shifts. Unlike a note, it can't be deleted.

Starting a session without the Task's context

Sometimes you want an agent that forms its own view, such as a reviewer that shouldn't be steered by the implementer's brief. To start a session without the Task's prompt and notes, follow these steps:

  1. Click + on the rail's Sessions group, or press Cmd/Ctrl+T. A draft session tab opens.
  2. In the draft's composer footer, untick the Task context chip. It's on by default, and its tooltip reads Gives the agent the task’s prompt and notes. Later changes reach it with your next message.
  3. Type your prompt and press Start.

The new session doesn't receive the Task's prompt or notes. It still gets the Task's worktrees, folders, files, and links, because it still needs to know where to work. The switch belongs to that draft, so turning it off doesn't change your other sessions. See The Task View.

Notes are how you give standing instructions

Attach a Note for anything every agent on the Task should follow: a style rule, a constraint, a decision you don't want re-litigated. Write it once and every session gets it, including sessions you start later. Because a session sees a note's title first and opens the note when it's relevant, give each note a title that says what it governs. A note can be up to 1 MB; a longer one is refused with This note is too long. Notes can be up to 1 MB.

To rename a note, double-click its name, press F2 on the open note, or choose Rename note from its right-click menu. The name becomes a field: Enter keeps the new name and Escape puts the old one back. A note with no name reads Untitled note. The Task's Prompt keeps its name.

An open note has Copy note beside Edit, and Enter on the focused note starts editing it. An edit you left unsaved is kept as a draft: the note reads Unsaved draft, and Edit becomes Resume draft.

Detaching and deleting

Most of what you remove from a Task isn't destroyed. The menu says which you're doing: Detach for something that lives elsewhere, Delete for something only the Task holds.

Kepler asks separately in each case, because the answer differs:

Resource Dialog What removing it does
Folder Detach folder? The folder stays on disk — it's only removed from this task.
Linked file Detach file? The file stays on disk — it's only removed from this task.
Uploaded copy Delete file? Deletes the copy in the task folder.
Pull request or issue Detach pull request? / Detach issue? It's removed from this task.
Link Delete link? It's removed from this task.
Note Delete note? Deletes the note.

The Task's Prompt has no remove action.

Worktrees get more care, because deleting one can lose work.

The worktree's menu, in the rail or on the worktree strip, offers both, as separate entries:

  • Detach worktree… removes it from the Task and leaves it on disk. Its dialog says only The worktree stays on disk — it’s only removed from this task.
  • Delete worktree… removes it entirely. It opens Delete worktree?, which warns This can’t be undone. while confirming would delete something. The repository's main worktree has no Delete worktree….

The Delete worktree? dialog lists the worktree with what deleting it would cost, such as No uncommitted changes and Branch kept, under a verdict like Safe to delete. Tick Also delete branch to remove the branch along with the worktree. A worktree another Task still uses can't be deleted: the dialog says which Task keeps it, and offers Detach worktree in place of Delete worktree.

Neither is possible while a session is still running in the worktree; the menu tells you how many to archive first. If another program has the folder open, the delete fails with Kepler could not delete {path} because another program is using it. Close any editors, terminals or file explorers that have the folder open, then try again.

Below that, a live check shows what deleting would cost, read fresh from the worktree rather than written in advance. Tick Also delete branch to take the branch with it, and the check re-reads, because the branch is a separate risk. Each worktree carries short tags for what's at stake:

Tag What it means
{count} uncommitted files Changes that exist only in this working copy
{count} commits not on any remote Commits the branch holds that were never pushed. Kepler counts them before it deletes anything
Detached HEAD Commits that aren't on any branch
In open PR #{prNumber}, Merged in PR #{prNumber}, Merged into {base}, Pushed The branch's commits are safe somewhere else, so deleting it loses nothing
No uncommitted changes Nothing to lose

Kepler recognizes a branch that landed through a rebase merge or a squash merge as merged, so cleaning one up doesn't warn about commits that are already in its target.

A worktree that would lose work is kept, not deleted, unless you tick the extra box that names it and what it would lose: Also delete {branch}, losing {what}. Kepler refuses the delete outright unless you confirmed it there. Nothing can destroy uncommitted or unpushed work behind your back, no matter what requested the delete.

Renaming, archiving, and deleting a Task

From the Task actions (⋮) menu, on the task's row or in the task view's header:

Action What it does
Mark as read / Mark as unread Mark as read appears while any of the Task's sessions is unread, and marks them all read. Otherwise the menu offers Mark as unread
Rename task Tasks name themselves when they're created; rename when the name stops fitting. The name becomes an editable field in place; its Auto-name button suggests one from the Task's prompt and resources, into the field, for you to accept or edit; on the task page, double-clicking the Task's name does the same
Copy task link Copies a Kepler link to the Task. Cmd/Ctrl+C on a focused task row copies it too
Refresh Re-reads the Task's worktrees and its pull request and issue links. Shortly after a refresh, the providers can't be read again yet, so the row is locked and says when you can try again
Archive task Takes it out of the active list and keeps it as history. Its sessions and resources are kept, and nothing is destroyed unless you ask for it
Restore task On an archived Task, in place of Archive task. It goes straight back, unless another active Task now holds the same pull request; then Kepler asks first, because restoring archives that one
Delete task Removes the Task along with its sessions and resources

Kepler suggests archiving a finished Task. Once a Task reaches Done, with no unpushed work in its worktrees and no session still working or waiting on you, its header asks Ready to archive? with an Archive button. Dismiss it and that Task stops asking.

Archive and Delete ask the same three questions, in the same dialog, because they're the same act. Each box stays unavailable until the one above it is ticked, and Kepler remembers your choices for next time:

  • Also delete worktrees
  • Also delete branches
  • Also delete the branch on the remote

Until you tick them, Worktrees and branches stay on disk unless you tick the boxes below. Tick the first, and Kepler lists every worktree with the same tags a single worktree delete shows, and says what will happen to each:

  • A worktree that's safe goes.
  • A worktree that would lose work is kept, because deleting it would lose work, unless you tick the box that names it and confirms the loss.
  • A worktree Kepler can't touch is listed with why it's staying: another Task still uses it, it's the repository's main worktree, a session still runs in it, or it's already gone from disk.

Ticking Also delete branches re-reads the list, so a worktree that was safe a moment ago can turn into a warning. If the check is slow you can confirm anyway; any worktree that would lose work is kept.

With Also delete the branch on the remote ticked, Kepler deletes each branch's remote copy after the local branch goes. It leaves the remote branch alone when deleting it could lose something, and a Kept a branch on the remote notice gives the reason: the branch has an open pull request, it has commits that are not on the remote, the remote has commits this branch does not, or the remote branch changed after it was checked.

Restore task appears in the task view's header as soon as the Task is archived. From a row in the main list, it appears only once you've filtered Activity to Archived. See Arranging Your Work.

You can archive and restore individual sessions the same way, from the session's own menu in the rail.

What an agent can do with a Task's resources

Agents reach the Task through Kepler's own MCP (Model Context Protocol) server, kepler-workspace, so an agent can keep the Task's resource list honest instead of leaving it to you, and can find and coordinate with the rest of your work.

The workspace tools were renamed in Kepler 0.15. Each tool is now named for what it acts on, then what it does, such as task_get or worktree_create. If an agent's settings allow-list mcp__kepler-workspace__ tools by name, update them to the names below.

The tools name things by id, and each id carries its kind: task:, session:, repo:, worktree:, folder:, note:, file: and account:. A Kepler link to a Task or a session that you paste into the chat works as its id. Wherever a tool takes a repository, the agent can also name it by one of its Aliases, set on the repository's page in Settings → Repositories. See Settings.

Reading Tasks and notes

Tool What it does
task_get Read a Task: its stage, its worktrees and folders, notes, files, links, related Tasks and newest sessions. On the agent's own Task, later calls return only what changed, and refresh re-reads pull request and issue state, the disk and Git first
task_list Find Tasks by name, prompt, note text or file name, filtered by stage, hold, repository, or whether they belong to the agent's own work. Archived Tasks only on request
task_note_read Read one note's full text. Notes otherwise arrive as references (title and size)

Finding sessions, agents and places

Tool What it does
session_list Find agent sessions by name, Task, agent or place, with each one's state and what it's waiting on: a permission, an answer, or an approval. It can keep only the sessions that need you
session_read Read another session's conversation, at the level of detail the agent asks for, by its id or a link from Copy session link. It returns the newest messages, or only the last reply with limit:1, and reports what a running session waits on, including a plan it's holding for your review
agent_list List the agents, accounts, models and effort values a new session can start with, and the modes each agent supports
repo_list List the repositories and folders work can happen in, marking the ones this Task uses. It finds one by name, path or alias

Notes, links and files

Tool What it does
task_note_write Create a note, or edit one: replace the whole note, replace the section under one heading, or add to the end. A replace carries the version the agent last read, so a note changed underneath it is refused rather than overwritten
task_note_delete Delete a note. The Task's Prompt can't be deleted
task_link_update Attach or detach issue, pull request and other links. Kepler classifies each URL and fetches its title and status when it can, and an agent that starts a server you're meant to open (a dev server, Storybook, a preview) attaches its local URL so it shows on the Task, and detaches it when it stops the server for good
task_file_attach Attach files by path, linked rather than copied. A file the agent wrote in a temporary folder can be attached as a copy saved with the Task instead, up to 10 MiB
task_file_detach Detach files. A linked file stays on disk; an uploaded copy is deleted

Worktrees

Tool What it does
worktree_create Create a worktree on a new branch and bind it to the Task, forked from the repository's remote default branch or from a base the agent names
worktree_attach Bind a worktree or folder that already exists on disk to the Task, changing no files
worktree_detach Remove a binding without touching the worktree, the branch, the files, or the sessions running there. Neither the Task's own folder nor the place the agent itself runs in can be detached
worktree_update Record a new merge target after the agent rebased a stacked branch or moved it onto another branch. A merge target you saved yourself takes precedence
worktree_delete Delete a worktree from disk. Its branch goes too when Kepler finds it merged; an unmerged branch is kept unless the agent asks to delete it

Where AI Sync and AI Compose are turned on, the sync_* and compose_* tools also let an agent update a branch from its base with a rebase or merge, and reorganize its commits. See Review Changes.

Progress and lifecycle

Tool What it does
task_create Create a separate Task beside this one, with its own prompt, places, notes, links and files, for work that deserves tracking in its own right. It's linked back to this one (as a sub-task, parent, follow-up, or just related), and nothing starts on it
task_update Set the stage your board shows (Exploration, In Development, In Review, Done), put the Task on hold or lift the hold, rename it, or restore it from the archive, or hand the stage back to Kepler's own reading of Git and pull requests. If it replaces a stage or a hold you set, it tells you
task_archive Archive a Task, which alone stops nothing and deletes nothing. With cleanup, it also deletes the Task's worktrees and their merged branches

Starting and coordinating work

Tool What it does
session_start Start an agent session on a Task and send it a first message, in a place the agent names or in a new worktree, as the starting session's child: a delegate, a reviewer, or a handoff. It runs on the same agent, account, model, mode and effort unless the agent picks others from agent_list, and it can start without the Task's context, as a reviewer would
session_send Send another session a prompt to act on, or a message for its inbox. A session reports back to the one that started it with a status (progress, done, blocked or failed), and can also stop the other session's running turn
session_update Rename a session. Sessions that run in the terminal or outside Kepler can't be renamed
session_archive Archive a session: its history stays and its agent stops. A session can't archive itself
inbox_read Read this session's inbox, or wait up to 60 seconds for reports from the sessions it started
approval_list List every request waiting on your approval. It can't approve or deny anything
theme_get / theme_save Read Kepler's theme format, then check a theme file and install it into the themes folder

What runs at once, and what asks you

Every workspace tool is pre-approved, in Chat and in Terminal alike, so none of them shows the agent's own permission prompt. Instead, Kepler decides for itself which requests can run at once.

An agent works freely within its own work: its own Task, and the Tasks and sessions connected to it because the agent created them or started sessions on them. Reads run at once anywhere, and so do creating a Task, a note or a worktree. Changes inside the agent's own work run at once too, except the ones that can lose something.

The following wait for your approval:

  • Sending a prompt to any session other than one the agent started itself, or a message to a session outside its own work
  • Stopping or renaming a session outside its own work, or archiving any session other than one it started
  • Changing a Task outside its own work: its stage, hold, name, links or notes, or detaching its files
  • Restoring a Task when another active Task holds the same pull request
  • Deleting any note
  • Detaching an uploaded file, because its saved copy is deleted
  • Archiving a Task outside its own work or with other live sessions, and any archive that also deletes worktrees
  • Deleting a worktree that has something to lose (uncommitted changes, unpushed commits on a branch being deleted, commits on a detached HEAD, a status Kepler can't read), or that another Task is bound to
  • Starting a session in a more permissive mode than the agent's own, or on a Task outside its own work
  • Overwriting a theme file where there's no Trash to keep the old one
  • The AI Sync and Compose tools that change a repository or rewrite its history

Modes are compared on three levels, plan, edit and auto, which Kepler maps onto each agent's own permission modes. A session an agent starts can't run above the mode of the session that started it unless you approve; the request says More permissive than the asking session, which is in {mode} mode.

The request shows up as an approval card above the asking session's composer, with Approve and Deny. Approve's menu can approve every request of that kind from the session, or always. A rule you make with Always approve is listed under Settings → Agents → Approvals, where Remove takes it away. Always approve isn't offered for actions that can lose work. See Agent Sessions.

The agent doesn't wait on the card. It's told the request is awaiting your approval and carries on, and the outcome arrives in its inbox. Before an approved request runs, Kepler checks again: if what it would act on changed in the meantime, such as a worktree that picked up new commits, nothing runs.

Deleting a worktree through worktree_delete carries the same protection your own delete does. It refuses while a live session is running in the worktree, including the agent's own. A worktree it deletes stays on the Task, marked gone, until it's detached.

Reports between sessions

Each session has an inbox for what other agents send it: reports and messages, changes in the state of a session it started, and the outcomes of its approval requests. When an item arrives for an idle session, Kepler wakes the session to read it; a busy session reads it when its turn ends. Sessions running in Terminal are woken the same way. A session whose agent can't reach Kepler's workspace tools isn't sent state changes.

A session with unread reports carries a badge with their count on its tab and rail row. Above its composer, Have it read them asks the agent to read them now.

Agents that keep waking each other can loop, so after 20 turns started by agents rather than by you, reports stop waking the session. The session reads Reports from other agents are paused, with Resume reports to turn wakes back on. Sending the session a message of your own resumes them too.

feature: Taskscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true7777 words · ~35 min1 TODO

Open a Task to see its resources, agent sessions, changes, and files side by side. Learn the header, the rail, columns and free-form tabs, the file viewer, and how to start sessions from a draft tab and open terminals.

Last updated: October 2026

Opening a Task gives you its own screen, the task page, with everything attached to it on the left and the sessions, working copies, files, and details you're looking at on the right.

Get here by double-clicking a row in the Kepler interface, or by clicking Open task page in the side panel. For what a Task is and what can be attached to one, see Tasks and Resources.

The header

From left to right, the header carries:

  • Show/Hide context panel, which puts the rail away and brings it back. See The rail.
  • Back to dashboard, a back chevron, returns you to the list exactly as you left it: same segment, grouping, filters, and selection.
  • The task switcher, the Task's name as a chip, jumps to another Task without going back first. It searches as you type, and each row carries the same session status dots a row does. Right-click the name for the same task actions the ⋮ menu holds, or double-click it to rename the Task.
  • ⋮ Task actions. See below.
  • The Progress control, showing where the work stands: Exploration, In Development, In Review, On hold, or Done. Click it to set the stage yourself or hand it back to Automatic. An archived Task reads Archived here instead. See Arranging your work.
  • Terminal, at the right edge, which shows and hides the terminal drawer and counts the terminals running in it.

When a Task looks finished, a Ready to archive? prompt joins the header with Archive beside it, until you archive the Task or dismiss it.

⋮ Task actions holds the following:

  • The Task's Progress submenu, with Reopen leading on a finished Task.
  • Mark as read while any of the Task's sessions is unread, which marks them all read. Otherwise the menu offers Mark as unread.
  • Rename task.
  • Copy task link, which copies a Kepler link that opens this Task.
  • Copy task prompt and Add resource….
  • Refresh, which reads the Task's worktrees and its pull requests and issues again. While the provider is cooling down it's locked, and its tooltip says when to try again.
  • Shut down all agents, while any are running.
  • Archive task and Delete task. On an already-archived Task, Restore task replaces Archive task.

Rename task, F2 anywhere on the page outside a text field, or a double-click on the task switcher turns the name into an editable field in place. The field carries Auto-name (Suggest a name from the task's prompt and resources), which fills the field with a suggestion for you to accept or edit. It never renames on its own: Enter or the check mark saves, Escape or the × cancels.

On a phone

At phone width, such as a phone browser connected through Remote Access, the task page folds its header into one bar:

  • Back on the left. It carries a dot when another task needs you.
  • The Task's name as a chip, which opens the task switcher. Swipe sideways on the name to go back to the previous task.
  • One segment each for Sessions, Changes, Terminal, and Resources. Resources drops first when the name would be cut off.

Tapping a segment goes to what's waiting first, such as a blocked question, or else to the last session you had open. Each segment has a picker listing that group's rows, with a way to archive or close each one and a way to add another: New session, New terminal, or Add resource. The terminal takes the whole column rather than a drawer.

Below the bar, a details row holds the Progress control, Refresh, and a fold chevron. Unfolded, it shows the checkout and its pull request and issue chips. It folds away while Terminal or Changes is on screen.

Pull down on the bar, or long-press a segment, to open Everything in this task. This sheet lists every session, checkout, terminal, and resource under the rail's headings, each with its own actions menu. It also offers New session, Add resource, New terminal, the archived sessions, and the sessions started outside Kepler. A long press opens it on that segment's group only.

The task switcher on a phone is a sheet listing every task in the Dashboard's inbox order, with the current Task's actions menu in its header.

The rail

The left rail lists everything attached to the Task, grouped by kind:

Sessions · Changes · Merged · Terminals · Folders · Files · Pull requests · Issues · Links · Notes · Related tasks

The worktree group is called Changes. Its rows carry each checkout's real state and open its diff review, so the header names what you go there for rather than the git object behind it.

Merged collects the worktrees whose branch has landed, so they stop crowding the work still in progress. Each row reads Merged via PR {pr} or Merged into {target} in place of its change chips. The group starts folded while the Task still has an active worktree. Delete all… on its header opens a dialog for deleting the merged worktrees in one go.

Related tasks lists the Tasks linked to this one, each with its stage, its relation, and its session dots: Parent, Sub-task, Follows up on, Follow-up, Related, or Created from. Point at a row for its relation and stage, plus the reason for the link, and click it to go to that Task.

Sessions always stays, even on a Task that has none, so you always have a way to start the first one. Every other group appears only once it has something in it.

The Task's prompt is a row of its own. It leads the Notes group as Prompt, with its own icon. Open it to read or edit what the Task was started with; editing it changes the Task's prompt everywhere it shows. It can't be deleted.

Files holds both files linked from disk and copies uploaded to the Task. An uploaded copy reads Task folder under its name where a linked file shows its path.

The rail can be put away. Hide context panel collapses it and hands the width to the content area; Show context panel brings it back. While it's hidden the control carries the task's own session status, so a session that needs you is still visible from a collapsed rail. Drag its edge to resize it.

  • Add resource sits at the bottom of the rail and opens the Add resources dialog.
  • The + on a group header adds straight into that group: Add issues, Add notes, and so on. On Changes it reads Add worktree, because that's what actually lands there. On Sessions the + is New session instead, which opens a draft session. See Starting a session.
  • A session you haven't started yet shows as a Draft row in Sessions. Right-click it for Discard draft.
  • Every session that isn't live (archived ones, and ones that ended) collects under a {count} archived fold beneath the live ones. It starts collapsed. Clicking an archived session brings it back and opens it in one gesture; Restore in its menu does the same.

What a row shows

Both of a row's lines truncate at rail width. Every row has a hover tip carrying its full name, plus the facts the row's lines had no room for:

  • Path
  • Repository
  • Base
  • Agent
  • Account
  • Status
  • Flags such as Repository's main worktree or Gone from disk

Some kinds of row show more:

  • A pull request or issue row shows the same live card the Dashboard does, ending with Status as of {time}. A pull request row leads its subtitle with what needs attention, such as CI failed, and its card adds the checks and whether it can merge.
  • A file row's card adds Size, Modified, and Type, plus when it was added. An image file shows its own thumbnail as the row's icon.

  • A terminal row's tip adds Started and, once it has finished, Ended.

  • A Changes row's subtitle is a run of glyph-and-number chips reading the checkout's real state: uncommitted files, commits on the branch, behind and ahead of the upstream, behind the merge target, with conflicts and an unpublished branch drawn as glyphs alone. A checkout with nothing to report reads Up to date; one that has been deleted says so. Its tooltip is a two-column table of the same facts (the branch, the repository, the path, the working tree, the commits, the upstream and the merge target), with the numbers aligned so they never wrap.

  • A session row leads with its state, and names which Account it runs under once a harness has more than one configured: the provider logo plus an ordinal, on the row, its tab, and its collapsed strip alike.

The end of the subtitle line carries the row's controls, always visible rather than revealed on hover:

Row Controls
Folder Open in…
Worktree (on disk) Open in… and the run-a-command control. The shell button stays out: the row's menu and Cmd/Ctrl+J both offer it, and the line has no room for a third control
Session Show or hide the Agent Graph for that session alone, and Archive

The run-a-command control shows the newest command running in that worktree, with Stop, and falls back to your default command once nothing is running.

Every rail row draws a selected fill when it's the one on screen, and the column you last pressed, focused, or opened from the rail carries an inset ring. The wide layout never moves focus on its own, so something has to say where it is.

The row menu

Right-click any row. Every row opens with Open, followed by Open to the side with tabs off or Open beside with tabs on (see Columns or tabs), when there's somewhere for it to go. A worktree row adds Open changes.

On Also holds
A worktree that's on disk Open changes, your default editor, Open in, and Open pull request when one is linked. A Branch submenu, then New session here, New terminal here, Run command here, Copy branch name, Copy worktree path, Copy remote URL, Show in Finder (macOS), Show in Explorer (Windows) or Show in file manager (Linux), and Refresh
A folder New session here, New terminal here, your default editor, Open in, Copy path
A file Open in default app, Show in Finder (macOS), Show in Explorer (Windows) or Show in file manager (Linux), Save as…, Copy path
A note Rename note, which opens the note with its name ready to edit, and Copy note
A session Rename session, New session from this one, Open source session on a session started from another, Copy session link, Continue as, Continue in Terminal or Continue in Chat, Restart agent, Shut down agent, Show Agent Graph, and Use another copy… when Kepler has more than one saved copy of the conversation
A session started outside Kepler Open preview, Continue in this task, Create task, Copy session ID, Copy working directory path, and a way to reveal the working directory in your file manager
A pull request or issue Open, which opens it in Kepler, then Open on {provider}, Copy link, Copy title, Copy number, Copy branch name on a pull request, Refresh, and Go to worktree on a pull request whose branch is checked out
A plain link Open in browser and the copy actions
A terminal that's closing Force close terminal

The worktree's Branch submenu gathers what changes the branch itself: Rename branch, the upstream (Set upstream, Change upstream, Unset upstream, or Publish as {remote}/{branch}…), Change merge target…, Rebase onto {target}, Merge {target} into this branch, and the sync control. A stopped rebase or merge adds rows to continue or abort it.

Run command here, and the Run a command control on the row and the worktree's own strip, lists the repository's commands (configured on the repository's own page in Settings → Repositories) and runs the one you pick in that worktree, revealing its terminal in the drawer without taking your focus. Each command row leads with its state: a play glyph, a spinner while it warms up, or Stop while it runs, and picking a running command stops it rather than starting a second copy. A repository with none reads No commands yet, and the menu ends with Create command… so you can add one from where you noticed you wanted it, plus Manage commands…, which deep-links to that repository's own settings. When you leave that settings page, whether you saved or not, Kepler takes you back to the Task.

Open in works the same way, with your default editor hoisted above the list rather than buried in it. On a worktree the list ends with Open on {provider}, which opens the branch on its git host, or the repository's home page when the branch has no upstream. To set the editor Kepler uses everywhere, see Open files and folders in under Settings → General.

Picking an editor or a command doesn't make it your default. Each row carries its own set/unset toggle at its trailing edge, reachable from the keyboard, so choosing something once and pinning it are two separate decisions.

Rename branch opens a dialog with the branch's own name field and Also rename remote branch, which renames the branch on its remote too rather than just locally. Change merge target… opens a picker of remote branches; see Review Changes.

Rename session names a Chat session yourself; automatic titling never overwrites a name you chose.

Copy session link copies a Kepler link that opens the session. When the window is connected to a remote machine, the link names that machine, so it opens there. With the row focused, Cmd/Ctrl+C copies the same link. See Agent Sessions.

Continue as moves the conversation to another signed-in account of the same agent, restarting the agent with the conversation as that account. Continue in Terminal and Continue in Chat move it between the two ways a session runs, keeping the conversation. While the session is mid-turn, these entries are locked: This session is mid-turn. Wait for it to finish, or stop it first. See Moving a session to another account and How a session runs.

Use another copy… opens a dialog listing each saved copy of the conversation, when it was updated and how many messages it holds, with In use on the current one. Pick the one to continue when you resume. See Agent Sessions.

Option/Alt-click a resource row to open it in your browser instead of in Kepler.

The last entry is how the row leaves the Task, and it differs by kind: Detach worktree…, Detach folder…, Detach file…, Detach pull request… or Detach issue… for something that stays on disk or at its provider, Delete file…, Delete note… or Delete link… for something Kepler holds itself, Archive session for a session, Close terminal for a terminal. A worktree also ends with Delete worktree…, except the repository's main worktree. Detach worktree…, Delete worktree… and Detach folder… are locked while a session of this Task still runs there, with the reason in their tooltip, such as To detach, archive the session in this worktree.

Columns or tabs

The content area takes one of two shapes, set by Show tabs on the task page in Settings → Tasks → Task page:

Setting What the content area does
Off (the default) Fixed columns, one per kind of thing, each showing one thing at a time
On Free-form tabs you can pin, drag between groups, and arrange side by side

Either way, terminals live in the drawer beneath the content area, never in a column or a tab, so opening a shell never evicts the changes or the conversation you were reading. And either way, closing something is a view change: it detaches nothing, and it doesn't stop a session. The rail still has it all.

The columns

With tabs off, the content area is fixed slots, not free-form panes. A rail row's kind decides which slot it opens into, so a session never lands next to a note and you never have to remember where you put something.

Column What opens here
Sessions Agent conversations, whether they run in Chat or in a terminal, and draft sessions you haven't started yet
Changes A worktree: a strip of verbs over its changed files. See Review Changes
Resources Folders, files, pull requests, issues, links, notes

Every boundary is a draggable sash, and the size you drag it to persists. The three content columns share whatever the rail leaves: equally until you drag one, and in the ratio you set from then on. Kepler keeps the ratios as proportions, not pixels, so they survive a window resize and a column opening or closing.

A rail row is a toggle. Click it to open it in its slot; click the row that's already on screen to put it away again. A slot with nothing open renders no column at all, and the others take its space.

The page opens on a conversation and on the checkout that conversation is working in, inferred from the session or terminal it auto-opened, so Changes isn't an empty column you have to go fill on every visit.

Close a column with the X on its strip, labeled Close panel.

Split a column to see two things of the same kind. Cmd-click a rail row (Ctrl-click on Windows and Linux), or pick Open to the side in its menu, and it opens beside what that column already shows. A plain click while a column is split replaces only the focused pane and leaves the others alone.

Shift-click opens a range. This is how you watch several agents at once: shift-click from your last plain click through another session and the whole run opens side by side in the Sessions column, so you can read them in parallel and answer whichever needs you. The anchor is your last plain click and a shift-click doesn't move it, the way it works in Finder or VS Code. With no prior click in that group, the range starts from the row already on screen.

A session's control on its strip is named for what it does: Archive session on one of Kepler's own sessions stops the agent, saves the conversation, and files it under the rail's archived fold. An external session or a preview gets Close tab, which only hides it.

Tabs and groups

With tabs on, anything you open from the rail (sessions, worktrees, files, and every other resource) becomes a tab, and tabs live in groups you arrange freely, the way editor tabs work in VS Code.

  • Drag a tab to reorder it, onto another group to move it there, or to a group's edge to split off a new group there. Drag the sash between groups to resize them.
  • A single click opens a preview. A resource opened with one click shows in an italic preview tab that the next single click replaces, so browsing the rail doesn't pile up tabs. Double-click a row, or start editing in the tab, to keep it open. A session always opens as a tab you keep. Turn Preview tabs off in Settings → Tasks → Task page to have every click keep its tab.
  • Cmd-click a rail row (Ctrl-click on Windows and Linux), or pick Open beside in its menu, to open it in a new group beside the one you're reading.
  • Shift-click opens a range of rows, each as its own tab.
  • Clicking the row of something already open just brings its tab forward. Close a tab from the tab itself.

A tab's Tab actions (⋮) button and its right-click menu hold everything the thing's rail row offers, plus Rename on a file or folder tab. They lead with the tab's own verbs: Keep open on a preview, Pin tab or Unpin tab, Close tab, Close others, Close to the right, Close all, and Close group when there's more than one group. A pinned tab shrinks to its icon at the front of its group and has no close control; unpin it to close it.

A draft session's tab reads (draft) after its name when the tab strip has room; in a crowded strip every tab drops its extra text, and a draft shows its name alone. With tabs off, right-click a draft tab for Discard draft, plus Move left and Move right where the tab can move. With tabs on, its menu holds the tab's own verbs instead: Keep open while it's a preview, Pin tab or Unpin tab, and Close tab, which is a draft's only close. Closing a draft that has text in it asks Discard new session? first, because Your draft prompt and attachments will be removed.

A session tab swaps its close control for the session's status dot while the session has something unread or needs you, so a group's tabs say which conversation wants attention.

Closing a tab with its × (Close tab), a middle-click, Cmd/Ctrl+W (or Ctrl+F4 on Windows and Linux), or Delete on a focused tab only hides it. Delete goes through the tab's own close, so a draft with text in it still asks first. Backspace doesn't close a tab, except on a Mac keyboard, whose delete key sends Backspace. A session keeps running and stays on the rail; to archive one, use Archive on its rail row. Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+T reopens the last tab you closed.

When every tab is closed, the content area offers New session and the external-session importer, so a Task with nothing open is never a dead end. With tabs on, those two controls also sit in the header, beside Terminal.

The importer lists the agent conversations Kepler found outside it, under Live now and Past sessions. Each row shows the agent's logo, a status dot, the folder it ran in, and when it was last active; point at one for its session card. Show past sessions and Show sessions from every folder widen the list. Sessions older than an agent's listing window are left out, and the picker names the window for each agent: {adapter} sessions older than {days} days aren't listed.

Keyboard

Keys What they do
Ctrl+Tab / Ctrl+Shift+Tab Switch between the Task's sessions, most recently used first
F6 / Shift+F6 Move focus to the next or previous region of the page
Cmd/Ctrl+1, 2, 3 Focus the Sessions, Changes, or Resources column (tabs off)
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+[ and Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+] Previous and next tab. Also Ctrl+PageUp and Ctrl+PageDown on Windows and Linux
Alt+1 to Alt+9 Jump to a tab in the focused group (tabs on)
Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+[ and Cmd/Ctrl+Alt+] Move the focused tab left or right. Also Ctrl+Shift+PageUp and Ctrl+Shift+PageDown on Windows and Linux
Cmd/Ctrl+W Close the focused tab or panel. Also Ctrl+F4 on Windows and Linux
Delete Close the focused tab, the way its × does
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+T Reopen the last closed tab (tabs on)
Cmd/Ctrl+T New session, which opens a draft tab
Cmd/Ctrl+J Show or hide the terminal drawer
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+J Maximize the terminal drawer, opening it first if it's hidden
Cmd/Ctrl+O / Cmd/Ctrl+P Open File… / Go to File…. See Opening a file
Cmd/Ctrl+F Find in the focused file or diff. See Finding text in a file
F2 Rename the Task, or the note whose pane or tab has focus
Cmd/Ctrl+C Copy what the focused row names, such as a session's link or a file's path
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+W Close the window
Tab to a divider, then the arrow keys Move the divider between panes, columns, or tab groups 16 pixels per press, or 64 with Shift. A screen reader announces each divider as Resize {first} and {second} with its size

With tabs off, the column strips' Tab actions menus also hold Move left and Move right.

Starting a session

New session opens a draft session in its own tab, marked (draft) when the tab strip has room. The draft holds the prompt composer, so you choose where the agent works and how it runs before anything starts. To open a draft, click the + on the rail's Sessions group, or press Cmd/Ctrl+T.

To start a session from a draft, follow these steps:

  1. Click + on Sessions, or press Cmd/Ctrl+T. A draft tab opens, and a Draft row joins the rail.
  2. Choose where the agent works from the place chip at the bottom of the composer. The chip names the place, then its branch, then whether the session gets a new worktree.
  3. Open Agent settings to choose the Agent and how to Start in, along with the agent's own options, such as Permission mode.
  4. Type your prompt.
  5. Press Start.

The place chip has three segments. The first opens a menu of places, with a Search places field on a long list and the following groups:

Group What it lists
Attached to this task The Task's worktrees, by branch, and its folders, led by All worktrees and folders. That row runs the session in the task folder with access to every worktree and folder, and reads Task folder while nothing is attached
Repositories The repositories the Task has worktrees of
Add a repository or folder A picker over all your repositories and folders. A folder you pick joins the Task right away; a repository joins it when the session starts

The second segment picks the branch the session works on. The third is a checkbox: tick it to start on a new branch in a new worktree of the session's own, or leave it clear to work in the place as it is.

A worktree that's gone from disk reads Not on disk. Start recreates it., and Kepler asks Recreate the worktree and start the session? before the session starts. A Task with no place to run at all says Add a worktree or folder to start a session, with Add worktree or folder.

Task context is a checkbox chip in the composer's footer, beside the place chip. It's on by default and Gives the agent the task’s prompt and notes. Later changes reach it with your next message. Its tooltip reads Leave out the task context while it's on and Include the task context while it's off. Turn it off for a session that forms its own view instead.

Which agent a draft starts with is a setting. New sessions start with, in Settings → Tasks → New tasks, is Last used agent by default (The agent, model and mode you last picked), or Default agent to always start with the default agent. See Settings.

If no agents are connected, Agent settings reads No agents available, and its menu offers Connect an AI agent: an Install row per agent, See all {count} agents in Settings, and Check again for one you installed yourself. See Agent Integrations. An agent that's signed out reads Sign in to {agent} to start a session. When an agent doesn't share its options, the menu says You can still start a session. It uses {agent}'s own model and mode.

Start a draft in a specific checkout. Right-click a worktree or folder row and pick New session here. A draft opens with that checkout already chosen as its place, ready for your prompt.

When a session can't start. If the agent fails after launch, the task page shows {agent} was unable to start with the agent's message. Press Retry to launch it again, or Open agent settings to fix the problem. Dismiss hides the notice in that window.

verifyDraft tab, target chip, Agent settings and start-failure labels verified against kepler v0.13.0.

Start from an existing session. New session from this one, on a session's row or tab menu, picks an agent and starts a session in the same working directory that can read the source conversation. New session from here, on a message in a conversation, does the same but cuts the source off at that message. The new session shows a Continues from divider naming its source; Open source session in its menu takes you back.

To fire a preconfigured prompt instead of typing one, open the chevron beside the session composer's Send button and pick an Action. Anything you typed rides along as a refinement. See Actions.

Opening a file

Any file can open on the task page, whether or not it's attached to the Task. It opens as a tab with tabs on, or in the Resources column with tabs off, in Kepler's file viewer. To see a worktree file alongside its changes instead, pick Open in Changes in its tab's menu, which opens it in the Changes overlay on File view. For what happens away from the task page, see Outside the task page.

How to open one What you get
File › Open File… (Cmd/Ctrl+O) A file picker for the machine the window is connected to
File › Go to File… (Cmd/Ctrl+P) Finds a file by name across This task or All tasks, opens a full path you type, and lists Recent files before you type anything. A path that names a folder, or ends in a separator, offers Open and opens the folder
A file row in the rail's Files group That file
A file path an agent writes in the conversation The file, at the line the agent named. A click or Cmd/Ctrl+click opens it in Kepler, and Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+click opens it in your default editor.
A file path in a terminal The file, at its line. See File paths are links

Every file link follows one rule. A click or a Cmd/Ctrl+click opens the file in Kepler, and a middle-click does the same. Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+click opens it in your default editor at its line, or in your default app when that's your pinned target or no editor is installed. If no editor takes it, the file opens in Kepler instead.

A path that's already one of the Task's files opens that file's own tab rather than a second copy, however the path was spelled. A file that isn't attached yet offers Add to task, which attaches it and turns the tab into its resource tab in place.

What the viewer shows

  • Code and text, highlighted, scrolled to and marking the line you opened it at. Wrap lines toggles soft wrapping.
  • Markdown, rendered, with a Preview / Source toggle. A Markdown file opened at a line, such as from a path an agent wrote, opens in Source so you see the line, and a toggle you make then applies to that open only.
  • Images and SVG, fitted to the pane, with Fit and 100%.
  • Anything else gets a No preview for this file card with the ways out the window allows: Open in default app, Show in Finder (macOS), Show in Explorer (Windows) or Show in file manager (Linux), and Copy path.

An open file follows the disk. When it changes, the viewer updates in place without moving you, and says Updated just now, or Updated just now by the session that changed it when Kepler saw the edit. Show changes shows what changed since the file was first shown; Back to file returns. A deleted file keeps showing the last version Kepler read, and says so.

A very large file shows its start first, labeled Showing the first {shown} of {total}, with Load the rest.

Open in on the viewer's header is an icon button that opens the file in your pinned editor, or else the one you used last, with its chevron listing the rest. Your computer's default app is one of the targets, listed as Default app, and like any editor it can be pinned as your default with Set as default.

Finding text in a file

Every code view, the file viewer and the diffs in the Changes overlay alike, has its own find bar. Press Cmd/Ctrl+F in the view to open it at the top right. It counts matches as {current} of {total}, or No results, and offers the following:

Control Keys
Next and previous match F3 and Shift+F3. Also Cmd+G and Cmd+Shift+G on macOS
Match case Alt+C, or Cmd+Option+C on macOS
Match whole word Alt+W, or Cmd+Option+W on macOS
Use regular expression Alt+R, or Cmd+Option+R on macOS

Escape closes the bar. In a diff, the search runs across every file in the stack but skips the unchanged lines that are folded away. A file that has only partly loaded says it searched the first part, with Load the rest.

Selecting text works across the whole file, not only the lines on screen. Drag or select all and copy, and Kepler loads the rest of the file if it needs to. Double-click selects a word.

Outside the task page

A file opened anywhere else, such as from the Dashboard or a conversation link outside a Task, opens in an overlay rather than a tab:

  • A file or folder inside a worktree opens in the Changes overlay on File view. A changed file lands on Working changes; anything else lands on the All files tree. A folder opens the All files tree with its changed files selected.
  • Any other file opens in the file overlay, beside a file tree rooted at the attached folder or worktree that holds it, or else at the file's own folder.

In the file overlay:

  • Show file tree and Hide file tree toggle the tree, and the tree's edge resizes it. On a narrow window the toggle is locked: Widen the window to show the file tree.
  • Filter files narrows the tree. Right-click a tree row for the ways to open it and copy its path.
  • The breadcrumb's folders each list their folders and files, plus Open {folder} in file manager. As room runs out, folders fold into More folders.
  • Opening a folder shows its tree with Pick a file in the viewer.
  • Add to task is the last item in More file actions.
  • Drag either edge of the overlay to widen it, or use Maximize.

The terminal drawer

Terminals live in a drawer beneath the content area, spanning all of it, sized by a sash of its own. The Terminal control at the right edge of the header shows and hides it, and counts what's running.

How to open one What you get
Cmd/Ctrl+J, or the header's Terminal Shows and hides the drawer
New terminal here, on a worktree or folder row A shell in that checkout
Run command here, on a worktree row The command you pick, running in that checkout, revealed without taking your focus
The strip's + A shell. With several checkouts it becomes New terminal in…, listing them by branch

Every terminal also has a row in the rail's Terminals group. Clicking it brings the drawer up on that terminal; clicking the row already showing puts the drawer away. Drag the drawer's tabs to reorder them.

Maximize terminal pane, or Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+J, takes the whole height; Restore terminal pane brings it back.

On a phone, a terminal takes the whole column, with a row of the keys a phone keyboard lacks beneath it, and you select text with a long press. See Remote Access.

When a terminal reconnects

A terminal reattaches to its running shell rather than starting a new one:

  • While it loads it reads Loading terminal…. When the connection drops, nothing shows for the first second. After that, a Reconnecting to the terminal… strip leaves the last screen readable and scrollable, and only after five seconds does it cover the terminal. If it can't reconnect, Unable to restore this terminal says The terminal is still running. Retry to load its output again., with Retry.
  • What you type while it reconnects is held and sent once the terminal is back. If the program printed something in the meantime, Kepler drops it instead and says The terminal changed while reconnecting: Nothing you typed was sent. Type it again. Typing that couldn't reach the terminal at all reads Unable to send what you typed.
  • One view types at a time. A terminal another view is driving, in another window or on a Remote Access device, reads Another view is controlling this terminal. Take control lets you type here and sizes the terminal to this view.
  • A terminal printing faster than the connection can show reads Showing the latest output: This terminal is printing faster than this connection can show, so older lines are skipped.
  • Input Kepler can't confirm is never resent. See Agent Sessions.

Reading a tab

Each tab carries one glyph that says both what the tab is and how it ended, so a clean finish and a crash don't look alike:

Kind What it is
Shell A plain terminal you opened
Agent A session running an agent in Terminal mode. See Agent Sessions
Command run One of the repository's commands

A shell tab also shows what its program reports, when the program reports a status: Running, Needs you, Done or Failed. Hover the tab to see the state and, from a minute on, how long it's been in it, plus its message, its progress as a bar reading {percent}% done, and any sub-tasks with their own ages. A program that's blocked or failed without saying why gets a next step, such as Approve it in the terminal. Reported by {app} shows only when the app isn't already the tab's name. An agent tab keeps its own glyph, because its session owns its status.

A shell tab is named after what runs in it. That's the app, when the program reports its status, or else the job in front, such as pnpm dev. On Windows, it can be the window title the program set. Back at the prompt, the tab returns to its own label, such as Terminal 2. While the name differs from the label, hovering the tab shows the label and the job's command line or window title. Command runs and agent tabs keep their names.

When a shell's program needs you or finishes, Kepler sends a notification that names the terminal and its Task, such as Approve it in {terminal} or Finished in {terminal}. Kepler stays quiet while that terminal is on screen in the focused window. These notifications follow the same notification settings as agent sessions, in Settings → Notifications.

A finished command run shows a result bar rather than leaving you to read the scrollback: Exited with code {code}, Exited by signal {signal}, or Stopped, with Run again and Close beside it. A still-running one offers Stop.

File paths are links

File paths printed in a terminal are links, including relative paths, paths with a line suffix such as src/app.ts:42, and paths wrapped across lines. Kepler also spots a path an agent mentions as @src/app.ts, and one glued to a flag, such as --config=/etc/app.json. Cmd-click one (Ctrl-click on Windows and Linux) to open the file on the task page at its line, and Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+click to open it in your default editor. The hover hint says Click to open, or Click to preview where the click opens a preview tab, and adds Click to open in editor when an editor can take the file.

Right-click a path for more: Open file, your default editor (opening at the line), Open in for the rest of your editors and your computer's Default app, Save as…, and Copy path. Any of those targets can be your default, with Set as default.

Kepler task and session links in a terminal are links too, and so are the task and session IDs Kepler recognizes. The hover hint says Click to open in Kepler; Cmd/Ctrl-click opens it, and right-click offers Open in Kepler and Copy link.

On a phone, long-press a path or link instead, then pick Open from the small menu over the selection, which also offers Copy, Paste, and Select all. See Remote Access.

Closing a busy terminal

Closing a terminal with something running in it confirms first, naming the job you actually launched:

Close terminal? Stops {command}, which is still running in this terminal.

Close terminal goes ahead. A terminal already closing offers only Force close.

Shell tabs survive a Kepler update and come back after a restart. With Keep agents and terminals running across restarts and updates on, in Settings → General, command runs do too, and each takes up its run again: still running with Stop, or finished with its result bar. A plain Quit ends them, the way quitting any terminal app does. Quitting Kepler counts busy terminals alongside busy sessions and asks before it takes them down.

What each resource pane shows

Resource Details
Worktree, on disk A strip of verbs (the branch, the sync control, Open in, Run a command) over its changed files, which lead into the Changes overlay. See Review Changes
Worktree, missing A metadata card instead: Current branch, Base branch, and the Recorded path, flagged not on disk or gone when it's been removed outside Kepler. A worktree that has gone stays bound to the Task, and Kepler offers to recreate it rather than quietly dropping it
Folder Path, plus an editable name and description
File The file itself, in the file viewer, with its description, where it came from, and its size on the viewer's toolbar. Rename is in File actions, and an uploaded copy says it's a copy in the task folder
Pull request / Issue Status, who it involves, the item's description and its labels. An issue adds its comments
Link Every plain link (one that isn't a pull request or issue) shares one Links pane, scrolled to the link you picked
Note The note itself, with Copy note and Edit. The Prompt note edits the Task's prompt

The worktree strip. The ⋮ beside the branch, Worktree actions for {branch}, and a right-click on the branch both open the worktree's full row menu. With more than one worktree, the branch name also switches between the Task's worktrees. Detach worktree… only detaches: The worktree stays on disk — it’s only removed from this task. Delete worktree… is its own entry, never offered on the repository's main worktree.

Folders and files. A name or description you edit saves as you type, and Done closes the fields.

Pull requests and issues. You can select and copy the description text. When Kepler can't read a description, the pane says where to read it, such as To read the full description, open it on {provider}. When the read from the provider fails, the pane says Unable to read this from {provider}, so its details may be missing., with a way to fix it.

A pull request's People section shows its author, its Assignees, and each reviewer with a badge for their review state, tallied as {reviewed} of {count} reviewed. Fold it to just the faces. An issue shows its Assignee.

An issue from GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab, GitLab Self-Managed, Jira, or Jira Server also shows its discussion under Comments · {count}: the latest comments, each with its author, its age, and its text. Show earlier comments pages back, and Refresh comments reads them again. Kepler shows the comments for you to read; it doesn't hand them to the agent. When it can't read them, the section says why, such as Sign in to read this issue’s comments. or Comments for {provider} issues aren’t available in Kepler yet.

Links. The Links pane lists every plain link on the Task with its title and URL, an Open in browser button, and Link actions. The link you clicked is scrolled into view and flashes.

Notes. A note has a name you rename in place: double-click it, press F2 with the note focused, or pick Rename note from its row menu. Enter saves the name and Escape puts it back. Clearing the name leaves an Untitled note. The Prompt note can't be renamed. Enter on a focused note opens it for editing. A note with unsaved edits says Unsaved draft, and Edit reads Resume draft. Double-clicking the note's text selects a word.

feature: Agent Graphcontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true2175 words · ~10 min
