Sessions you started in your own terminal appear too, marked (external), read-only. See Sessions started outside Kepler above.

When the agent asks you something

Kepler pauses and asks you directly when an agent needs a decision it can't make on its own. It does this in the following ways:

  • A permission request before a risky tool call.
  • A structured question when the agent wants your input mid-task.
  • A plan review when the agent proposes a plan before it starts.
  • An approval card when the agent asks Kepler itself to do something that needs your say, such as deleting a worktree.

Reports from other agents arrive in a session's inbox rather than asking you anything; see Reports from other agents.

Permission requests

A request pins to the top of the session: an amber shield, the tool being asked about as the heading, and the call's input beneath it. When the agent says why it's asking (for example, a Claude Code session in auto mode that still hits a safety check), that reason sits just above the buttons. A request to reach outside the agent's network sandbox says so in Kepler's own words, Reaches outside the network sandbox., with the agent's reason when it gives one. The buttons are Kepler's own wording rather than the agent's, so a request reads the same whichever agent raised it.

Button What it does
Allow Approves this one call
Allow for this session Approves this call, and auto-approves matching follow-ups for the rest of the session. The agent is told only that you allowed it once, so it writes no rule of its own
Allow always Hands the agent a permanent approval, which the agent stores in its own settings
Reject Refuses this call
Reject always Refuses it permanently

You get the tiers the agent actually offers. Allow for this session is Kepler's own middle tier and appears only next to an Allow. A request that offers nothing but a permanent approval, as Claude Code's plan-exit prompt does, has no one-shot decision to widen. Where an agent draws its own distinction between several permanent choices, its labels are kept instead of a single Allow always, and any Tool(...) pattern a rule would cover is spelled out on a RULE line beneath the buttons.

Kepler coalesces repeats. A command that fires many identical requests (an install reaching the network over and over) asks once, and your answer settles the whole group.

A request raised by a subagent that another subagent started surfaces on the outermost subagent card, under the path of subagent names that leads to it (Parent › Child). See Reading the conversation.

Answered requests stay in the transcript, showing the tier you picked: Allowed for this session for the middle one, otherwise the button's own label, falling back to Approved or Denied.

Requests that arrive through hooks

A session running in Terminal mode, and one Kepler detected outside itself, both ask through the agent's own hooks rather than over ACP. You still answer in Kepler either way, without switching to the CLI's own prompt to reply. The card is the agent's, though, so it carries scopes instead of Kepler's tiers: Deny, Allow once, and one Always allow per scope the hook suggests, narrowest first.

Scope Meaning
this session Until this session ends
this project (local) This project, your machine only
this project (shared) This project, committed for your team
all projects Everywhere

Kepler prints the rule each button would write above them. A rule with no command pattern is annotated (this tool), meaning it covers every call to that tool rather than one command.

Kepler never approves a hook request on your behalf. If it stops holding one without your answer (the turn ends, the conversation moves elsewhere, or Kepler quits), it hands the request back to the CLI, which asks in its own dialog.

A Codex question asked through its hooks shows in Kepler as a question you can answer, with its options and their descriptions. Cursor has no permission or notification hook, so a Cursor Terminal or outside session can't tell Kepler that it's waiting on you: it reads Running until it stops, and you answer in the terminal.

Multiple-choice and multi-select questions

Agents can ask you structured questions instead of guessing. The form renders in the conversation at the point the agent asked. In Chat, Claude Code, Codex, and Cursor questions all arrive in this form, and a Cursor question that takes several answers works as a multi-select.

Kind How it behaves
Multiple choice Radio-style. One option is the answer
Multi-select Checkbox-style, hinted Select all that apply. Every ticked option is sent

Both kinds work the same way otherwise:

  • Options show a label and, when the agent supplied one, a description.
  • Press 1–9 to pick an option by the number shown on it.
  • Other... opens a free-text field (Type your answer...), which becomes the answer on its own for a single-choice question, and joins the ticked options on a multi-select. For Codex, this field takes the place of its own None of the above option.
  • Several questions arrive as steps with a {current}/{total} counter and a tab strip; submit is enabled only once nothing is outstanding, and until then you see {count} questions still unanswered.
  • Chat about this declines the form so you can answer in your own words in the chat. It does not cancel the session.
  • If the agent set a deadline, the form counts down ({count} seconds remaining), and a form that was never answered is recorded as Left unanswered rather than left on screen as if it had been submitted.

A restored session shows the question and the answers you gave as a read-only record.

Reviewing a plan

When an agent in plan mode proposes a plan, Kepler opens it for review in a Plan panel beside the chat, with Preview and Edit tabs, and the transcript shows the plan with Open plan. Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, and OpenCode Chat sessions offer plan review. Cursor holds its plan request open while you review it, so the session waits with the reason Plan review.

To review the plan, use the following:

Control What it does
A comment on a passage Select text in the plan and say what should change (What should change?). Comments collect under Comments ({count})
Request changes ({count}) Sends your comments back so the agent revises the plan. Each revision is kept, so you can step back to an earlier one
Approve or Approve & implement Accepts the plan, and the agent leaves plan mode to carry it out. The chevron lists any other ways the agent offers to approve

While comments are pending, approving is blocked: Submit or delete your comments before approving. Auggie shows its plan as a task checklist instead, and Hide these tasks clears it.

Approval requests

Some things an agent asks Kepler itself to do, through Kepler's workspace tools, wait for your approval, for example:

  • Sending a prompt or a message to another session, or stopping, renaming, or archiving one.
  • Editing or deleting a task note, changing another task or its links, detaching files, or archiving a task.
  • Deleting a worktree.
  • Starting a session in a more permissive mode than the asking session's own.
  • Syncing a branch with its target, or rewriting its commits.
  • Overwriting a theme file.

The agent is told the request is waiting and can carry on; your answer reaches its inbox later. Meanwhile the session reads Approval needed, or Approval needed, destructive. The request is pinned above that session's composer as a card naming the action, such as Delete worktree {worktree} and its branch, with what's at stake (At risk: {loss}), Approve, and Deny. A card for an action that can lose work is amber; other cards are blue. Several requests page through with {index} of {count}.

The Approve chevron offers two wider answers:

  • Approve all {label} from this session approves this kind of request for the rest of the session.
  • Always approve {label} adds a standing rule. It isn't offered for an action that can lose work (Not offered for actions that can lose work.) or for starting a session.

An approved request shows Running while Kepler carries it out, after checking that it still applies. One that fails shows Not done: {summary}, with Dismiss. A waiting request expires when its session is archived.

To review your standing rules, open Settings → Agents → Approvals: Some actions your agents take wait for your approval. These rules approve them for you; anything not covered still asks. Each rule under Always approved says when and where it was added and how often it has been used, with Remove.

Reports from other agents

Agents that work together report to each other through Kepler. Each session has an inbox, which collects the following:

  • Reports and messages other agents send to the session.
  • A change in the state of a session this one started.
  • The answer to one of its approval requests.

When an item arrives for an idle session, Kepler wakes the session to read its inbox, and the conversation shows a row such as 2 new items to read. A busy session is woken once its turn ends.

A session with unread reports carries a reports badge with a count on its tab and rail row ({agent} hasn't read {count} reports from other agents), and the band above its composer offers Have it read them.

So that agents can't keep waking each other indefinitely, wakes pause after 20 turns in a row that agents started without a prompt from you. The band then reads Reports from other agents are paused: They no longer wake this session on their own. Resume to let them wake it again. Resume reports, or your next prompt, lets them wake it again.

In Terminal mode, only Claude Code, Pi, and OpenCode 2 sessions can be woken to read their inbox. Another Terminal agent shows No Kepler tools on its status bar: {name} can't use Kepler's workspace tools or inbox in a terminal.

Usage indicators

Two different readouts, and they measure different things.

Readout What it shows Availability
Context window How much of this session's context window is used, as a ring. Hover for tokens used, available, and total, the Estimated cost at API rates, and the Prompt cache: when it expires, and how much of the session's input came from it ({percent}% cached) Whenever the agent reports it
Plan usage Your provider plan's windows (5h and 7d) as Used and Resets columns, plus whatever else the provider reports about the plan Claude Code and Codex sessions report their own 5-hour and 7-day limits. The full readout, and Auggie, need Check plan usage with each agent's provider, which is on by default

While a session rests, the context ring warns about the prompt cache on its own: an hourglass with the time the cache has left, or Expiring or Expired. The context card then leads with the cache and warns The next message re-reads most of the context. With no context reading, the ring is drawn as a dashed circle, and its card still shows the cost when the agent reports one.

Check plan usage with each agent's provider fetches the full readout, including per-model limits and extra usage, from each agent's provider. It's on by default, in Settings → Agents → Usage limits:

Kepler asks each agent's provider for your plan usage, per-model limits and extra usage, using the agent's own sign-in. The sign-in goes nowhere else and is never changed. When off, Kepler shows only what agents report, and a message queued at a usage limit is sent automatically only if the agent says when the limit resets.

With the setting off, Kepler makes no request to any provider, but a Claude Code or Codex session still reports its own 5h and 7d readings as it runs, and those show. A card drawn from those readings alone says so: Reported by the agent. Turn on {setting} in {section} to also see per-model limits and extra usage.

The meter previews the shortest window, or the remaining plan balance where the provider reports a balance but no expressible percentage; hovering opens the detail. A spent window reads Limit reached until {time}, and every reading says how old it is (Updated {when}), with Refresh to ask again.

A usage bar is judged by pace: the share of the window you've used against the share of the window that has passed. Its detail reads On pace or Ahead of pace, and when you're ahead it estimates when the window runs dry, for example Runs out in ~{duration} at this rate. The bar turns amber once your use is more than 15 points ahead of pace, and red past 30 points; 90% used is always at least amber, and 100% is red. Below 10% used, or when there's no pace to judge, fixed bands apply instead: amber above 75% and red above 90%.

Provider What its popover carries
Claude Code The 5h and 7d windows, and an Extra usage pool with credits used against any monthly limit
Codex The same two windows, plus Usage limit resets: earned resets that clear an active limit early, each with its expiry
Auggie No windows, only the plan's credit balance: a plan line naming the plan with what is left of it, {remaining} / {included} credits left, or {remaining} credits left on an unmetered plan

An account on a third-party Claude provider shows no usage. If the figures cannot be fetched, the meter says which problem it hit rather than showing a stale number, for example Sign in to {agent} to see usage data. or This agent doesn't expose usage data. It adds Your sessions aren't affected. where that applies.

When you hit a usage limit

A session doesn't keep sending into a spent limit. As soon as the account's usage reading, or the agent's own report, shows its limit spent, Kepler holds prompts you queue until it lifts, rather than waiting for a turn to fail first. The queue banner reads Sending at {time}, when the quota resets. The prompts go out on their own shortly after the reset, even with no Kepler window open and across a restart. Send now releases one session's queue early. A spent limit on one model doesn't hold prompts for the account's other models. With Check plan usage with each agent's provider off, a queued prompt waits only if the agent itself says when its limit resets.

screenshotNew: The queue banner holding a prompt until the quota resets (*Sending at {time}, when the quota resets.*) with **Send now**.

A turn that hits the limit before the agent produced anything isn't lost: Kepler sends the same prompt again after the reset. If a turn is cut off by the limit after the agent had started replying, with nothing queued, the session stays put unless you turn on Settings → Agents → Usage limits → Resume after the usage limit resets: When a turn is cut off by your account usage limit and nothing is queued, Kepler queues a "continue" message that is sent shortly after the limit resets. It's off by default, and the queued message can be sent early or removed like any other.

When a session's account reaches its limit, a banner says so, {login} reached its usage limit, with Available again at {time}. To keep going now, Continue as {login} moves the conversation to the account with the most room left, and its menu lists your other accounts. See Moving a session to another account. With no other account to take the conversation, the banner suggests Add another account to keep going.

This applies to agents Kepler can read usage for: Claude Code, Codex, and Auggie.

Voice input

Every session's composer has a microphone. Click to record, speak, and the transcript lands in the prompt; transcription runs on your device. Voice input is off until you enable it and download a model.

screenshotReplace — ade-start-voice-input-01: the composer microphone hovered with a Qwen3-ASR engine ready, tooltip Start voice input · Qwen3-ASR 1.7B (needs the voice model downloaded).

When a Qwen engine is dictating, the microphone's tooltip names it, for example Start voice input · Qwen3-ASR 1.7B. When voice input can't start, the tooltip says why, such as a graphics card that isn't responding or a model that needs WebGPU, and links to the engine's settings. See Voice Input.

Session status

Each session carries a state, shown as a coloured dot on its tab and rows.

Status Meaning
Spawning Starting up
Ready Connected, nothing sent yet
Running Working on a turn
Waiting Blocked on you, and it says on what: Permission, Question, Plan review, or Prompt not sent. A pending Kepler approval also counts, as Approval needed or Approval needed, destructive, even while the agent keeps working
Unread Finished a turn you have not looked at
Idle Connected, nothing running
Working in background The turn ended, but a Claude Code background task is still running. Shown like Running, and it keeps the machine awake
Reconnecting Kepler is reattaching to a session that kept running while Kepler restarted
Error The turn or the process failed. A live session whose last turn failed on a rate limit, an exhausted quota, or an overloaded provider reads Error too
Ended The agent process isn't running. The conversation is kept, and a dormant session reads {state} (ended), its last state, until your next message starts it again

When Kepler has to show one status for several sessions, the most urgent wins: Waiting and Error outrank live work, and Unread sits above Idle so a finished turn is not buried.

Away from the session itself those states collapse into a coarser ramp of five (attention, active, idle, errored, inactive), which is what the main list's row and preview indicators read, and what decides where an Action fires. Attention covers both a waiting session and one holding an unanswered permission request, question, or approval request.

Task and session rows add one line saying what a session needs from you, most pressing first: Approval needed for {tool}, Asking: {question}, Waiting for you, Terminal waiting for you, or New reply.

Tabs, archiving, and restoring

Open sessions form a tab strip. Each tab shows the agent, the account when more than one exists, and a status dot. Drafts sit in the same strip, marked (draft) when the strip has room.

Hover a session's tab, its rail row, or a Terminal session's status bar for its card. Under the account, the card can add the following lines:

  • Running in the background, with and {count} more, for background tasks still running.
  • Continued from {name}, for a session started from another.
  • Rate limited · Resets {time}, when the account's limit is spent.
  • Context {percent}%, the share of the context window in use.

The following table lists what you can do with tabs:

Action Result
Cmd/Ctrl+T or + Opens a draft tab for a new session. See Draft session tabs
Alt+1–Alt+9 Jumps to a tab by position
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+[ / ] Previous or next tab
× on a tab in the Sessions column (Archive session) Archives the session
Archive on a rail row The same thing
Restore Brings an archived session back into the strip
Shut down agent Ends the process without archiving the session
Restart agent Shuts it down, then reconnects
Drag a tab On the Dashboard, reorders session and terminal tabs

In the Sessions column, closing a tab and archiving are one operation. Kepler stops the agent process, saves the conversation and its resume handle, and takes the tab out of the strip. Nothing is deleted. With Show tabs on the task page turned on, a tab's × is Close tab instead: it only hides the tab, and the session keeps running. See Task View.

Shutting down and restarting an agent

Ending a session and filing it away are separate acts, on a live session's row menu:

  • Shut down agent cancels the turn, waits a bounded grace for it to settle, saves the conversation at a turn boundary, and then ends the process. The session stays listed and stays resumable.
  • Restart agent does that and reconnects, so the session comes back on whichever agent build is installed now. That's how you pick up an agent you just updated without losing the conversation. A restart also re-reads your login shell, so an environment variable you added, changed, or removed in your shell's startup files reaches the restarted agent without restarting Kepler.

Restart shows its progress rather than going blank. A session you shut down before its first prompt stays listed as dormant rather than disappearing.

Dormant and archived sessions

A dormant session (one restored from disk, or stopped before it ever ran) shows its transcript, with the session's title leading its context card. It doesn't spawn an agent until you send the first message, so restoring a pile of old sessions costs nothing.

An archived session can be read without being restored: it opens read-only, labelled Read-only. Restore it to keep working., with Restore this session and open it live and Permanently delete this saved session beside it.

Some agents can store one conversation more than once. A session with more than one saved copy resumes the newest one it can run without asking. To pick another, choose Use another copy… on the session's menu: Kepler found more than one saved copy of this conversation. Pick the one to continue when you resume it. Each copy lists when it was updated and how many messages it holds.

Pointing an agent at another session

Copy session link, on a session's row or tab menu (or Cmd/Ctrl+C on a focused row), copies a Kepler link to the session, such as kepler://session/<id>. Copy task link on a task's menu does the same for a task. Paste the link into a prompt and the agent can read that conversation through the workspace tools. That's useful for "read what the other agent concluded and carry on". See Tasks and Resources.

An agent that can't use Kepler's workspace tools gets a copy instead. Kepler attaches a Markdown snapshot of each task or session linked in the prompt, up to five per prompt, and the same happens to a link you paste into such an agent's terminal. A snapshot is a copy taken when you send, not a live view.

Archived sessions collect under a {count} archived fold beneath the live ones in the task rail. On the Dashboard, the session strip's Archived sessions button searches them (Find an archived session…). Restoring puts the session back in the list; it reconnects when you open it.

Deleting a task or removing its repository ends that task's live sessions.

Keeping agents running through a restart

Settings → General → Keep agents running → Keep agents and terminals running across restarts and updates is on by default, though the setting still marks it experimental: Agent sessions and terminals stay alive while Kepler restarts or updates, and reconnect afterwards. Experimental. New sessions pick up a change to it. A build that can't keep agents running shows the switch off and greyed out.

screenshotNew: A session showing the *Reconnecting to the agent…* overlay after Kepler restarts.
Switch on (the default) Switch off
Quit Agent sessions keep running, and Kepler reattaches to them when it comes back. Shell tabs end, as they do when you quit any terminal app Asks first when sessions are running, and warns that each one will stop. Busy terminals are counted too
Updating Kepler Warns only about agents the update would actually interrupt. Shell tabs keep running and come back after the restart Warns how many agent sessions the restart will stop
Quit & End Sessions In the app and tray menus, including when only shell tabs are open. Ends the sessions on the way out; asks first only if one is mid-turn (End Sessions and Quit). Each can be continued again from its task Not applicable

While Kepler reattaches, an overlay covers the session, and the session offers no actions until it's back. The overlay reads Reconnecting to the agent…, then Restoring conversation history…. If the reattach fails, the session falls back to its saved conversation: Unable to reconnect to the agent. Resume to pick up from the saved conversation. A session whose background process dies keeps its conversation the same way, rather than starting a new one.

A session left running after a quit, with Kepler never coming back to it, is ended on its own after an hour without any output from the agent. An agent still mid-task keeps producing output, so it finishes first.

A terminal session kept running this way also keeps its keyboard and paste modes across the restart, and a reboot that ends it still leaves it resumable.

A session kept running this way can stop taking output if it reaches a storage or log limit. It then shows Agent output paused: Kepler stopped accepting agent output after reaching a storage or log limit. Your draft is preserved while output is paused.

Your computer will not go to sleep out from under a running agent. While any session is spawning, running, or waiting on you, Kepler holds the machine awake, and lets go as soon as the last one settles. It is on out of the box and turned off in Settings → General → Keep agents running → Keep this machine awake while agents work. See Settings.

When a session drops

A session that can't carry on says so on its context card, with a badge, one sentence, and only the recovery that applies.

Badge What happened What to do
Ended The process is gone; the conversation is not Resume
Exited {name} exited on its own. Restart it to continue from the saved conversation. Restart the agent
Can't resume {name} no longer has this conversation, so it can't continue. You can still read it below. Archive session, or Start new conversation
Sign-in failed {name} was unable to sign in. Sign in again to continue this conversation. Sign in again
Can't start {name} isn't installed or can't run, or The installed {name} can't run this session. Fix the install or path in agent settings, then restart
Folder missing The folder this conversation ran in was removed or moved. Recreate worktree and resume or Recreate folder and resume
Disconnected Kepler lost its connection to this session and ended it. Restart to pick up where it left off. Restart the agent
Unable to resume Another {name} is still running this conversation. Close it, then try again. Close the other copy, then resume

A card that has more to say carries Details, which opens the Diagnostics behind it with Copy, for a bug report.

An Agent error banner names the cause: {name} binary is missing or not executable., Agent authentication failed., Agent process exited on its own. It offers Reconnect, Authenticate, Compact, or Dismiss as the cause warrants. A usage limit reads {login} reached its usage limit, or {adapter} reached its session limit, with Available again at {time}. See When you hit a usage limit. A Codex turn the provider couldn't serve reads Codex was unable to complete this turn: OpenAI reported a capacity problem, not a usage limit. Reconnect to this conversation, then retry in a moment or switch models.

A Chat turn that fails on the provider's side says which kind of failure it hit and what to do:

  • {name} hit a rate limit. Wait a moment, then send your message again.
  • {name} is out of quota or has a billing problem. Check your plan or billing, then try again.
  • {name} is overloaded right now. Try again shortly.

While the agent retries a failed turn by itself, the session shows Retrying with the reason, such as {name} hit a rate limit and is retrying., the attempt (Attempt {attempt}.), and Next try at {time}.

In Terminal mode a dead process reads Terminal exited. Resume to reattach to this conversation.

If a session fails to start after you launch it, the task page and the Dashboard panel show {agent} was unable to start with the agent's message. Click Retry to try again, or Open agent settings to fix the agent's setup. Dismiss hides the notice in that window.

Sessions recover on their own where they can: Kepler renews an authentication token that expires mid-session so the session continues, and remote connections re-establish after a restart or sleep.

Notifications

Kepler tells you when a session replies, needs you, or fails. Each notification uses one of the following statuses:

Status When
Replied An agent finished replying and it's your turn
Asked a question The agent asks you something and waits for your answer
Needs permission The agent wants permission to do something
Approval needed An agent asks Kepler to do something only you can approve, like deleting a worktree. A destructive request reads Approval needed for a destructive action
Error The session stopped because something went wrong

A Terminal or external session blocked on a prompt in its own CLI notifies you too, for every agent. Sessions started outside Kepler notify only once they're on a task.

Titles read {status}: {task}, with the agent and the repository or branch beneath ({adapter} on {repo}/{branch}), and clicking one takes you to the session. Kepler shows it in the window already showing that session or its task, or else in the window you used last. An approval notification is titled with its status alone, with the action and Asked by {session} beneath.

By default, Kepler stays quiet about the work you are already watching: it sends no notification for the task open in the window you're using. To change that, turn on Notify even for the task you're looking at. While you're working in any Kepler window on a computer, that computer posts no desktop notifications; the window you're in shows them in Kepler instead, and several windows on one computer post a single alert. A cancelled turn is not a completion, and a session reconnecting is not a completion either, so neither notifies.

To choose which events notify you, and whether each one appears as a Desktop notification, In Kepler, or both, open Settings → Notifications. The same page has Play a sound with desktop notifications. In the desktop app, every event except Replies notifies on the desktop until you change it; in a browser, desktop notifications start off. After your first session starts, the desktop app sends a one-time Notifications are on notification, and clicking it opens Settings → Notifications. See Settings.

Notifications from terminal programs

A plain terminal can notify you too. When the program in a terminal blocks on you, or finishes or fails after working, Kepler sends a notification that names the terminal and its task, for example:

  • Approve it in {terminal}
  • Answer it in {terminal}
  • Sign in from {terminal}
  • Finished in {terminal}
  • Failed in {terminal}

Click View to open that terminal. Terminal notifications follow the same Settings → Notifications choices as agent sessions, and Kepler stays quiet only while that terminal is on screen in the focused window.

In-app toasts carry a close button on hover, and Clear all ({count}) dismisses the lot.

Reviewing what the agent changed

Reading the diff and syncing the branch are covered on their own page. See Review Changes.

feature: Ask Keplercontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true1178 words · ~5 min

"Ask Kepler is a chat and a terminal in a panel beside whatever you're looking at. Ask what needs you, have it check on your tasks and sessions, hand a chat on to a new task, or open a quick shell that belongs to no task."

Last updated: October 2026

Ask Kepler is a panel with two sections: a Chat with an agent that can see what's on your screen and check on your tasks and sessions, and a Terminal of quick shells that belong to no task. Use it for a question that doesn't deserve a task of its own, or to find out what needs you without leaving the page you're on.

This page covers opening and arranging the panel, the chat, and the panel's terminals. For the agent sessions that run inside a task, see Agent Sessions.

Open and close the panel

You can open Ask Kepler from anywhere in Kepler in one of these ways:

How Opens
The Ask Kepler button (the Kepler mark) in the top bar The panel, on the section you used last
Cmd/Ctrl+L (Toggle Ask Kepler chat) The Chat section
Ctrl+` on every platform, macOS included (Toggle Ask Kepler terminal) The Terminal section, starting a shell if none is open

Press the same shortcut again while focus is still inside the panel to put it away. On Windows and Linux, a focused shell keeps Ctrl+L for itself, so press it outside the terminal to toggle the chat.

The panel floats as a card over the left or right edge of the page, under the top bar. Nothing dims behind it, so the page underneath stays usable. Its header holds the open section, a button for the other section, and the panel's own controls:

  • Move to the left or Move to the right docks the panel to the other edge of the window.
  • Close Ask Kepler puts it away.

Resize. Drag the panel's inner edge (Resize Ask Kepler) to change its width, or focus the edge and use the arrow keys. Double-click the edge to return to the default width. The panel is never narrower than 360 pixels or wider than 60% of the window. Kepler remembers the width and the side on this computer, for every window.

Escape also closes the panel, unless the key means something else where you are: a text field that holds a draft, a find bar, a shell (where the program owns the key), or a chat whose agent is still answering, where Escape stops the turn instead.

Closing only hides the panel. A half-typed question, the open chat, and every shell's scrollback are still there the next time you open it.

The chat

The Chat section is a conversation with an agent that works for you across Kepler rather than inside one task. A new chat opens on Ask Kepler anything, Ask a quick question, or have Kepler check on and manage your tasks and sessions., and a composer that reads Ask anything. Pick the agent from the pill in the composer, type your question, and send it.

Starter prompts

A new chat offers starting points you can send with one click:

  • What needs me right now?
  • Summarize this task, only while you're on a task's page
  • What changed across my tasks today?

A starter goes out as the chat's first message and leaves anything you already typed in the composer alone.

What the chat knows

The chat keeps up with what you have on screen. With each message, Kepler tells the agent where you are when that has changed since its last message:

  • The page you're on: the Dashboard, a task, Settings, or somewhere else
  • The task you're looking at, and its checkouts
  • The sessions on screen

So a question such as what's this one stuck on? reads against the task in front of you. Kepler also tells it which sessions are waiting on you, such as approvals, permission requests, questions, and failed or unread sessions, and asks it to bring these up only when you ask. Ask What needs me right now? to get that list.

The agent can read your tasks and sessions and act on them through Kepler's own tools. Before it starts a session on a task that it didn't create, Kepler asks you first. When it mentions a task or a session, it names it as a link you can click.

Which agents can answer

A panel chat always runs over the Agent Client Protocol (ACP), so the agent pill lists only agents that Kepler can drive over ACP. An agent that runs only in a terminal, such as Pi, can't answer in the panel. See Agent Integrations for which agents run in Chat.

Past chats and new chats

The chat's title in the panel header is also a picker of your chats. Open it to find the following:

  • New chat, which returns the section to a fresh chat
  • Continue in a new task, for the chat that's open
  • Past chats, newest activity first, each with when it was last active. Click one to open it again, or use Archive {title} beside it to put it away. With none yet, the list reads No past chats yet.

The New chat button beside the title does the same as the picker's first row.

Continue in a new task

When an answer turns out to be the start of real work, choose Continue in a new task. It sits under the chat's latest reply and in the past-chats picker. It opens the Task Composer with the prompt Pick up where the chat left off., the chat attached as the session the task continues from, and the same agent, account, model, and mode the chat used. Edit anything you like before you start the task. See Create a Task.

A panel chat belongs to no task, so when a task's session links back to the chat it continued from, the chat opens in the panel. A notification from a panel chat also opens the panel on that chat.

Quick terminals

The Terminal section holds shells that belong to no task, in the same tab strip a task page's terminal drawer uses. The first time you open the section, Kepler starts a shell so that the strip is never empty. Press Ctrl+` with no shell open and Kepler starts one, the way the same key does in VS Code. After you close the last shell, the strip stays empty until you ask for another.

Where a new shell opens depends on where you are:

  • On a task's page, a new shell opens in one of that task's checkouts.
  • Anywhere else, or on a task with no checkouts, it opens in your Home folder.

The panel's shells stay the panel's own. They never join a task's terminal drawer, and they keep running when you move between pages or close the panel. While the Terminal section is open, its tab strip doubles as the panel header, with Chat as a button at its leading edge.

feature: Review Changescontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true6649 words · ~30 min1 TODO

An agent finished. Read the diff in the Changes overlay, comment on it, stage and commit, update the branch from its base, resolve update conflicts with AI, and hand the rest to AI Sync or AI Compose.

Last updated: October 2026

An agent has stopped and says it is done. Now you read what it actually did.

All of that happens in the task's worktree: the Git working copy the agent made its changes in. The task view rail groups a task's worktrees under Changes: that's where you go to review them. See the task view.

Reading a diff has two surfaces, and they answer different questions:

Surface What it is for
The worktree tab's changes panel The table of contents: which view, how much changed, the list of files, and the commit box
The Changes overlay The reading surface: a large diff over the page, one file or many, with the file tree and commit box beside it

Every file in the panel leads into the overlay, opened on exactly the view the panel was showing.

The worktree tab

Open a worktree from the rail's Changes group and it opens in a tab of its own: a strip of verbs across the top, the changes panel below it.

The strip

Control What it does
The branch The branch this checkout is on. ⋮ beside it, or a right-click, opens the worktree's menu, the same menu the worktree has in the rail and on the Dashboard. On a task with several worktrees, the branch name also switches between them
The sync control One button whose verb follows the branch's state (Publish, Push, Pull, Fetch or Update), with the counts beside it. See Publishing and syncing the branch
Open in Hands the checkout to another app. The split button runs your default; the chevron lists the rest, ending with Open on {provider} where the branch lives on a supported host
Run a command The repository's configured commands, and Manage commands…. See Settings

The worktree's menu holds the following:

  • Open changes, Open in, and Open pull request when one is linked.
  • Continue {operation} and Abort {operation}…, during a stopped rebase or merge.
  • A Branch submenu: Rename branch, the upstream (see Set or change the upstream), Change merge target…, Rebase onto {target} or Merge {target} into this branch, and Push, Pull and Fetch.
  • New session here, New terminal here and Run command here.
  • Copy branch name, Copy worktree path and Copy remote URL.
  • Refresh, Detach worktree… and Delete worktree….

The strip's controls stay quiet until you need them: each draws its outline only on hover, on keyboard focus, or while its menu is open.

The strip fits itself to the column one step at a time rather than all at once at a breakpoint: the sync control's counts fold first, leaving its icon; then the branch name truncates; then Open in and Run a command drop out of the row. Both stay a right-click, or the tab's own menu, away.

For a shell in this checkout, use the rail row's New terminal here, or Cmd/Ctrl+J. Terminals live in the drawer beneath the columns, not on this strip. See The Task View.

The changes panel

The panel is a row of views over a list of files, with the commit box under the list.

Part What it does
Working changes What isn't committed yet. Its button carries a count
Commits What the branch adds against its merge target, with a count. The commit list leads with All commits; click a commit to narrow to it, or Shift-click another to take a range
All changes Both together: everything from the merge target to the files on disk. With no merge target it reads No base branch, so this shows uncommitted changes
All files Every file in the worktree, not only what changed
The counts Commits, files and the line delta, then from {branch}: the merge target the counts are measured against; in Commits and All changes, click it to compare against another branch. See Merge targets
View as tree / View as list How the files are drawn
Filter files Narrows the list
Refresh Reads the worktree again

The view is chosen once and then left alone. Kepler won't flip you to Commits the moment the last working change is committed, or back to Working changes because an agent touched a file. Either would move the list under whoever was reading it.

In All changes, a file that also has uncommitted work carries a pencil mark, and its tooltip reads Has uncommitted changes.

All files reads the whole worktree. Changed files read as they do in All changes, and a file you didn't change opens as it is. Files and folders that Git ignores are set apart and marked Ignored, and symbolic links are marked Symbolic link. A folder lists its contents when you open it, and a very large one shows only its first items (Showing the first {countText} items).

The header above the commit list says how far the branch has come: {count} commits ahead of {branch}, with branched here marking the merge base. Commits the upstream doesn't have yet are drawn with a green dot and line, and read Not pushed. Hover a commit for its card: the author, when it was committed, the full message with its lists and trailers, the short hash, its line changes and its file count. Right-click a commit for Copy commit SHA, Copy commit message, Open commit on {provider} and Copy commit URL. A commit that isn't pushed yet has no page on the host, so it leaves out the last two.

A wholly untracked folder with hundreds of files, such as a build output nobody ignored or a test sandbox, shows as one muted row with its file count instead of thousands of rows. Show on the row lists them. Untracked files never carry line counts, and a line total that leaves out a collapsed folder's files is marked partial. Staging, discarding and committing take the folder as a whole, and the confirmation names its file count.

The Changes overlay

Click a file, or Open changes from the worktree's menu, and the overlay opens over the page. It opens at a width sized for one column of code, or two when you last used Split, and you can drag its edges to resize it. When it's at that width, Maximize in its header fills the window, and Restore size brings it back.

The overlay is where you act on a diff, not only read it. In Working changes and All changes you can edit a file in place. In Working changes you can also stage it, commit it, resolve its conflicts, discard it or delete it. You can leave review comments for your agent too. See Staging and committing and Review comments.

The layout

Part What it holds
The tree The same file tree the panel had, over the same view, with its own view and layout controls. In Working changes, each row has a staging checkbox and the commit box sits beneath the list
The viewer The diff, or several diffs stacked when you opened a folder or a selection. A single file can show as File, the whole file with its changes marked in the gutter, or Changes, the diff

Hide changes panel, or Cmd/Ctrl+B, collapses the tree and hands its width to the viewer. With the tree hidden, the view picker and a Jump to file… switcher move onto the viewer's own header, so you never lose the way back.

A file's header holds File and Changes, Stacked and Split, then Find, Wrap lines, Open in, and a menu that includes Add to task. A file that's unchanged always shows as File, and a deleted file as Changes. A file in Commits shows As of {sha}: the file as that commit left it. While an edit is unsaved, the toggle waits: Save or discard your edit to switch views.

Moving around

Gesture What it does
Click a file Opens it on its own
Cmd/Ctrl-click Adds a file to the set on screen
Shift-click Extends the selection to a range
Click a folder Opens every file in it, stacked
Type a name in the tree Jumps to the first file that starts with it
Alt+↑ / Alt+↓ Previous and next file
F7 / Shift+F7 Next and previous change, carrying on into the next file that has one
Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+A Open all changes
Cmd/Ctrl+B Shows or hides the changes panel
F6 / Shift+F6 Moves between the header, the tree, the commit message and the viewer
Cmd/Ctrl+P Filter files, or Jump to file… when the tree is hidden
Cmd/Ctrl+F Filters the tree when the tree has focus, and finds in the code anywhere else
Escape Closes the overlay. With an unsaved edit, it asks first

The header counts what you are reading: {index} of {total}, and {count} files shown once a filter is narrowing it.

Reading a diff

  • Each diff is headed by the file name, then its folder, with its +/− line stats. Hover the name for the full path.
  • Added lines are green, removed lines red.
  • Unmodified regions are collapsed, leaving three lines of context around each change.
  • Expand all and Collapse all act on the whole stack; a single diff folds from its own header.
  • Wrap lines on the viewer's header wraps long lines instead of scrolling them sideways.
  • A file that is unchanged in the current view is drawn as Unchanged in this scope rather than hidden.
  • A conflicted file is marked ! in the tree and the viewer.

Right-click a file row for its own menu: Stage file or Unstage file, Discard changes, Delete, Open in editor, and an Open in submenu with your editors, Open in default app, Show in Finder (macOS), Show in Explorer (Windows) or Show in file manager (Linux), and Open file on {provider} where the file exists on a supported host. Copy path and Copy relative path end the menu. Select several rows and the items act on all of them.

Finding in the code

Cmd/Ctrl+F in the viewer opens a find bar at its top right. It searches every file in the stack, edits included, and counts the matches as {current} of {total}, or No results. Lines folded away as unchanged aren't searched.

Control Windows and Linux macOS
Next match F3 Cmd+G
Previous match Shift+F3 Cmd+Shift+G
Match case Alt+C Cmd+Option+C
Match whole word Alt+W Cmd+Option+W
Use regular expression Alt+R Cmd+Option+R

Escape closes the bar. Kepler remembers the three toggles, and a one-line selection fills the field when you open the bar.

Selecting and copying work across a whole file, including lines that aren't on screen, and double-clicking selects a word.

Editing in place

In Working changes and All changes, what you see is the file on disk: click into the diff, or into the whole file in File view, and type. Cmd/Ctrl+S saves.

An unsaved edit shows Unsaved with Save and Discard beside it. Nothing throws it away silently: Escape, closing the overlay, or moving to another file, view or worktree asks Save your edit? first. A file with an unsaved edit can't be staged or committed until you save or discard it, since Git records what is on disk.

Editing a file reached through a symlink saves to the file it points at, and the link stays a link. A file you can't edit carries a lock after its name. Hover or focus it for the reason:

  • To edit this file, switch to Working changes or All changes. Commits are read-only.
  • A symbolic link's target can't be edited here.
  • Only UTF-8 text files can be edited here.
  • This file is read-only here.

Stacked or split

Stacked puts removals and additions in one column; Split puts the old and new file side by side. The toggle sits on the viewer's header, and Settings → Appearance → Diff view sets what it starts on.

Split needs room for two columns of code. Below roughly 640 pixels of viewer width the diff falls back to Stacked whatever the setting says. The header says Too narrow for a split diff rather than truncating both sides into noise.

Diffs that are not code

Case What you see
Images and SVGs Before and After previews, each with its size on disk
A new file The whole file, syntax-highlighted, rather than an all-additions diff
A file with no textual change No textual changes to display
A file whose change is too large This file is too large to show here ({size}).
A very large diff Once the diff is too large as a whole, each remaining file reads This change is not shown because the whole diff is too large. and has no line stats

Diffs normalize line endings, so a file that changed from CRLF to LF does not read as a rewrite.

When a scope can't be read

A failed read says so rather than standing in for an empty one: Unable to load these changes, with Try again, and Git did not say why. when Git offered no reason. An empty view reads No working changes or No changes in this scope; a branch with no base yet reads No base branch to compare against yet.

On a phone

In a phone's browser, through Remote Access, the overlay drops its header and tree for one bar:

  • Close.
  • The file's name, which opens the list of changed files as a sheet from the bottom of the screen.
  • Previous and next file.
  • A menu of everything else: the view and the commit pick, File or Changes, the diff layout, Wrap lines, saving or discarding an edit, why a file is read-only, and the file's own actions.

The overlay opens Stacked on a phone, with lines wrapped.

See Remote Environments.

Review comments

Read the diff, mark what needs fixing, and hand the whole list to the agent in one go.

To comment on Do this
One line Hover it and click the + beside its line number
A range Click the + on the first line, then Shift-click the last, or extend with Shift+↑ / Shift+↓
What you selected in the editor Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+C, which also works on the line the caret is on

A draft opens under the last line. Type your note and press Comment, or Cmd/Ctrl+Enter. Each comment becomes a card under its lines, with Edit and Delete comment. Comments work on every diffed file in a stack, in Working changes, and in Commits once you pick a commit or a range. All changes, All commits, and a file shown whole, such as a new file, take none. The file header and its tree row show how many comments a file has.

Comments stay in Kepler. Nothing is posted to your Git host, and the agent doesn't see them until you send them.

The overlay's header sums them up: {count} comments, Add to prompt, and a Comment options menu with Discard all comments. Add to prompt turns every comment into one block and adds it to the composer of the session running in that worktree. Each comment in the block carries its path:lines, the quoted code, and your note. It does not send: you read the prompt, add to it, and press Send yourself. Each card has its own Add to prompt for sending just that one. Sent comments are cleared from the diff.

Add to prompt needs a session in this task running in the same worktree as the diff; the button's tooltip names the session it will write into. From the Dashboard, open the task first. In an overlay opened from outside a conversation, such as from a file path, the button is locked: Open this file from a conversation to send comments.

Typed text is never thrown away: Escape only leaves a draft's field, and a draft with text survives closing the overlay or moving to another file. A comment whose lines changed or whose file left the view is kept and listed as {count} comments outside this diff rather than drawn under the wrong code.

Staging and committing

In Working changes, the tree is where you stage and the commit box beneath it is where you commit. The panel and the overlay share one commit box per worktree, so a message you start in one is waiting in the other, and it's kept when the overlay closes.

Staging

  • Each file and folder row has a checkbox: tick it to stage, untick to unstage. Space on a focused row does the same. The tree's header box stages or unstages everything listed, and its badge reads {staged} of {total} staged.
  • A filter narrows what the header box acts on, so a file you filtered out is never swept up with the rest.
  • Discard changes, or Delete on a focused row (Backspace on a Mac), asks first. Your current version goes to the Trash (the Recycle Bin on Windows), so you can get it back.
  • Delete in the row menu removes the file from disk without touching the index, so Git reports the deletion like any other. It moves the file to the Trash too.

Where Kepler can't use a Trash, the confirmation says the change is permanent before you confirm it.

The commit box

The commit box is framed like the prompt composer: one message field whose first line is the subject. A counter appears once the subject passes 50 characters.

Control What it does
The message field Cmd/Ctrl+Enter commits
Amend last commit Rewrites the last commit instead of adding one. Ticking it fills an empty box with the last commit's message, and the button reads Amend commit. An amend can change only the message, with nothing staged. Amending a commit you've already pushed asks Amend a pushed commit? first, because the next push will have to force
Commit Commits what is staged

When the button can't commit yet, its tooltip says why: Stage changes above to commit, Enter a commit message, Save or discard your edit first, Wait for staging to finish, or Resolve the conflicts first.

The commit is exactly what you were looking at. It waits for staging still in flight. If an agent changes the index in the meantime, Kepler refuses the commit, keeps your message, and says The staged files changed since you looked. Check them, then commit again.

When a Git hook stops the commit, the box says A Git hook stopped the commit and shows the end of the hook's output, with Show output for the rest. A hook that never finishes can be stopped with Cancel commit.

Resolving merge conflicts

When a merge, rebase, cherry-pick or revert stops on conflicts, the conflicted files show with a !.

To resolve a file Do this
By hand Edit it in the viewer, save, then tick its checkbox or choose Mark resolved. If conflict markers are still in it, Kepler asks first
Take one side whole Use current version or Use incoming version from the row menu. Your hand-merged copy goes to the Trash first

Once nothing is conflicted, finish the operation. A stopped merge, cherry-pick or revert finishes with a commit from the commit box. A rebase continues from the worktree's menu: Continue rebase, locked until every conflict is resolved. Abort {operation}… puts the branch back the way it was before the operation started.

Other ways to commit

How When
Ask the agent The common case. The agent that wrote the change commits it, and you read the result
The worktree's terminal git add, git commit, your own aliases. New terminal here on the worktree's rail row opens one already in the right folder
AI Compose Hand an agent the tools to reorganize messy changes into clean, focused commits. See Agent tools: AI Sync and AI Compose

Publishing and syncing the branch

The strip's sync control is one button, and its verb is whatever the branch actually needs.

State The button reads What it does
Behind or diverged from its merge target Update, or Review conflicts with a warning icon when updating would conflict. After an applied update, Update again once the base has moved on Opens the update view in the worktree panel. See below
No upstream Publish, with Creates {remote}/{branch} on the remote Publishes the branch
Ahead Push, with {count} ahead Pushes
Rebased by an applied update Force push, with Rebased onto {target} — force push to update the remote Replaces the remote's old commits after you confirm Force push?
Behind Pull, with {count} behind Pulls. A branch that is both ahead and behind its upstream reads Behind, pull first on Push
In step Fetch, with Up to date Updates your remote-tracking refs, and removes the ones whose branch was deleted on the remote, so deleted branches leave every branch picker

Counts only move on a fetch, so the control says when it last looked: Checked {time}.

The chevron beside it opens a menu that leads with whatever the button runs. Then it lists the following:

  • Push (or Publish), Pull and Fetch. Each says where it goes: To {upstream}, From {upstream}, and From {remote} or From all remotes: {remotes}.
  • Set upstream or Change upstream, on any branch that isn't a detached HEAD. See Set or change the upstream.
  • Force push: Rewrites the remote branch. Offered when the branch has diverged from its upstream, as a deliberate, separate click.
  • Update: where the branch stands against its merge target, and the way into the update view. It reads Review conflicts, Roll back update or Update again when one of those applies.

After you apply a rebase, the remote still has the branch's old commits, so Force push becomes the main button instead of a menu item. The Force push? confirmation explains that the branch was rebased and that Kepler refuses the force push if someone else pushed in the meantime.

SSH keys that ask for a PIN or passphrase. On macOS and Linux, a fetch you start yourself, such as Fetch or Refresh branches, can use your SSH_ASKPASS helper, so a key that needs a PIN or passphrase, such as a security key, can fetch. Kepler allows at most three prompts per connection, and 60 seconds for each. Fetches Kepler runs in the background never prompt. Kepler never prompts on Windows; use an SSH agent there.

Set or change the upstream

The upstream is the remote branch that Push and Pull use. To set it, change it or remove it, open Set upstream or Change upstream from the sync control's menu or from the worktree's Branch submenu. Change upstream shows the branch it tracks now.

The picker lists every remote branch under Search remote branches:

  • A tracked branch that no longer exists comes first, marked Not found.
  • A remote branch with the same name as yours is marked Same name, and the one you track now Current.
  • Publish as {remote}/{branch}… sits above the list when no remote has a branch of the same name, and opens the publish dialog.
  • Unset upstream sits above the list when the branch tracks one.

Any remote branch can be the upstream, whatever its name. Pick one and Kepler saves it at once: Upstream set to {upstream}. The picker lists only branches already fetched, so a branch someone else just pushed needs Refresh branches, beside the search field, which fetches from every remote.

Updating from the base

The update view rebases or merges the branch onto its merge target. It works on a copy first, and your branch changes only when you apply the result.

To update the branch from its merge target, follow these steps:

  1. Open the update view from the sync control. The title reads Update {branch}, with from {branch} under it. Kepler picks the detected base. To use another base, choose it from the picker. Refresh branches, beside the picker's search field, fetches from every remote first.
  2. Press Rebase, or choose Merge from its menu. To abandon a run in progress, press Stop. Your branch is never touched.
  3. When the copy reaches Ready to apply, press Apply to move your branch to it, or press Discard to throw the copy away. If the branch can push, the Apply menu also offers Apply & push. After a rebase, Apply & push force-pushes, because the remote still has the old commits.
  4. To undo an applied update, press Roll back. The view returns to Ready to apply and keeps the result, so you can apply it again. The sync control's menu offers the same thing as Roll back update.

The Roll back menu holds two more options:

  • Roll back and discard: returns the branch to where it was and throws the result away.
  • Roll back & push: rolls back and then force-pushes the restored branch. This option appears only when the branch can push.

A result waiting to be applied doesn't lock the branch, so the branch or its base can move on in the meantime. You can also pick another base in the picker after a run. Either way, the result no longer matches, and the view reads The last update result is out of date with Recalculate and Discard. Recalculate runs the same update again. Kepler won't apply a result whose base moved.

After an applied update, when the base moves on again, the view reads Another update is available for {base}. Run another update to include it. and offers Update again. If your branch changed after the update instead, it reads Your branch changed after the update. You can discard the saved result.

A run that fails says why, with Try again and Dismiss.

When updating would conflict, the sync control reads Review conflicts. The view title reads Review conflicts in {branch}, with with {branch} under it, and lists the conflicting files instead of offering a plain update. Pick a file to see its conflicting hunks.

You have two ways through the conflicts:

  • Resolve them with AI. Press Rebase with AI, or choose Merge with AI from its menu. See Resolve update conflicts with AI.
  • Resolve them yourself. Rebase in the worktree's terminal and resolve each file in the file tree.

Resolve update conflicts with AI

AI conflict resolution runs the rebase or merge on a copy and resolves each conflicted file for you. Your branch changes only when you apply the result.

AI conflict resolution needs all of the following:

  • A paid GitKraken plan. On the Community plan, the button shows a padlock and its tooltip reads AI conflict resolution needs a paid plan.
  • AI Sync turned on in Settings → Agents → Agent tools. While it's off, the tooltip reads Turn on Sync in Settings → Agents → Agent tools to resolve conflicts with AI.
  • A GitKraken sign-in. If you aren't signed in, the run stops with Sign in to GitKraken to resolve conflicts with AI.

To resolve conflicts with AI, follow these steps:

  1. Open the update view from Review conflicts on the sync control.
  2. Check the base under the title. To use another base, choose it from the picker.
  3. Press Rebase with AI, or choose Merge with AI from its menu.
  4. Watch the progress line, which reads Resolving conflicts · {count} files resolved. To abandon the run, press Stop.
  5. Read the review when the run reaches Ready to apply. The next section describes what the review shows.
  6. Press Apply, or Apply & push if the branch can push. To throw the result away, press Discard.

If the AI can't finish some files, the run stops without touching your branch: Your branch wasn't changed. The AI was unable to finish these files, so the update stopped. Each file is listed with the reason, such as a binary file, or a conflict Git can't merge as text, which you resolve by hand.

Read the AI review

The review explains what the AI changed so you can decide whether to apply it. It contains the following parts:

  • A verdict badge: No issues found, Needs review or Blocked.
  • A summary: a short explanation of the run, followed by any Check: items that name a file and the reason to look at it, such as low confidence, possible issue or left unresolved.
  • A summary line: AI resolved {count} files · confidence {confidence}%.
  • A card for each commit: the commit's short SHA and subject, or Merge for a merge run, and its file count. A card shows Resolving… while the AI works on it.
  • A row for each file: the method used and a confidence badge, followed by the AI's reasoning for that file. A low confidence shows as a warning.

Each file row shows one of the following methods. Ours and Theirs are Git's names for the two sides of the conflict. In a merge, Ours is your branch and Theirs is the base. In a rebase they swap: Ours is the branch you're rebasing onto, and Theirs is your branch.

Method Meaning
AI The AI wrote the resolution
Ours Kept the Ours side
Theirs Kept the Theirs side
Deleted Resolved by deleting the file
Auto Resolved mechanically, without the AI
Skipped Left unresolved

The review persists across reloads and windows, so you can close the view and come back to it before you apply.

Click a file row to review that file in full. Its review names the commit, the method (Kept {branch} when the AI kept one side), the confidence and the AI's reasoning. Then it walks through each conflict as Conflict {n} of {total}, at its line, marked Kept {branch} or Merged, with its own confidence and the AI's reason for it. Conflict layout sets how each conflict reads:

  • Conflict | Result: the conflict with Git's markers for both branches and the common ancestor, beside the result.
  • Branches + ancestor: both branches and the Common ancestor side by side, then the result.

Show {count} more lines above and below, or Show all lines, bring in the rest of the file around a conflict.

verifyResolution method and persistence behavior verified against kepler v0.15.0 strings and src/ui/components/git/sync/; check against a live AI run before publishing.

Merge targets

A branch's merge target is the branch it will merge into: what its commits, counts and Update are measured against. Kepler detects one, and you can change it.

To Do this
Compare against another branch for now In Commits or All changes, click from {branch} in the panel or the overlay and pick a branch. The commit list, counts and strip follow it, and the label's tooltip reads For this view only. Nothing is saved
Keep that comparison Click Save as merge target, beside the pick. In a narrow panel it reads Save. Only remote branches can be saved
Change it outright Change merge target… from the merge target chip on the Dashboard, or from the worktree's Branch submenu, which shows the target in force beside it. Remote branches only; choosing one saves it
Go back to the detected one Reset to automatic ({ref}) in the picker
Find a branch someone else just pushed Refresh branches, beside the picker's search field, fetches from every remote. The pickers list only branches already fetched
Update the branch from its merge target Rebase onto {target} or Merge {target} into this branch from the merge target chip's menu on the Dashboard, or from the worktree's Branch submenu. Both open the update view

Kepler and GitLens share a branch's merge target, so a change made in GitLens shows up in Kepler while it's running.

A branch that landed in its merge target without a pull request counts as merged. That includes rebase- and squash-merged branches, which Git itself doesn't see as merged; Kepler checks their content. The Dashboard says Merged into {target}.

Where else changes show up

The Dashboard's task preview shows each worktree as a two-row worktree pill:

  • The top row: the branch and repository, its menu, the pull request, and the sync control.
  • The bottom row: chips for the uncommitted files, the commits and the merge target, with Open in and Run beside them. Each chip has a hover card that says what a click does, and a right-click menu of its own.
  • A task with several worktrees shows one pill at a time. Its leading edge shows the position ({position}/{count}) and expands to Show all {count} worktrees. The branch name becomes a switcher you can also step through with the arrow keys, and Auto-switch to active worktree lets it follow whichever worktree is busiest.

Clicking the uncommitted or commits chip opens that worktree's changes panel, on that view, in a side pane of the task panel, without going to the task page first. The pane sits beside the sessions when the panel is wide enough, and in place of them when it isn't. Its files open the same Changes overlay. Click the chip again to close the pane, or press Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+G to show or hide it. See The Kepler Interface.

A file or folder in a worktree opens in the Changes overlay too, wherever you open it by its path, such as a file path in a terminal or Open in Changes on a file tab's menu. A file opens in File view, at its line: on Working changes when it has uncommitted changes, and on All files otherwise. A folder opens on All files, with its changed files stacked.

Update many worktrees at once

To bring several worktrees and local branches up to date with their bases in one pass, use Update in Settings → Repositories → Worktrees and branches. The Update worktrees panel checks each one and lists what it will update and what it skips, such as a worktree with uncommitted changes, one where an agent is working, or a branch already checked out in a worktree. A local branch with no worktree is updated in place, without being checked out.

Choose Rebase or Merge, and tick Push after update to push each updated branch too. Branches with no upstream are updated but not pushed, and say No upstream, update only. Nothing changes until Update is pressed, and Roll back undoes a worktree's update. See Settings.

Opening a pull request

Kepler has no "create pull request" button. You open the pull request outside Kepler: the agent opens it, you run a command in the worktree's terminal, or you use your host's website. Kepler's part starts once the pull request exists: it tracks the pull request as a task resource. Three paths attach one:

Path What happens
The agent publishes it When an agent publishes a pull request, Kepler reads the result, checks that it belongs to one of the worktree's remotes, and attaches it to the task: no action from you
The agent attaches it Agents can attach a link to the task themselves through Kepler's workspace tools. Kepler works out from the URL whether it is a pull request, an issue, or a plain link, and fetches its title and status where it can. Kepler attaches the link even when that lookup fails. The same tools detach one, including a link you attached yourself
You attach an existing one Add resource → Pull requests in the task view searches your connected hosts. Add to task attaches what you pick

Kepler asks an agent to attach only the pull request the task is actually about, not every URL it reads while investigating. Anything the agent attaches shows up in the task right away.

Attaching a pull request also unlocks the Address Feedback Action on that task. See Actions and Pull Request Integrations.

Agent tools: AI Sync and AI Compose

AI Sync and AI Compose give agents Git tools they don't otherwise have. Both are off until you turn them on, and both need a paid GitKraken subscription. Turn them on in Settings → Agents → Agent tools, which Kepler describes as Kepler's own tools your agents can call, starting with rebasing, merging and cleaning up commits. The section lists the following tools:

Tool What it gives agents
AI Sync Lets agents rebase or merge your branch and resolve conflicts for you. Every change can be undone.
AI Compose Lets agents turn messy changes into clean, focused commits. Every change can be undone.

With a tool turned on, the agents in your sessions gain the matching tools, and you ask for the work in the chat. AI Sync also powers the Rebase with AI and Merge with AI buttons in the update view. See Resolve update conflicts with AI.

Tool How an agent uses it
AI Sync Updates the branch from its base with a rebase or merge, and resolves conflicts as it goes. By default, the agent prepares the result for review and leaves the branch untouched until it applies the result. You can read the outcome first and have it applied or rolled back. AI Sync backs up every run. It also drops commits whose changes are already upstream, which helps after a squash-merge. Tell the agent what matters, such as which side is authoritative and what it must preserve, and it passes that guidance to the conflict resolver
AI Compose Plans the reorganization first and applies it as a second step, so you can read the plan before anything moves. It can also split a multi-commit branch into a stack, and undo what it applied

When you turn either tool on, Kepler confirms that New agent sessions will pick up this change. A running session keeps the tools it started with. To use a new tool, start a new session.

Both sets of tools default to the session's own worktree. Each can also name another worktree attached to the same task, so one session can tidy a branch it doesn't sit in.

On the Community plan, both rows still appear, with a padlock and the tooltip Not available on the Community plan. Upgrade to unlock. Everything else on this page works on the Community plan, except AI conflict resolution.

Related

  • The Task View: the rail, the columns, and where the worktree opens
  • Tasks and Resources: worktrees, and detaching or deleting one safely
  • Agent Sessions: directing the agent that produced these changes
  • Actions: Review on a task reviews exactly this uncommitted work
  • Settings: Diff view, Agent tools, and Worktrees and branches
feature: Voice Inputcontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true1977 words · ~9 min1 TODO

Dictate to an agent instead of typing. Voice input transcribes on your device with Qwen3-ASR, or with Whisper where WebGPU is unavailable, learns the words you fix, works push-to-talk or hands-free, and is available in remote windows.

Last updated: October 2026

Voice input puts a microphone in the agent prompt so you can talk instead of typing. Transcription runs on your device, using a model Kepler downloads once. Kepler never uploads your audio.

Kepler has two transcription engines:

  • Qwen3-ASR is the default. It reads what you're writing, so it gets developer terms such as Jira, rebase, or pull request right more often than Whisper. It needs a graphics card with WebGPU.
  • Whisper works on any machine. Kepler uses it where WebGPU is unavailable, and keeps it for anyone who set up Whisper before Qwen3-ASR was available.
screenshotReplace — ade-start-voice-input-01: on a machine with a WebGPU graphics card, set up Qwen3-ASR 1.7B in Settings → Voice input, then hover the composer's microphone so the tooltip reads Start voice input · Qwen3-ASR 1.7B.

Voice input is off until you set it up in Settings → Voice input: Kepler downloads a model, and the microphone in the prompt goes live when the model is ready.

Choose what happens when you stop talking

To record, click Start voice input in the prompt, speak, then click Stop and transcribe.

To choose what Kepler does with what you say, go to Settings → Voice input → When you stop talking and pick one of these options:

Option What it does
Insert the text so you can edit it Puts the text in the prompt for you to review. This is the default
Send the prompt Sends the prompt as soon as you stop recording
Keep listening, transcribing each phrase as you pause Keeps the microphone open and transcribes each phrase as you pause

With Qwen3-ASR, Keep listening groups phrases you speak without a long pause into one paragraph. As the paragraph grows, Kepler transcribes it again as a whole and replaces the text it already inserted for it, so earlier words can change as you keep talking. Once you edit past that text, your edit wins: Kepler adds only the new words and never overwrites what you typed.

Kepler treats short, whole utterances as commands rather than dictation: saying "send", "cancel", or "clear" acts on the prompt instead of adding text to it. Kepler dictates anything longer verbatim.

While a transcription is in flight, the microphone shows Transcribing…. If a streaming session falls behind, it pauses the microphone rather than dropping audio: Transcribing too slowly — pausing the mic. Wait, or pick a smaller model.

Teach dictation your words

Qwen3-ASR learns how you spell words it gets wrong. When you fix a single dictated word in the prompt before you send it, Kepler notes the fix. Kepler learns from a fix only after the prompt is sent and the agent has finished its turn. After you make the same fix in two sends, Kepler suggests the word, with a notice: A dictated word fix is ready to review. Review opens Settings. Dictation uses a suggested word only after you approve it.

Settings → Voice input lists the words in two groups:

  • Suggested words: Fixes you made in two sends. Dictation uses a word only after you approve it. Each shows what Qwen3-ASR heard, with Approve and Dismiss. A dismissed fix is never suggested again.
  • Learned words: approved words, marked In use, and words you added, marked Added by you. Each has a remove button. Type in Add a word and click Add to add one yourself. Forget learned words removes every learned, suggested, and added word on this device.

Kepler stores only the words, on this device only, and keeps them after you sign out. Learning runs only while Qwen3-ASR dictates.

Set up voice input in Settings

Settings → Voice input has four sections: Voice input, Transcription engine, When you stop talking, and Learned words.

Transcription engine offers three cards. Every engine runs on this computer, and nothing you say leaves it.

Engine What it is
Qwen3-ASR 1.7B The most accurate, especially with developer terms and mixed languages. Needs a recent graphics card
Qwen3-ASR 0.6B Better than Whisper with technical terms, and lighter on memory, for graphics cards with less memory
Whisper Works on any machine, including without a compatible graphics card. Less accurate with technical terms

Kepler checks the graphics card and marks the Qwen3-ASR size it suits as Recommended. A device that never chose an engine starts on that size. Without WebGPU, the page says Qwen3-ASR needs a graphics card with WebGPU, which is not available here. Whisper works on this computer., and Whisper is the engine.

To set up Qwen3-ASR, follow these steps:

  1. Open Settings → Voice input.
  2. Under Transcription engine, pick a Qwen3-ASR card.
  3. Click Download and set up, which shows the download size.

To set up Whisper, follow these steps:

  1. Under Transcription engine, pick Whisper.
  2. Under Set up voice input, click Get started. Kepler checks what this machine can run. This check runs once.
  3. Under Choose transcription quality, pick Fast, Balanced, Accurate, or Most accurate. Kepler marks the best fit for this machine as Suggested.
  4. Click Download and set up.

Setting up turns voice input on. Downloads keep going if you leave the page, and Kepler notifies you when the model is ready. Until a newly chosen model is downloaded, dictation keeps using a model that is already ready, and the page says so.

Once a model is installed, the page offers these settings:

Setting What it does
Use voice input in the chat prompt Turns the microphone on or off. Turn it off to pause voice input. The downloaded models stay on this computer
Dictation language With a Qwen3-ASR model ready: the language you speak. See Dictation language
Remove Qwen3-ASR models Deletes every downloaded Qwen3-ASR model and frees their storage. If Qwen3-ASR is what dictates, the microphone stops using it
Change quality With Whisper: reopens the quality picker, so you can switch to another Whisper model
Remove Whisper Deletes the Whisper model and frees its storage. If Whisper is what dictates, the chat microphone turns off
Technical details With Whisper: shows the model ID, its revision, and the backend and data type it runs on
When you stop talking Chooses what happens to what you say. See Choose what happens when you stop talking
