If you use Whisper and never chose an engine, on a computer that can run Qwen3-ASR, the page offers Try Qwen3-ASR for better results once, with Switch to Qwen3-ASR (size) and Not now.

Dictation language

With Qwen3-ASR, Dictation language is Auto-detect by default, or one of 30 languages, each listed by its own name. Choose one to always dictate in it. Auto-detect picks among your system's preferred languages and the app language, or among all languages when those name fewer than two. It also leans on the languages this device has reliably dictated in before. Whisper ignores this setting.

Model updates

When a newer version of a Qwen3-ASR model ships, a notice on this device says once that a newer version of the voice model is available, with Update. Settings → Voice input keeps offering Update (size), and says A newer version of this model is available. Dictation keeps using the installed one until you update.

Linux graphics cards

On Linux, Kepler turns WebGPU on so Qwen3-ASR can use the graphics card:

  • Under Wayland, WebGPU is on by default.
  • Under X11, start Kepler with --enable-linux-webgpu to turn it on.
  • Start Kepler with --disable-linux-webgpu to turn it off.

If Kepler's first window hangs or never draws with WebGPU on, Kepler turns WebGPU off for later launches and restarts. Without WebGPU, use Whisper.

For the full walkthrough of the setup sequence, see Settings.

Why the microphone is unavailable

Hover the microphone, and its tooltip explains why:

Tooltip What it means
Voice input is off. Turn it on in Settings. Not set up yet, or turned off. Clicking opens Settings
Voice input needs a one-time setup. Open Settings to finish it. Enabled, but no model is downloaded. Clicking opens Settings
Voice input requires a secure (https) connection Kepler is being used over plain HTTP, where the browser will not grant microphone access
Voice input is not supported in this environment This build or browser cannot run it
The selected voice model needs WebGPU, which is not available here. Open Settings to choose Whisper. A Qwen3-ASR model is selected, but this computer or browser has no WebGPU. Clicking opens Settings at Transcription engine
The graphics card is not responding, so voice input cannot start. Open Settings to check it again. The graphics card didn't answer. Clicking opens Settings at Transcription engine
Loading voice model… / Transcribing… Busy. The button is disabled for the moment

A microphone that is available but idle reads Start voice input; while recording, it reads Stop and transcribe. With Qwen3-ASR, the tooltip also names the engine, for example Start voice input · Qwen3-ASR 1.7B. You can always stop. If the surrounding prompt goes read-only mid-recording, the transcript still lands in the draft.

When voice input fails, Kepler names the cause, with Open Settings where Settings can fix it. For example:

  • A voice model is being installed. Try again when it finishes.
  • The voice model is no longer downloaded on this computer. Download it again in Settings.
  • The voice engine stopped responding. Try again; the model reloads on its own.
  • Voice input stopped unexpectedly.

Microphone permission on macOS

macOS asks for microphone access the first time you record, and Kepler prompts for it rather than failing silently.

If access is denied or restricted, Kepler explains what to do:

Kepler can't use the microphone because access is denied or restricted. Turn it on in System Settings → Privacy & Security → Microphone if your device policy allows it. You may need to restart Kepler for the change to take effect.

Open System Settings takes you straight to the Microphone pane. The guidance stays on screen if the deep link cannot open, so you keep the instructions.

Remote windows

Voice input works in a window attached to a remote environment. The agent runs on the remote machine; the microphone and the transcription stay local, on the computer in front of you.

Over a plain HTTP connection the microphone is unavailable, and its tooltip reads Voice input requires a secure (https) connection.

Voice settings belong to each computer:

  • A desktop window, local or connected to a remote, uses this computer's voice settings.
  • A Remote Access browser uses the settings of the Kepler it's connected to.
  • Neither the settings nor the models sync between devices.

Kepler caches downloaded models per browser storage context:

  • A remote Electron window reuses its model across reconnects and restarts, as long as you're returning to the same saved remote connection from the same desktop app. A different machine, a different profile, or a connection that falls back to a different port needs its own download. Until a Qwen3-ASR model is verified in that window, dictation there uses an installed Whisper model rather than failing or starting a download on its own.
  • Browser-based Remote Access caches the model per browser, device, and profile. A different browser, device, or a private window needs its own download. The same browser returning to the same address can reuse what it already has, unless that browser has since cleared its storage.

Learned words and the languages this device has dictated in stay in the same storage, on the device in front of you, and never go to the remote machine.

See Remote Environments.

feature: Agent Integrationscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true3755 words · ~17 min

Kepler runs the coding agent you already have. Connect Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Auggie, OpenCode, Grok Build, Pi, or Google Antigravity, or add your own custom agent.

Last updated: October 2026

Kepler runs the coding agent you already have. You sign in with your own agent account, and Kepler adds no markup on the agents you bring.

Kepler ships support for nine agents. You can also add any other agent that speaks the Agent Client Protocol (ACP), or whose command-line interface you want to run inside a task.

All of it lives in Settings → Agents.

The nine supported agents

Each agent gets its own section in Settings → Agents, in this order:

Agent Name in Settings How Kepler runs it Run modes Multiple accounts
Claude Code Claude Code Your installed claude CLI Chat or Terminal Yes
Codex Codex Your installed codex CLI Chat or Terminal Yes
GitHub Copilot GitHub Copilot Your installed copilot CLI in ACP mode (--acp) Chat or Terminal Yes
OpenCode OpenCode Your installed opencode CLI, v2, in ACP mode (acp) Chat or Terminal No
Pi Pi Your installed pi CLI, in its own terminal interface Terminal only No
Auggie Auggie Your installed auggie CLI in ACP mode (--acp) Chat or Terminal Yes
Google Antigravity Antigravity Your installed agy CLI in a terminal, or Google's separate Antigravity ACP server (agy_acp_server) for Chat Terminal (default) or Chat No
Cursor Cursor Your installed cursor-agent CLI in ACP mode (acp) Chat or Terminal No
Grok Build Grok Build Your installed grok CLI in ACP mode (agent stdio) Chat or Terminal Yes

Auggie is Augment's coding agent. It runs over the same ACP path as the rest, and its model and mode pickers work like any other agent's. It is also one of the three agents that report plan usage back to Kepler, alongside Claude Code and Codex. Auggie reports only its credit balance, with the credits your plan includes, rather than usage windows. See Agent Sessions for the plan usage meter. Kepler asks each agent's provider for plan usage while Check plan usage with each agent's provider is on, which it is by default, in Settings → Agents → Usage limits.

Grok Build is xAI's coding agent. It signs in through your browser, or you can supply an xAI API key. Its data lives under ~/.grok, which is what lets Kepler keep several accounts apart.

Google Antigravity runs two separate binaries. Terminal mode runs the agy CLI. Chat mode runs Google's Antigravity ACP server, which is a separate download with its own version. The agent's section in Settings → Agents lists both under Components: Antigravity CLI (Needed for Terminal mode.) and ACP server (Rich-text (ACP) mode. Installed separately from the Antigravity CLI.). Each has its own Install or Update to {version}. Either one alone is enough to use the agent in that mode. Sessions stay in Terminal until you pick Chat for Antigravity, because the ACP server signs in separately from the CLI.

Pi is terminal-only. Its CLI has no ACP mode, so Kepler runs its own interface in a terminal inside the task instead of leaving it out. Kepler does no auth probing for Pi: it uses its own sign-in (/login, or the provider API-key environment variables it reads directly). With Pi 1.x, Kepler loads its own extension for each launch, which gives the session the task's context and Kepler's workspace tools and leaves your APPEND_SYSTEM.md and ~/.pi/agent untouched. Other Pi versions get the task's context pasted in instead. A Pi session resumes by its own session ID, and Pi doesn't ask again whether to trust a worktree of a repository you already trust.

OpenCode needs version 2. Kepler reads and resumes only OpenCode 2's sessions and won't start a session on an OpenCode 1 binary: its card warns OpenCode {version} is not supported. A conversation you started under OpenCode 1 resumes from OpenCode 2's copy of it.

Inbox wake in Terminal mode. Kepler can wake an idle Terminal session to read its inbox for Claude Code, Pi, and OpenCode 2.1.1 or newer. Pi gets the wake through Kepler's per-launch extension and OpenCode through a plugin Kepler loads for each launch, and neither touches your own config. Kepler delivers the wake as a queued follow-up prompt. Other agents in Terminal mode can't use Kepler's workspace tools or inbox, and Kepler suggests Continue in Chat from the session menu instead.

Claude Code folder trust is read where the CLI records your answer, so a worktree inherits the trust you already gave its repository and a session doesn't stall on the trust prompt.

Codex now runs your own CLI. Earlier builds shipped a bundled codex-acp engine; Kepler resolves and spawns the codex you have installed, like every other agent, so its section reads Installed and it has a binary picker.

Every agent that offers both modes is configured the same way. Settings → Agents → your agent → New sessions start in picks between Chat and Terminal, per agent. To start one session the other way, use Start in in the composer's Agent settings. See Agent Sessions.

Two agents need a recent enough build to be driven over ACP:

Agent Minimum
GitHub Copilot CLI v1.0 or newer. Older builds reject --acp
Cursor CLI A build that exposes the acp command, added in early 2026

Kepler checks both before it starts a session and tells you to update rather than failing with a protocol error.

Install an agent

When an agent is not installed, its section reads Not installed and offers Install. Kepler runs the install command on your behalf and streams the output, so you can read a failure rather than guess at one. Cancel stops an install in progress (Install cancelled: {name}). Installs and updates run one at a time: start a second while one is running and it waits its turn. One that produces no result for 20 minutes stops on its own and says so in the output.

Kepler declares install methods per operating system, so the list you see depends on the machine you're on:

Agent macOS Linux Windows
Claude Code Native installer, Homebrew, npm (global) Native installer, npm (global) Native installer, winget, npm (global)
Codex Standalone installer, Homebrew, npm (global) Standalone installer, npm (global) npm (global)
GitHub Copilot Native installer, Homebrew, npm (global) Native installer, Homebrew, npm (global) winget, npm (global)
Cursor CLI Native installer, Homebrew Native installer Native installer
Auggie npm (global) npm (global) npm (global)
OpenCode Native installer, Homebrew, npm (global) Native installer, Homebrew, npm (global), pacman npm (global)
Grok Build Native installer Native installer Native installer
Pi npm (global) npm (global) npm (global)
Google Antigravity Native installer, plus the ACP server component Native installer, plus the ACP server component Native installer, plus the ACP server component

Every OpenCode method installs OpenCode 2. Windows offers only npm, because Scoop's and Chocolatey's opencode packages still install OpenCode 1.

If an agent offers no install method for your OS, Kepler says so and points you at the custom binary path instead.

Kepler doesn't close the install output pane automatically when the install finishes. Mid-run errors (a permissions failure, a partial install, a post-install warning) surface only in that pane, so read them before you dismiss it.

Binary detection

Kepler resolves each agent's binary the way your shell does: the first match on your PATH, so it agrees with which. Beyond PATH, it also sweeps your login shell's environment and well-known install directories. That's how Kepler finds an agent installed by nvm or Homebrew, even when you didn't launch Kepler from a terminal. Kepler reads your login shell again on Restart agent, Re-scan, or a retry, so an environment variable you add, change, or remove in your shell's startup files reaches the restarted agent without restarting Kepler.

Each agent's card in Settings → Agents shows what Kepler resolved. The version and Install sit in the card's title line; Binary, Components and Data folder sit under Program and data folder:

Control What it does
Version The installed version, or Not installed
Install Runs one of the install methods above. Shown when the agent is not installed
Binary The resolved absolute path, or Not found, with where it came from (PATH, Shell, or Common) and its version
Re-scan Re-detects this agent's binary. Use it after installing or removing one outside Kepler
Custom path… Pins an absolute path of your own. Kepler validates it and refuses a directory or a path that is not a file
Clear custom Drops the pinned path and goes back to automatic resolution. Shown only while a custom path is set
Installed versions A picker listing every install Kepler found, with an Auto row. Appears only when Kepler finds more than one
Data folder Overrides the agent's own data and config directory. Leave it empty for the agent default
Enabled Whether Kepler offers this agent when you start a session

If Kepler can't read an installed agent's version, its section shows a Version unknown badge rather than plain Installed: Kepler was unable to read {name}'s version. Features that depend on the version stay off, such as sending a new session's first message automatically. Reinstalling or updating {name} usually fixes this.

When an installed version is known to misbehave, the agent's card carries a warning note under its title line that names the version and what to do:

Agent Warning
OpenCode OpenCode {version} is not supported. Sessions with it don't start; install OpenCode 2 or pick an OpenCode 2 binary
Auggie Auggie {version} may not load your MCP servers in chat
Google Antigravity ACP server {version} can fail to start on Linux. Reinstall the ACP server under Program and data folder
Codex Codex {version} can show approval prompts that need no answer. Update Codex to 0.163.0 or newer

If a pinned path later disappears (for example, when an auto-updater cleans up an old version), Kepler falls back to automatic resolution instead of reporting the agent as missing.

Two buttons sit above the agent cards in Settings → Agents: Re-scan re-detects every agent at once, and Update all runs each installed agent's own updater.

Sign in to an agent

An installed agent shows Sign in until it has a credential, and Sign out once it does. Sign in opens a modal listing the methods that agent supports on this machine, plus any methods Kepler owns itself:

Agent How you sign in
Claude Code Browser sign-in. Kepler runs the sign-in for you and your browser opens; you paste a code back only if the browser callback cannot reach Kepler. More sign-in options adds Amazon Bedrock, Google Vertex AI, Microsoft Foundry, and LLM gateway
Codex Sign in with a ChatGPT account through your browser, or enter an API key. On a remote, ChatGPT (device code)
GitHub Copilot Sign in with GitHub: Kepler's own device flow. Open github.com/login/device and enter the one-time code
Cursor CLI Cursor's own browser sign-in, driven from the modal, or Paste an API key from the Cursor dashboard. On a remote, only the API key works
Auggie Sign in with browser, or Paste session token
OpenCode OpenCode account (Go or Console): sign in through your browser with a one-time code. OpenCode also resolves providers from its own config file and provider environment variables
Grok Build Browser sign-in, or Paste an API key for a deployment with no local browser
Google Antigravity Google OAuth, Google Cloud project, or Google Cloud project (SSO), driven from the modal. The ACP server used for Chat keeps its own sign-in, so Kepler checks whichever one the mode you picked uses
Pi Nothing to do in Kepler. Sign in inside the CLI itself, or set the provider environment variables it reads

Claude Code through a cloud provider or gateway

To run Claude Code through your cloud provider rather than a Claude account, click More sign-in options in the Sign in to Claude Code modal and pick a method:

Method What you enter
Amazon Bedrock A Bedrock API key (AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK) and the AWS region (AWS_REGION)
Google Vertex AI The project (ANTHROPIC_VERTEX_PROJECT_ID) and region (CLOUD_ML_REGION), and optionally a credentials file (GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS). Leave the file empty to use application default credentials
Microsoft Foundry The resource name only (ANTHROPIC_FOUNDRY_RESOURCE), and optionally an API key and your Opus, Sonnet, and Haiku deployment names. Without a key, it signs in with Microsoft Entra ID credentials on this machine
LLM gateway The gateway's URL (ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL) and the token it issues (ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN), plus any other NAME=value variables your gateway needs

The account then reads as the provider and its region, project, or resource, or as the gateway's host. Kepler shows no plan usage for it, since the plan belongs to your cloud provider. Saving one method clears the values stored for the others, and Sign out clears the provider. You can set a gateway's extra variables only on the computer running Kepler, not from a paired device.

OpenCode account

OpenCode account (Go or Console) signs OpenCode in to your OpenCode account:

  1. Click Sign in on OpenCode's card and pick OpenCode account (Go or Console).
  2. Click Open sign-in in browser and enter the One-time code the modal shows.
  3. Choose the workspace with your Go subscription.
  4. Select a Go model to use your plan.

The agent's card then shows OpenCode as signed in.

Browser sign-in, including on a remote

Claude Code and Codex both sign in through your browser, and neither drops you into a terminal to do it.

That holds on a remote environment too:

  • Claude Code on a remote offers Sign in with browser, which opens the sign-in page in your local browser and takes the code you paste back. The credential lands on the remote machine.
  • Codex on a remote offers ChatGPT (device code): Sign in with a one-time code in your browser. Works on any remote. Kepler shows the sign-in link and a One-time code; the remote CLI waits for you to finish and writes the token there. Some ChatGPT workspaces turn device sign-in off, and Kepler then points you at the alternatives.
  • Cursor CLI on a remote offers only Paste an API key, since its browser sign-in can't complete there.

When a sign-in runs in a terminal on a remote and the browser page won't load after you sign in, paste that page's address into the box under the terminal (Browser page won't load after you sign in? Paste its address here.) and click Send. Kepler requests the address on the remote machine, and only when the sign-in itself printed it. You then finish in the terminal.

Import local Codex login is the other remote route, in the desktop app: it copies this machine's ~/.codex/auth.json to the remote target, and asks you to confirm on this machine before it reads the file. Treat this import as handing over a credential: anyone with access to that remote machine can send Codex requests on your account until you sign out.

A Codex API key works locally and on a remote: Kepler collects the key in the modal and hands it to Codex for that account. Sign out on Codex signs the CLI itself out, and Kepler follows a sign-in you do in the Codex CLI outside Kepler.

Auggie's two methods

Auggie supports two sign-in methods:

Method What it does Where it works
Sign in with browser Runs Auggie's sign-in and finishes it in your browser. Auggie stores the credential under ~/.augment Local, and over SSH: the remote variant opens the page in your local browser and takes a pasted JSON response
Paste session token You paste the JSON from auggie token print into Kepler. Kepler stores it and supplies it on every session Anywhere, including CI-style setups with no browser

If you run Auggie's browser sign-in on this machine yourself, Kepler detects it and you can skip the token paste.

Multiple accounts of one agent

Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Auggie, and Grok Build support more than one signed-in account. Settings → Agents → your agent → Accounts → Add account adds one; each account keeps its own credentials and history.

Give each added account its own colour from the swatch at the left of its row: Rose, Amber, Lime, Cyan, Blue, or Violet. The agent icon is tinted with it on session tabs, the task rail, and the terminal status bar, so sessions on different accounts are easy to tell apart. The default account stays uncolored. Accounts also appear on the agent's card in Settings.

For Auggie, you can only add a second account with Paste session token. Its browser sign-in writes to one fixed file, so it would sign the second account into the first account's identity.

Run modes

Most agents run two ways, and each agent keeps its own default. To pick the default for an agent, open Settings → Agents, expand the agent, and set New sessions start in:

Mode What you get
Chat {agent} built into Kepler, with replies, diffs and plans you can read, review and search. Kepler drives the agent over ACP and renders the conversation itself. This is the default
Terminal {agent}'s own command-line app, with its commands, shortcuts and settings, just as you use it today. It runs in an embedded terminal inside the task

To start one session in the other mode without changing the default, set Start in in the composer's Agent settings before you send the first message. An agent that offers only one mode shows no switch at all. See Agent Sessions.

Detecting sessions started outside Kepler

Settings → Integrations → Your terminal → Show sessions started outside Kepler is a separate, cross-agent setting, on out of the box. It installs GitKraken hooks so sessions you start in your own terminal show up in Kepler, and it covers every agent Kepler can track that way: Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, GitHub Copilot, OpenCode, Auggie, Grok Build, and Pi. Google Antigravity isn't tracked this way. See Agent Sessions.

Kepler also follows its own Terminal sessions through those hooks. If a live terminal session keeps reporting status but its hook events stop arriving, Kepler warns Agent sessions aren't reporting to Kepler, because status and notifications may be out of date. Repair reinstalls the reporting configuration. Restart any session that still doesn't report afterwards. A window that only views another computer's sessions gets the warning without Repair, because you repair reporting on the computer running the sessions.

Programs that report their own status

Some terminal programs report their own status to Kepler (through the OSC 7501 escape sequence). When an agent's terminal program does this, Kepler shows the session as working, blocked, or done from what the program reports, without hooks. If hook events also arrive, the hooks take precedence. See Agent Sessions.

Your default agent

Settings → Tasks → New tasks → Default agent sets which agent is preselected when you start work, and supplies the model, mode, and thinking effort a new session starts with.

Kepler saves agent options per account. Picking a different account of the same agent brings up that account's own saved model and options, so a heavier model on your work account doesn't follow you onto your personal one.

Only agents that are installed and Enabled appear in the picker. If your saved default is no longer installed, Kepler says so and asks you to pick another.

An Action can override all of this. See Actions for how an Action pins a provider, account, model, mode, and options together, and which choice wins when they disagree.

Custom agents

If your agent isn't one of the nine, you can add it by its command. To add one, open Settings → Agents → Custom agents, click New custom agent, and fill in these fields:

Field What it holds
Name What the agent is called in Kepler's pickers
Runs in Chat: Kepler's chat. Needs a program that speaks ACP (the Agent Client Protocol). Terminal: The command's own app, in a terminal.
Command The binary to run: an absolute path, or a name Kepler resolves on your PATH
Args (one per line) Arguments for the command. For a Chat agent, the arguments that put it into ACP mode, for example --acp
Environment variables (KEY=value, one per line) Variables merged into the agent's environment on every session

Kepler treats environment values as secrets: it encrypts them at rest, hides them after you save, and never sends them to any client. When you edit a custom agent, leaving a value empty (KEY=) keeps the stored secret, and typing a new value replaces it.

Custom agents appear alongside the built-in agents everywhere you can choose an agent. A custom agent that runs in Terminal works the same way: Kepler works out what terminal mode can offer from the command's own flags rather than from a list of agent names, so your own agent degrades exactly the way a built-in one does.

MCP servers

Kepler lists the MCP servers each agent loads, and lets you add, remove, sign in to, and turn off servers or single tools, per repository from the composer's Agent settings pill or as defaults in Settings → Agents → MCP servers. See MCP Servers.

What Kepler tells the agent about itself

Agent CLIs report Kepler as the client name, so your sessions show up as Kepler in places like your Anthropic dashboard rather than as an unlabelled non-interactive run.

This labeling applies to the sessions Kepler drives over ACP. Terminal mode deliberately leaves the label alone, because overriding it there would break key handling in the embedded terminal.

Sessions

Connecting an agent is the setup; running one is a session inside a task. Agent Sessions covers sessions: starting, resuming, queuing prompts, and reviewing what the agent produced.

A task holds resources and does not require a worktree, or even a repository. See Tasks and Resources.

Not every conversation starts in Kepler, either. See Agent Sessions.

feature: MCP Serverscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true1643 words · ~7 min

See the MCP servers each agent loads, turn a server or a single tool on or off per repository or for every repository, add and remove servers, and sign in to the ones that need it, from inside Kepler.

Last updated: October 2026

Your coding agents load Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers from their own config files. Kepler reads those files and lists exactly the servers each agent will load, so you can see them, turn a server or one of its tools off where you don't want it, add or remove a server, and sign in to a server that needs it. You don't have to edit the agent's config by hand.

You manage them in three places:

  • Per repository, from the composer's Agent settings pill
  • Per repository, in Settings → Repositories → repository → MCP servers
  • As global defaults, in Settings → Agents → MCP servers

Requirements and limits

  • Works for Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, OpenCode, Auggie, and Grok Build. Pi, Antigravity, and custom agents don't expose their MCP servers to Kepler; their manager reads This agent does not expose its MCP servers to Kepler.
  • The agent must be installed. Settings lists only the agents that answer.
  • Kepler shows the servers the agent's config files define. Servers that don't live on disk, such as claude.ai connectors, aren't listed.

Where you manage them

Where What a change applies to
The composer's Agent settings pill → MCP servers → Manage This repository, for that agent
Settings → Repositories → repository → MCP servers This repository, for the agent you pick
Settings → Agents → MCP servers Every repository, as the default each one inherits

The MCP servers dialog in the composer includes Open repo settings, which opens the same list in Settings → Repositories.

Every place shows the same manager. It groups servers by where they're defined:

Group Where the server comes from
Added by you Your own config for the agent, used in every repository
In this repo, shared with your team Checked into this repository
In this repo, just you This repository only, not shared
From plugins Installed by a plugin
Set by your organization Managed by your organization

Each row is a server. The switch on a row turns the server on or off, and clicking the row opens the server's own page. A row can carry these badges:

  • Sign in needed: the server needs authentication before the agent can use it.
  • Every repo: a change to the server reaches every repository.
  • Locked: Kepler cannot edit this entry in the agent's config file, such as a server your organization manages.

Per repository

In a session or in the New task composer, open the Agent settings pill. Its MCP servers row reads {enabled} of {total} enabled; Manage opens the MCP servers dialog. Its subtitle reads {agent} in {repo}, the agent and the repository it applies to. When you open a server's page, All servers takes you back to the list.

A change here applies to that repository only. Worktrees of one checkout share the setting, so a server you turn off in a repository stays off in every task's worktree of it.

If a server is off in Settings and you haven't turned it on here, it says Off for every repo in Settings. Turn it on to use it here., with Open Settings to go to the global default. Turning it on in the dialog turns it on for this repository only.

Turn off a single tool

A server's page lists its Tools under the Let {agent} use this server switch. Kepler connects to the server to ask for its tools, so the list can take a moment (Asking the server for its tools…). If the server needs authentication first, the list reads Sign in to this server to see its tools.

To turn off one tool, open the server's page and turn off the tool's switch. Turn a tool off to keep {agent} from calling it.

Turning off a single tool works for Claude Code, Codex, and Grok Build. For the other agents, turn the whole server off instead.

Global defaults

Settings → Agents → MCP servers is the same manager with no repository. Its description reads Give your agents extra tools, like a database, a browser or your issue tracker. Use the Agent picker at the top to choose the agent, and the account when that agent has more than one.

Keep the GitKraken MCP server installed in every agent is on by default. Gives every command-line agent Git tools and your issues and pull requests through GitKraken. Turn off to manage it yourself.

The two levels combine like this:

  • A repository uses the global value until you change that server in its own dialog.
  • A repository's choice wins over the global one, in either direction: a repository can turn off a server that's on globally, or turn on a server that's off globally.
  • Kepler stores a choice only while it differs from the level beneath it, so switching a server back to match the global default hands control back to the default.

Plugin servers show in Settings but can't be changed there. Neither can Cursor servers: Cursor keeps its on and off state per project, so switch a Cursor server from a repository's dialog instead.

Adding and removing servers

To add a server, click Add server: beside the section's heading in Settings, or at the foot of the list in the MCP servers dialog. The Add MCP server ({agent}) page opens with these fields:

Field What it takes
Name Letters, numbers, ., _, or -, up to 64 characters, unique where it's saved
Applies to Every repo, This repo only, or This repo, shared with your team. The choices depend on the agent and on whether you're in a repository, and the field shows only when there's more than one, as in a repository's MCP servers section
Runs as Command for a local server, or URL or URL (SSE) for a remote one
Command and Arguments For a command server, for example npx -y @some/server
URL For a remote server, an http:// or https:// address
Environment variables / Headers Key and value pairs. Values stay hidden until you choose Show value

Kepler writes the new server into the agent's own config file, in the format that agent uses, so the agent's CLI sees it too.

To remove a server, open the server's page and click Remove server. Kepler deletes the server from the agent's config. You can't undo this. Plugin and managed servers can't be removed from Kepler. A GitHub Copilot server defined in a repository can't be removed from Kepler either; only Copilot servers in your own config can.

Copy a server to other agents

To use a server you already set up with another agent or account, copy it instead of adding it again. Kepler copies the server's full definition, including its environment variables and headers.

  1. Open the server's page.
  2. Click Also add to….
  3. Select each agent, or each account of an agent, that should get the server.
  4. Click Add to N destinations.

Kepler adds the server to each destination in turn. If one copy fails, the others still go ahead, and the failed destination stays selected with its error.

Servers from plugins can't be copied, so their page doesn't show Also add to….

Signing in

For a server that needs authentication, its page has a Sign-in section. It offers Sign in, or Re-authenticate once you're signed in, and Clear authentication to sign it out. Kepler runs the agent's own CLI to do it, under the same account the session uses, so the credentials end up where the agent looks for them. Sign-in is available for Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, and Cursor servers.

In a local window, Open sign-in page opens your browser and Kepler finishes when you're done (Finish signing in in your browser). In a window connected to a remote environment, sign in on that page, then paste the URL you land on into Redirect URL.

When changes apply

Where the change lands When it takes effect
A Claude Code Chat session Straight away. Kepler reconnects the session with the new servers and keeps the conversation and your model and mode picks
A terminal session, or any other agent's session On restart. The Agent settings pill and the dialog show {count} MCP changes apply when the agent restarts. with Restart agent
The New task composer The session you start comes up with that state

Turning a server or tool off doesn't edit the agent's config for Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, or GitHub Copilot: Kepler keeps the choice itself and passes it to the agent when it launches, so the same agent run from your own terminal is unaffected. The one exception is turning on a Claude Code server that Claude's own config lists as disabled, which Kepler can only do by editing that list. For Cursor and Grok Build, Kepler writes the change into the agent's own files, which their CLIs also read. Auggie servers are listed but have no switch: Auggie offers no way to turn a server off, so edit its config to stop using one. Adding and removing a server always edits the config file.

Each agent account keeps its own MCP setup. When Kepler launches a Claude Code account other than the default one, it first copies in the default account's servers.

Related

  • Settings: Settings → Agents, including the MCP servers section.
  • Agent Integrations: the agents Kepler runs and how to set them up.
  • Agent Sessions: the composer's Agent settings pill, and restarting a session.
feature: Issue Tracker Integrationscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true3103 words · ~14 min

Connect your issue trackers to Kepler, cloud or self-hosted, so the issues you're working on land in one list, with everything an agent needs already attached.

Last updated: October 2026

Connect an issue tracker and the issues you're working on show up in the Kepler interface, alongside your pull requests and the tasks you already have running. Starting work on one becomes picking a row rather than describing the work from scratch.

Manage providers in Settings → Integrations, in the Issues and pull requests section.

Trackers Kepler can read issues from

Ten of Kepler's twelve providers return issues:

Provider Also returns pull requests
GitHub Yes
GitHub Enterprise Yes
GitLab Yes
GitLab Self-Hosted Yes
Azure DevOps Yes
Azure DevOps Server Yes
Jira No
Jira Server / Data Center No
Linear No
Trello No

Bitbucket and Bitbucket Data Center are the other two. They return pull requests only. See Pull Request Integrations.

Self-hosted instances are first-class: GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, and Jira Server / Data Center read issues the same way their cloud counterparts do, and two servers of the same kind stay separate even when their issue keys overlap. Which fields come back depends on your server's version, and your machine needs to be able to reach the server. You connect a self-hosted instance inside Kepler with its address and a personal access token. See Connect a self-hosted tracker.

Kepler trusts the certificates installed in your operating system's certificate store, so a server signed by your company's own certificate authority, or a network that re-signs HTTPS traffic, works without extra setup.

Connect a tracker

Connecting a cloud tracker (GitHub, GitLab, Azure DevOps, Jira, Linear, or Trello) takes four steps:

  1. Open Settings → Integrations.
  2. Find the provider in Issues and pull requests and click Connect.
  3. Authorize Kepler in the browser window that opens.
  4. Return to Kepler, where the provider shows a Connected badge in Issues and pull requests.

Your GitKraken account holds integrations, not this copy of Kepler, so a provider you connect here also becomes available to the gk command-line interface (CLI) and GitKraken Desktop. You need to sign in to a GitKraken account to manage them; Kepler prompts you if you are not signed in.

Connect hands the authorization for a cloud tracker to GitKraken's website. Kepler opens /connect there in your system browser, names the provider, and includes a redirect back into Kepler. When you return, Kepler refetches your providers instead of reusing its cached list.

While you authorize, Kepler keeps a Connect {provider} in your browser dialog open, with the provider's name in place of {provider}. If your browser didn't open, copy the Connection link from the dialog and open it in a browser yourself. Try again makes a fresh link, since each link works once. The dialog closes by itself as soon as the new connection appears, and Kepler checks for it again whenever you come back to a Kepler window. If Kepler can't start the connection, the dialog reads Unable to connect {provider} and says why.

Connect a self-hosted tracker

GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, and Jira Server / Data Center connect inside Kepler with a personal access token rather than through the browser:

  1. Open Settings → Integrations.
  2. Find the provider in Issues and pull requests and click Connect. The Connect {name} dialog opens.
  3. In Server URL, enter the server's address, including the port and installation path when your server uses them.
  4. In Personal access token, paste a token with access to the issues you need.
  5. Click Save connection.

Kepler checks the token against the server from your machine, keeps it in a protected credential store on this device, and syncs the connection to your GitKraken account. The dialog reports whether the server accepted the token, whether Kepler saved it without being able to reach the server or verify its certificate, or whether the server refused it (The server refused this token. Check its value and permissions.). Until the upload to your account lands, the provider's row shows Cloud sync pending. Reconnect on a self-hosted provider opens the same dialog, so you can replace an expired or rejected token there. See Pull Request Integrations for every message the dialog shows.

Three controls sit on a connected provider's row:

Control What it does
Reconnect Re-runs authorization. Use it when a sign-in has expired
Disconnect Removes the provider's primary connection (see below)
Refresh At the top of the section, re-checks every provider

A provider's row can also carry a warning:

Warning What it means What to do
Sign-in expired The provider's sign-in has expired Kepler tries to refresh the token on its own; Reconnect is the manual fix
Cannot connect to server Kepler couldn't reach the provider's server. The integration is still connected Nothing to reconnect. Check that the server is up and reachable from your network or VPN; the warning clears once it answers
Unexpected response The provider answered with data Kepler couldn't read, so some of its items may be missing. The integration is still connected Reconnecting won't fix it. If it persists, check for a proxy or gateway between Kepler and the provider
An organization named under the row One organization refuses Kepler's access, so its items are left out while everything else still loads. GitHub organizations that restrict OAuth app access, for example, are named with a Request access on GitHub link Follow the row's own advice, which names who can lift the block

You don't have to open Settings to find a failing provider. Todo, the Add resources lists, and a link preview name it too, with the one fix that applies: Reconnect, Reconnect in settings for a self-hosted provider, Open settings, or Retry. See Pull Request Integrations for each warning.

Choose what Kepler brings in

Each connected integration carries a visibility line under its row in Settings → Integrations. The line reads Everything visible, or {count} hidden with the number of items you've hidden. Trello has no visibility line.

To hide part of an integration:

  1. Click Choose what's visible… on the provider's row. The Choose what {name} brings in dialog opens.
  2. Clear the checkbox of each item to hide. Type in Filter… to narrow a long list.
  3. Click Save.

What the dialog lists depends on the provider:

Provider Lists
GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Bitbucket, Bitbucket Data Center Organizations and Repositories
Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server Organizations, Repositories, and Projects
Jira, Jira Server / Data Center, Linear Projects

A ticked item is visible. Hiding an organization also hides its repositories, which show Hidden with its organization and can't be ticked on their own. A hidden project leaves the issue lists but stays in the project pickers, and a project with no issue returned yet may be missing from the list.

Hidden items are left out of Todo, the issue and pull request lists, and the pickers. Tasks and branches already linked to a hidden item keep resolving. Kepler stores the choice on your GitKraken account, so it follows you to other machines where you sign in.

More than one account of the same provider

You can connect several accounts of the same provider: two GitHub accounts, a work Jira site and a personal one. When a provider has two or more, an Accounts list appears under its row with one entry per account.

Each entry carries two independent controls:

Control What it changes How far it reaches
Read from this account Which account Kepler reads this provider's issues and pull requests from Kepler only, and non-destructive
Set as primary / Primary The provider's primary account Everywhere: other Kepler windows, the gk CLI, and GitKraken Desktop

Browsing a secondary account does not change your primary. Selecting Read from this account on a second account switches what Kepler shows you and leaves the primary alone. That's why Read from this account is a radio control rather than a button. The primary is the default read account, so a provider with no override reads through it.

Switching either one clears that provider's saved filters (an organization or project from the old account need not exist on the new one) and re-fetches the list.

Reconnect keeps your choices. When one account's sign-in fails, Reconnect renews that account's connection without disconnecting it first. Once the same account comes back, its new connection takes the old one's place, as primary or as the read account if it was either, and Kepler removes the old one. Another account of the provider never becomes primary by accident.

The read account also authenticates git. Starting work on an issue in a repository you have never cloned makes Kepler clone that repository, using the same connection it reads through. See Pull Request Integrations for which hosts this covers and what happens on the hosts it does not.

Linear connections stay separate by workspace. Each Linear account's entry shows Workspace: {workspace}, the Linear workspace that connection was granted. Two connections to different workspaces are separate accounts, so Kepler never reads one workspace's issues through another workspace's connection.

Disconnect a provider

Click Disconnect on the provider's row in Settings → Integrations and confirm.

Disconnecting reaches further than Kepler: it removes the connection from your GitKraken account, so the gk CLI and GitKraken Desktop lose it too. What happens to the provider depends on whether you have another account connected:

  • With another account connected, Kepler promotes it to primary and the provider stays connected under it. Nothing else changes.
  • With no other account connected, the provider is removed entirely. Kepler drops its saved filters and its rows from your lists, so nothing lingers behind pointing at a provider you no longer have.

You can reconnect at any time.

Jira, Linear, and Trello have no pull requests

Kepler keeps two separate lists of what each provider can return: which providers can list issues, and which can list pull requests. Jira (Cloud, Server, and Data Center), Linear, and Trello are on the first list only.

Two consequences you will notice:

  • Kepler never asks them for pull requests. Kepler excludes them from the pull-request read instead of querying and ignoring them.
  • The interface hides the dead filters. A provider filter only offers providers that can return results for what you are looking at, so Jira never appears as a pull-request filter. In the list, Kepler builds facets from the rows actually loaded, so a facet with nothing to offer never appears.

The same rule runs the other way for Bitbucket and Bitbucket Data Center, which have no issues.

What Kepler reads from an issue

When an issue becomes a task, Kepler attaches what it read, so the agent starts with that context instead of asking for it. Kepler pulls these fields from the issue:

Field Notes
Identifier The issue key or number: GK-1234 for Jira, DRE-2 for Linear, a number for GitHub, GitLab, and Azure DevOps
Title
Description The issue body. When an Azure DevOps work item keeps its content in another field, such as Repro Steps on a Bug, Kepler reads that field and labels the description with its name
Issue type The provider's own vocabulary: a Jira issue type, an Azure DevOps work item type
Author When the provider reports one
Assignees
Status The issue's current state, in the provider's own words (a Jira To Verify rather than a generic open), when the provider reports one
Last updated When the issue was last updated
Labels The provider's own labels, shown as badges in issue previews. Kepler distinguishes "this issue has no labels" from "this provider cannot report labels"
Project or board Jira projects, Linear teams, Azure DevOps areas. Git hosts hang issues off a repository instead
Iteration or sprint Azure DevOps iterations and Jira sprints. A Jira issue carried across sprints keeps them all
Repository Name, owner, and host, for the git hosts
URL Used by the open control, which names the provider: Open on GitHub, Open on Jira, and so on

Kepler does not read an issue's priority field, due date, milestone, Trello checklist, or Linear cycle. An agent can still find these by opening the issue's URL itself, but Kepler does not deliver them as attached context. Todo's Priority grouping and filter come from labels instead: a label such as P1, priority: high, blocker, or critical sets the level, and the most urgent label wins. Jira and Linear keep priority in a field Kepler doesn't read, so their issues show as Unprioritised unless a label says otherwise. See Arranging Your Work.

Kepler converts descriptions to Markdown when the provider stores them in another format: Jira's wiki markup and Azure DevOps work items' HTML. Headings, code blocks, and tables then read the same in Kepler's previews and in the agent's context. Images embedded in an issue's description, including Jira and Azure DevOps attachments, display in Kepler's issue previews. On a self-hosted server (GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, or Jira Server / Data Center) they stay blocked and don't display, though the server's member avatars do. The agent receives the description as text: Kepler does not pass the attachments or images themselves.

Comments

When you open an issue in a reader, either the task panel's side pane or the issue's pane on the task page, a Comments section under the summary shows its discussion. Kepler reads comments for GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Jira, and Jira Server / Data Center, through the same connection it reads the issue with. For other providers the section says comments for that provider aren't available in Kepler yet.

Kepler reads up to the newest 100 comments, shows the latest ones with the newest at the bottom, and offers Show earlier comments for the rest it read. Refresh comments reads the discussion again. Each comment shows its author, when it was posted, and its body as Markdown. Comments are for you to read: Kepler shows them in the reader but doesn't attach them to the task or hand them to the agent.

Which issues Kepler lists

Kepler lists open work. An issue that's finished or cancelled in its tracker (a Linear issue in Done or Canceled, for example) drops out of Todo and the issue pickers. An issue already attached to a task keeps showing on that task with its real state, finished or not.

Todo shows the issues related to you on each connected integration, across every organization you belong to. Providers answer different questions about you, so what counts as related differs:

Provider Issues Todo lists
GitHub, GitHub Enterprise Yours, assigned to you, or mentioning you
GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server Yours or assigned to you
Jira, Jira Server / Data Center, Linear, Trello Assigned to you

An issue that's there only because you were mentioned is marked Mentioned, so you can tell it apart from work that's yours. Hover the Related to me chip in Todo's header to see the rule for each integration you have connected.

To browse beyond that, use All visible in the issue list of Add resources. For GitHub and Azure DevOps it reads every issue across your organizations, including repositories you can read outside them, such as ones you're an outside collaborator on, and it loads more slowly. Jira and Linear show only your issues there until you pick a project or a team:

  • Jira: when the issue list shows Jira alone, a project picker appears, starting with All projects. Pick a project to see every open issue in it. Kepler remembers the pick for each Jira connection.
  • Linear: when the issue list shows Linear alone, a team picker appears. Pick a team to see every open issue in it, whoever it's assigned to.

Where your issues turn up

Your issues turn up in three places:

Surface What it gives you
The Kepler interface The Todo segment lists your issues across every connected tracker, grouped and filtered how you like
Actions The Action button on a row hands the issue to an agent with its context attached. Firing an Action on an untracked issue is also how it becomes a task
Tasks and Resources An issue is a resource on a task, so you can attach one to work that already exists

The Issue type, Label, Project, and Status filters carry your provider's own vocabulary rather than a Kepler translation of it. Azure DevOps and Jira issues also filter by Iteration and Sprint, each named the way its tracker names it.

Kepler recognizes an issue from its URL when you paste or attach one, including these work item URLs:

  • GitLab work items, at /-/work_items/<number> in a project. Kepler stores them as issues of that project. A group-level work item belongs to no repository, so it stays a plain URL.
  • Azure DevOps work items, at /<organization>/<project>/_workitems/edit/<number> or at the organization level, /<organization>/_workitems/edit/<number>.

Search by key or link. In the issue list of Add resources, type a Jira or Linear issue key, such as OPS-42, or paste a Jira Cloud, Jira Server / Data Center, Linear, or Azure DevOps work item link. Kepler looks that issue up directly from its tracker, whatever its state, rather than only filtering the rows already loaded. A Jira Server / Data Center link resolves when it points at an installation you have connected.

Pick from the list with the keyboard. The issue list in Add resources has a preview pane and keyboard picking: arrows move between rows, Space selects, and Enter or a double-click adds. See Picking issues and pull requests.

Paste a link on the Links tab. An issue URL pasted on the Links tab of Add resources is looked up and added as that issue, with Add as plain link to keep it a plain link. See Pasting an issue link.

Paste a link into a prompt. Paste an issue or pull request URL into a session's or a new task's composer and it becomes a link chip, which Kepler attaches to the task when you send. See Sending a prompt.

feature: Pull Request Integrationscontent_type: how-tostatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true3548 words · ~16 min3 TODO

Connect your Git hosts to Kepler, cloud or self-hosted, so the pull requests you authored and the ones waiting on your review land in one list, already matched to the worktrees they belong to.

Last updated: October 2026

Connect a Git host and the pull requests you authored, plus the ones waiting on your review, show up in the Kepler interface. Kepler also matches them to the worktrees you already have, so a branch you are working on carries its pull request wherever you see it.

Manage providers in Settings → Integrations, in the Issues and pull requests section.

Each connected Git host carries a visibility line under its row, which reads Everything visible or how many items you've hidden. Click Choose what's visible… to leave organizations or repositories out of Todo, the lists, and the pickers. See Choose what Kepler brings in.

Hosts Kepler can read pull requests from

Eight of Kepler's twelve providers return pull requests:

Provider Also returns issues
GitHub Yes
GitHub Enterprise Yes
GitLab Yes
GitLab Self-Hosted Yes
Azure DevOps Yes
Azure DevOps Server Yes
Bitbucket No
Bitbucket Data Center No

Jira, Jira Server / Data Center, Linear, and Trello are the other four. They return issues only. See Issue Tracker Integrations.

Self-hosted instances are supported. GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, and Bitbucket Data Center read pull requests and merge requests the same way their cloud counterparts do, and two servers of the same kind stay separate; which fields come back depends on your server's version. You connect a self-hosted instance inside Kepler with its address and a personal access token, and Kepler checks the token against the server from your machine (see Connect a Git host). The instance must be reachable both from your machine and from gitkraken.dev, so a server that's only reachable inside your network or VPN can't be connected.

verifyv0.15 checks the personal access token from this machine; confirm with engineering whether syncing it to the account still needs gitkraken.dev to reach the server

Kepler trusts the certificates in your operating system's certificate store, so a server signed by your company's own certificate authority works without extra setup.

GitLab merge requests appear as pull requests throughout Kepler. There is one list, not two.

Connect a Git host

Connecting a cloud Git host (GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, or Azure DevOps) takes four steps:

  1. Open Settings → Integrations.
  2. Find the provider in Issues and pull requests and click Connect.
  3. Authorize Kepler in the browser window that opens.
  4. Return to Kepler, where the provider shows a Connected badge in Issues and pull requests.

Your GitKraken account holds integrations, not this copy of Kepler, so a provider you connect here also becomes available to the gk command-line interface (CLI) and GitKraken Desktop. You need to sign in to a GitKraken account to manage them.

Connect hands the authorization for a cloud host to GitKraken's website. Kepler opens /connect there in your system browser, names the provider, and includes a redirect back into Kepler. When you return, Kepler refetches your providers instead of reusing its cached list.

While you authorize, Kepler keeps a Connect {provider} in your browser dialog open, with the provider's name in place of {provider}. If your browser didn't open, copy the Connection link from the dialog and open it in a browser yourself. Try again makes a fresh link, since each link works once. The dialog closes by itself as soon as the new connection appears, and Kepler checks for it again whenever you come back to a Kepler window. If Kepler can't start the connection, the dialog reads Unable to connect {provider} and says why. Reconnect runs through the same dialog.

verifythe browser-side steps and the scopes each cloud provider asks for live on GitKraken's website (see help.gitkraken.com/gk-dev/gk-dev-integrations), outside the Kepler repo. Confirm the live /connect steps and provider scopes for GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, and Azure DevOps with the web team. Self-hosted providers no longer use this flow at v0.15 (src/ui/components/settings/SelfHostedProviderDialog.tsx).

Connect a self-hosted instance

GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, and Bitbucket Data Center connect inside Kepler with a personal access token rather than through the browser:

  1. Open Settings → Integrations.
  2. Find the provider in Issues and pull requests and click Connect. The Connect {name} dialog opens.
  3. In Server URL, enter the server's address, including the port and installation path when your server uses them.
  4. In Personal access token, paste a token with access to the repositories you need.
  5. Click Save connection.

Kepler accepts only an http or https address without credentials, a query, or a fragment. It checks the token against the server from your machine, keeps it in a protected credential store on this device, and syncs the connection to your GitKraken account. The dialog reports how the check went:

Message What it means
Credentials verified with the server. The server accepted the token
Credentials saved. Unable to verify the server certificate. Check its TLS configuration. Kepler saved the token but couldn't verify the server's certificate
Credentials saved without verification. Unable to reach the server. Kepler saved the token without checking it
The server refused this token. Check its value and permissions. Fix the token and save again

Until the upload to your account lands, the provider's row shows Cloud sync pending, and you can't make that connection the primary. If Kepler can't reach GitKraken, it retries when you refresh your providers. Reconnect on a self-hosted provider opens the same dialog, so you can replace an expired or rejected token there.

Three controls sit on a connected provider's row:

Control What it does
Reconnect Re-runs authorization. Use it when a sign-in has expired
Disconnect Removes the provider (see below)
Refresh At the top of the section, re-checks every provider

A row marked Sign-in expired needs its token refreshed. Kepler tries to do that itself; Reconnect is the manual fix. A row marked Cannot connect to server is different: the integration is still connected, but Kepler couldn't reach the server, so check that it's up and reachable from your network or VPN rather than reconnecting. When one organization refuses Kepler's access, such as a GitHub organization that restricts OAuth apps, Kepler names it under the row and leaves only its items out. See Issue Tracker Integrations for the warnings in full.

Cloning a repository you don't have

Starting work on a pull request whose repository you have never cloned makes Kepler clone that repository first, into your Default Repositories Folder. That clone authenticates with the provider token your connected GitKraken account already holds, the same connection the pull request itself came through. As a result, a private repository clones without you having to set up a git credential helper. Kepler authenticates the clone differently depending on the host:

Host How the clone authenticates
GitHub, GitHub Enterprise Your connected account's token
GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted Your connected account's token
Bitbucket, Bitbucket Data Center, Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server Falls through to your own git credentials

For an enterprise or self-managed host, Kepler matches the clone's host against the domain stored on your connection, so the repository clones through the account connected to that instance rather than through a cloud account of the same family. When a provider has more than one account, the clone follows the same choice your reads follow: Read from this account where you set one, otherwise the primary.

Kepler hands the token to that one git process and nothing more. Kepler does not write it into the clone's .git/config, does not add it to the remote's URL, and never exposes it to the interface.

Where Kepler holds no usable token for the host, the clone falls back to your own git credential helper. Kepler never lets git open an interactive prompt, so when no credential helper is available, the clone fails with a message that names the host and tells you what to do: git has no saved credentials for this remote and Kepler cannot prompt for them. Sign in with a git credential helper (e.g. gh auth login, or the macOS keychain helper) or use an SSH url, then try again.

More than one account of the same provider

You can connect several accounts of the same provider. When a provider has two or more, an Accounts list appears under its row with one entry per account.

Each entry carries two independent controls:

Control What it changes How far it reaches
Read from this account Which account Kepler reads this provider's pull requests and issues from Kepler only, and non-destructive
Set as primary / Primary The provider's primary account Everywhere: other Kepler windows, the gk CLI, and GitKraken Desktop

Browsing a secondary account does not change your primary. Selecting Read from this account on a second account switches what Kepler shows you and leaves the primary alone. The primary is the default read account, so a provider with no override reads through it.

Switching either one clears that provider's saved filters (a repository from the old account need not exist on the new one) and re-fetches the list.

Disconnect a provider

Click Disconnect on the provider's row in Settings → Integrations and confirm.

Disconnecting reaches further than Kepler: it removes the connection from your GitKraken account, so the gk CLI and GitKraken Desktop lose it too. What happens to the provider depends on whether you have another account connected:

  • With another account connected, Kepler promotes it to primary and the provider stays connected under it. Nothing else changes.
  • With no other account connected, the provider is removed entirely. Kepler drops its saved filters and its rows from your lists, so nothing lingers behind pointing at a provider you no longer have.

You can reconnect at any time.

Bitbucket has no issues

Kepler keeps two separate lists of what each provider can return. Bitbucket and Bitbucket Data Center are on the pull-request list only, so Kepler never asks them for issues, and a provider filter for issues never offers them. In the list, Kepler builds facets from the rows actually loaded, so a facet with nothing to offer never appears.

The same rule runs the other way for Jira, Linear, and Trello, which have no pull requests.

How pull requests attach to worktrees

Kepler matches a pull request to a worktree by branch, and it tries the worktree's upstream branch as well as its local one.

That second pass matters when the two names differ. If your local branch is fix-login but it tracks origin/justin/fix-login, matching on the local name alone finds nothing. The pull request's head branch is the upstream name. Kepler runs a second pass against the short branch of the tracking ref and catches it.

Matching runs against open and closed pull requests together, so a merged pull request still shows on the branch it merged from.

Confirmed against inferred

Every link Kepler shows records how it was made, so you can tell a hard link from a guess:

How the link was made Reads as What it means
Recorded on the task Confirmed The link is authoritative. Kepler records one when you launch the task from the pull request, when an agent opens a pull request, when you attach one yourself, or through the branch auto-attach below
The pull request's head branch matched the worktree's local branch Confirmed A direct branch match
The head branch matched the worktree branch's upstream branch Confirmed, and marked Git-native rather than a guess, but the local name differs, so Kepler labels it Matched from upstream branch
An identifier parsed out of the branch name Inferred A heuristic. Issues only: pull requests are never linked this way

A confirmed link carries no extra marking. Kepler underlines with a dotted line any link it wants you to look twice at, and its tooltip says why: Matched from upstream branch, or Matched from branch name for an inferred issue link.

When Kepler finds one pull request more than one way, the most authoritative wins: the task's own recorded link, then a local branch match, then an upstream branch match.

Kepler scopes branch matching to each item's own repository, so two repositories with a branch of the same name do not borrow each other's pull requests.

Auto-attaching a matched pull request

A pull request you open outside Kepler (from a terminal gh, from GitKraken, from the web) doesn't stay a read-time match. Kepler records it on the task for you.

Whenever fresh pull-request data arrives, Kepler looks over your active tasks that hold no pull request yet and attaches one whose head branch matches a worktree's branch. It then behaves like a pull request you attached yourself: it sits in the task's resources, and it reaches the agent as context. See Tasks and Resources.

Auto-attaching is deliberately stricter than the matching above, because Kepler saves an attached resource and sends it to an agent, where a read-time match only draws a card. Auto-attach follows five rules:

Rule Why
Exact matches only The pull request's head branch has to be the worktree's own branch name, and its repository has to match a remote exactly. The upstream-branch pass and the rename-tolerant fallback still draw the card; neither attaches
Open pull requests only A merged pull request cannot claim a branch name that gets reused later
The head branch has to live in the same repository Branch matching keys on the head branch name, and the author of a fork picks that name freely. An attached resource reaches your agent, so a stranger's pull request must not claim your branch
One pull request per task per pass A task drops out of the next pass as soon as it holds one
A pull request another active task already holds is left alone One active task per pull request, without taking it off the task that has it

Auto-attach never archives anything and never replaces anything. A branch match is a guess, so it never supersedes work you started deliberately, and it never removes a link because a pull request briefly dropped out of a read.

What Kepler reads from a pull request

Kepler pulls these fields from a pull request:

Field Notes
Number and title
Description The pull request body
State Kepler derives one of Open, Draft, Merged, or Closed
Repository Name, owner, and host
Author When the provider reports one. Kepler also tracks whether you authored it, which is what splits Yours from Review on a row
Head and base branch When the provider reports them. These drive branch matching and the branch a task starts from
Fork Whether the head branch lives on a fork, and where, so Kepler can fetch it from the fork remote instead of origin
Created at
URL Used by the open control, which names the provider: Open on GitHub, Open on Bitbucket, and so on
verifyre-checked field by field against the shared PullRequest shape in src/shared/provider/pull-request.ts at kepler 7c31af83e. It's unchanged, and the table above is complete apart from the base-repo clone URLs, which only prefill the Clone form. The June 2026 version of this page also claimed Kepler pulls the diff, open review comments, and the current review state when you attach a pull request; none of those are fields on this shape. Confirm with engineering which path supplies review comments and the diff to a Review or Address Feedback run before documenting them.

A merged pull request keeps its description and author for as long as a task, archived or not, still links it.

On a self-hosted server (GitHub Enterprise, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps Server, or Bitbucket Data Center), images embedded in a pull request's description stay blocked and don't display in Kepler's previews. The server's member avatars still load.

Azure DevOps reports pull requests without a usable number, so Kepler identifies them by URL instead. You should not see a difference; identifying by URL is why a pull request you look up by link resolves correctly there.

Where your pull requests turn up

Your pull requests turn up in four places:

Surface What it gives you
The Kepler interface The Todo segment lists the pull requests related to you on each connected host, with your role on each
Actions The Action button hands the pull request to an agent. Review and Address Feedback are the two built-in Actions aimed at pull requests, and Kepler picks between them by who authored it
Review Changes Reading the diff and the commits behind a pull request
Tasks and Resources A pull request is a resource on a task, so you can attach one to work that already exists

Todo's scope differs by host. Each provider answers a different set of questions about you, so Todo lists a different set of pull requests from each:

Host Pull requests Todo lists
GitHub, GitHub Enterprise Yours, assigned to you, or ones you review
GitLab, GitLab Self-Hosted, Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server, Bitbucket Data Center Yours or awaiting your review
Bitbucket Yours, or awaiting your review in your workspaces and repositories added to Kepler

Hover the Related to me chip in Todo's header to see the rule for each integration you have connected. See Issue Tracker Integrations for the issue side.

Review requests come from every host. Pull requests waiting on your review show up from Azure DevOps, Azure DevOps Server, and Bitbucket Data Center as well as GitHub and GitLab. Bitbucket Cloud has no account-wide way to ask for them, so Kepler reads review requests repository by repository, starting with the repositories you have in Kepler and then the rest of your workspaces at a pace that stays inside Bitbucket's hourly request limits. On a large workspace they can take a while to appear.

Kepler loads what you're looking at. Pull request and issue lists refresh while they're on screen, and again when you come back to the window, rather than downloading your whole account's lists in the background. A task's own pull requests refresh when an agent ends a turn, a branch is pushed, or a pull request is published. When one provider fails to load, the others keep loading, and the failed provider's last loaded pull requests stay in the list.

A failing provider is named, with its fix. When a connected provider's read fails, Todo, the Add resources lists, and a link preview show a warning that names the provider and what went wrong, such as GitHub needs you to sign in again, so its work may be missing. On Todo the warning sits under Unable to load your work. Each warning offers one fix:

What went wrong The fix offered
The sign-in needs renewing Reconnect, or Reconnect in settings for a self-hosted provider, which needs a new token
The provider refused part of the request, so some organizations or repositories may be missing Open settings
Rate limiting, an unreachable server, an unreadable answer, or a temporary problem Retry

Rows kept from the last good load carry a cloud mark on their provider chip, labeled Not refreshed: shown from the last successful {provider} load.

The pull request picker says why its list is empty. When you attach a pull request from Add resources, the picker explains an empty list instead of showing No matches:

  • If the Linked work filter hides pull requests, the picker says how many it hides and which filter hides them, and offers Show all.
  • If no connected provider can list pull requests, the picker reads Connect your code host and lists the providers that can fill the tab, each with Connect.

The Linked work filter defaults to No active task. A task counts as active until you archive it, so Done and on-hold tasks count. The filter has five options:

Option Shows pull requests
Any Whatever their task history
No active task With no task, or with archived tasks only. This default keeps earlier work findable, so you don't duplicate it
No tasks ever Never linked to a task
Has an active task Linked to at least one task that isn't archived
Archived tasks only Linked only to archived tasks

A row with task history carries a badge: Task or {count} tasks when one of them is active, Archived task or {count} archived tasks when all are archived.

When nothing you have connected can list issues or pull requests, Add resources opens on a connect view, Connect where your work lives, with a tile for each provider. Connect each one you use, then click Continue. The provider filter's menu also offers Connect another provider…, which opens the same view with a Back button.

You can also start work on a pull request from GitLens or GitKraken Desktop. See Create a Task.

feature: Settingscontent_type: referencestatus: GAlast_verified: 2026-10llms_include: true15241 words · ~69 min

A reference for every setting in Kepler across the 15 Settings pages, plus folder locations, branch prefixes, the Variables menu, per-repository commands (including setup commands that run on every new worktree), worktrees and branches, themes, MCP server defaults, notifications, and the keyboard shortcut list.

Last updated: October 2026

Settings has 15 pages, each with its own sections. This page documents every one of them: what each setting controls, what it defaults to, and what changes when you change it.

Open Settings from the gear icon in the top bar, or with ⌘ , (Ctrl , on Windows and Linux). The same shortcut closes Settings again, the way the gear button does.

The Settings pages

The left rail starts with a Search settings field and Setup, a progress ring that reopens the first-run checklist. Below them, the rail lists the pages in five unlabeled groups, separated by thin lines. The following table lists the pages in rail order:

Page What it covers
Account Who you're signed in as, your organization, what your plan unlocks, and GitKraken AI credits
Agents Your agents, custom agents, MCP servers, agent tools, approvals, and usage limits
Tasks How new tasks start, including the default agent and the agent new sessions start with, and how a task's page looks
Repositories The repositories folder, your repos and folders, worktrees and branches, and Projects
Integrations Issue tracker and Git host connections, and sessions started in your own terminal
Actions Preferred Actions per item kind, and the Action list
General Language, windows, the default editor, and keeping agents running
Notifications Which agent events notify you, on the desktop and in Kepler
Appearance Light or dark appearance and themes, the chat, the terminal font, code font size, and diff layout
Voice input On-device speech-to-text, its transcription engine, and learned words
Keyboard shortcuts Kepler's keyboard shortcuts
Remote environments The machines your agents can run on, over SSH or WSL
Remote Access Opening this Kepler from another device
Updates The installed version and app updates
Help The docs, release notes, onboarding guides, and Kepler's logs

Account is first because it's the only page about the person rather than the app, and it's where the account menu hands off to.

Find a setting

To find a setting by name, type in Search settings. Search matches section titles, control labels, help text, and common synonyms, so a word like theme or notifications finds the right section. With no match, the results read No settings match "{query}".

Under the page you have open, the rail lists that page's main sections so you can jump to one. Under Agents, the rail also lists each supported agent below Your agents. A page with fewer than two listed sections shows no list.

Sub-pages and unsaved changes

Item editors open as sub-pages inside their Settings page, with a breadcrumb back to the page. Examples include a repository, a project, a custom agent, an MCP server, and an Action.

If you leave a sub-page with unsaved changes, Kepler asks before it discards anything. It asks on every way out: Escape, Back, the form's own Cancel, a page in the Settings rail, or a link elsewhere in the app:

  • A form that can save asks Save your changes?, with Cancel, Don't save, and Save.
  • A form that has nothing to save yet, such as a step-by-step add flow, asks Discard your changes?, with Keep editing and Discard.

A form with a Save button keeps it in a footer with Cancel. While you have unsaved edits, the footer reads Unsaved changes. If a save fails, the footer shows why and keeps your edits on the form. A save that succeeds confirms with Saved {name}.

Most fields outside those forms save themselves. Each one shows its own status beside it: Saving, Saved, or Unable to save with the reason. A switch shows only Saving and a failure. If a save fails after you've left the page, Kepler tells you which setting it was: Unable to save {label}.
